#2766. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for smaller loan exemption from appraisal requirements for higher-priced mortgage loans

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, the Federal Reserve Board, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that the 2027 threshold for higher-priced mortgage loans that are subject to special appraisal requirements will increase from $34,200 to $35,500.

The threshold amount will be effective January 1, 2027, and is based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W, as of June 1, 2026.

The Dodd-Frank Act added special appraisal requirements for higher-priced mortgage loans to the Truth in Lending Act, including that creditors obtain a written appraisal based on a physical visit to the interior of the home before making a higher-priced mortgage loan. The rules implementing these requirements contain an exemption for loans at or below a threshold amount that is adjusted annually to reflect CPI-W increases.

Federal Register notice: Appraisals for Higher-Priced Mortgage Loans Exemption Threshold (PDF)

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

#2764. Rams safety ejected after huge hit on Packers’ Christian Watson

The Rams, who have had issues at defensive back, will have bigger worries in the second half of Wednesday night's game against the Packers.

Rams safety Kam Kinchens was ejected for an illegal hit on Packers wide receiver Christian Watson, leaving him lying on the ground for an extended time.

Rams safety Kam Kinchens ejected for illegal hit. AP Photo/Gregory Bull

The play happened near the 1:54 mark of the second quarter, when Watson ran a corner route near the Rams' sideline.

Packers quarterback Jordan Love threw a floater to Watson but led him too far outside.

Watson was attempting to haul in the reception, but Kinchens led with his helmet, injuring the Packers receiver.

While Watson was unable to get back to his feet, Kinchens was seen celebrating the play.

Watson eventually got up and walked away under his own power.

The Packers announced during the second half that Watson was ruled out with a concussion.

Watson was off to a hot start for Green Bay as the team's leading receiver, having four receptions for 65 yards and a touchdown.

The Rams went on to pick up their fifth consecutive victory and beat the Packers, 26-13.

#2763. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for applicability of truth in lending and consumer leasing rules for consumer credit and lease transactions

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau today announced the dollar thresholds used to determine whether certain consumer credit and lease transactions in 2027 are subject to certain protections under Regulation Z (Truth in Lending) and Regulation M (Consumer Leasing).

By law, the agencies are required to adjust the thresholds annually based on the annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W. Transactions at or below the thresholds are subject to the protections of the regulations.

Specifically, based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the CPI-W as of June 1, 2026, Regulation Z and Regulation M generally will apply to consumer credit transactions and consumer leases of $76,300 or less in 2027. However, private education loans and loans secured by real property, such as mortgages, are subject to Regulation Z regardless of the amount of the loan.

Federal Register notice: Consumer Leasing (Regulation M) (PDF)

Federal Register notice: Truth in Lending (Regulation Z) (PDF)

#2761. Lakers’ red-hot start to season will strike fear in Celtics fans hearts

The Lakers are on a quest to become a championship-level team.

And if history can be an indicator, their hot start to the 2026-27 NBA season suggests they're on the right path.

Lakers star guards Luka Doncic (77) and Austin Reaves (15). NBAE via Getty Images

With Monday's home win over the Jazz, the Lakers improved to 15-3 on the season, which was not only tied with the Spurs for the league's best record, but also a positive sign for the Lakers' hopes of being title contenders.

This season is the sixth time this century the Lakers have started a season by winning at least 15 of their first 18 games.

All five of those seasons ended with the Lakers making the NBA Finals, including winning the title in four of them.

Los Angeles Lakers head coach JJ Redick watches his players. AP Photo/Kyusung Gong

The other five times since 2000 that the Lakers won at least 83 percent of their first 18 games were: 2001-2002 (16-2), 2003-04 (15-3), 2008-09 (16-2), 2009-10 (15-3) and 2019-20 (16-2).

They won the 2002, '09, '10 and '20 championships after their hot start to those seasons.

They made it to the Finals in 2004, which was their fourth championship series appearance in five years, before losing to the Pistons in five games.

Walker Kessler, left, and Luka Doncic high-five during a game. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers have won at least 15 of their first 18 games 13 times in franchise history.

Half (six of 13) of those seasons ended with them winning the NBA title, while three more ended with them at least making it to the NBA Finals.

Another commonality between those seasons is that the Lakers won at least 54 regular season games (or the equivalent of it in an 82-game season in the case of the pandemic-shortened 2019-20) each time they had such a hot start to the season.

For a team that was projected to be a borderline playoff contender, that's great company to be in — a high standard to live up to — but also a sign they're on the right path.

#2760. Here's a roadmap to the Aaron Rodgers developments, one of the craziest stories in years

If you were traveling on Wednesday or otherwise not plugged in for one of the most rollicking, unpredictable days in years if not ever, you're in luck.

We've reconstructed the full timeline as to the events regarding now-former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers.

It actually started on Monday, when coach Mike McCarthy was asked whether he has considered using one of the team's other three quarterbacks when the offense is struggling. McCarthy, whose body language in hindsight suggests he was dreading the question, said without equivocation, "Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback."

On Tuesday, Rodgers took his usual reps at practice. He conducted his usual midweek press conference. Unbeknownst to most of the media or the general public, the Steelers were changing their play-calling procedures to have quarterbacks coach Tom Arth, and not McCarthy, communicate the calls to Rodgers through the speaker in his helmet.

Then came Tuesday night. At 11:10 p.m. ET, the Steelers issued a statement — not from coach Mike McCarthy or G.M. Omar Khan or owner Art Rooney II but from team spokesman Burt Lauten — that Mason Rudolph will start on Friday against the Broncos and Rodgers will be inactive.

The news forced the Broncos to make a quick pivot in their preparations from Rodgers to Rudolph. As one source put it, the Broncos weren't sure whether it was a good thing or a bad thing.

It was definitely a bad thing, all things considered, for the Steelers.

By Wednesday morning, reports emerged that it was a coach's decision. At 8:00 a.m. ET, McCarthy informed the team of the decision.

Rodgers wasn't there. As it turns out, he was excused.

Enter Pat McAfee. He teased a surprise guest at 1:40 p.m. ET. As expected, it was Rodgers.

Rodgers took a flamethrower to McCarthy, airing out dirty laundry that created a major mess for his now-former team. He exposed the fact that, at halftime of the Week 11 loss to the Eagles, McCarthy broke down the locker-room gathering by saying, "1-2-3 Packers."

Rodgers also said McCarthy was falling asleep during some meetings and missing other meetings, expressing (in passive-aggressive fashion) concern for McCarthy's health.

But Rodgers also said it was nothing new, and that McCarthy had done the same things in Green Bay, when they worked there together a decade ago.

Rodgers also made it clear he wouldn't stay with the Steelers. After his session with McAfee and company, Dulac reported that the Steelers were expected to release Rodgers. By the official close of business, they'd waived him.

A question lingers as to whether the Steelers will try to deny Rodgers the balance of his $22 million salary. It won't be easy; they'll need to both void the guarantees and wipe out his eligibility for termination pay (in the form of his remaining salary) under the Collective Bargaining Agreement.

McCarthy met with reporters on Wednesday, after all hell had broken loose. He admitted to the "1-2-3 Packers" comment, calling it an "[e]mbarrassing moment."

McCarthy also said that Rodgers was benched after issuing an "ultimatum" that was "clearly outside of his job description."

ESPNFL thereafter reported that McCarthy "has the full support" of the organization, and that the team is "not planning to make any immediate changes." The key word may be "immediate"; with few viable options only two days before a Black Friday game against the Broncos, changes could come later.

The overriding takeaway is that someone with the Steelers knew or should have known that McCarthy and Rodgers couldn't co-exist. Because, obviously, they didn't co-exist.

If, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy believed Rodgers got him fired in Green Bay, McCarthy shouldn't have wanted a reunion with Rodgers. And if, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy was doing the same kinds of things in Green Bay that he did in Pittsburgh, Rodgers shouldn't have wanted a reunion with McCarthy.

The reunion has now ended abruptly, with seven games left in the 2026 regular season. It remains to be seen whether Rodgers wants to play with another team, or whether another team wants him. Any team that signs him risks being on the receiving end of a future gripe session from Rodgers in which he lists all the things that team and/or the coach did wrong — without ever acknowledging his own role in the reality that it didn't work out.

The good news, if there is any, is this: If Rodgers never plays again, his final NFL pass will have been a touchdown.

Which is a better outcome than if he'd retired after the 2025 season. His final pass that year was a pick-six in the playoffs.

#2757. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

#2756. Trail Blazers Make Statement With NBA Cup Win Over Spurs

The Portland Trail Blazers made their biggest statement of the season on the eve of Thanksgiving. Coming home from a five-game road trip, the Blazers handed the San Antonio Spurs their first road loss of the season with a 116-111 win Wednesday night at Moda Center. Deni Avdija banked in a 3-pointer with 37.9 seconds left to give Portland enough cushion to close it out and improve to 12-6.

Avdija finished with 33 points, 12 rebounds and seven assists, following up his 31-point performance in Monday's win over the Philadelphia 76ers. In consecutive games, Portland beat two teams that entered Wednesday with a combined 29-7 record. The first win came on the road to close out a long trip. The second came against a Spurs team that had won its first eight road games.

Damian Lillard added 29 points, and he and Avdija each made five 3-pointers. They scored 18 apiece in the first half to give Portland a 60-57 lead at the break. But Victor Wembanyama scored San Antonio's first 12 points of the third quarter, and the Spurs took an 87-84 lead into the fourth.

That was when Portland's veterans played their best. Jrue Holiday hit two timely 3-pointers and Lillard added another during a 12-2 run to open the fourth quarter. That put the Blazers up 96-89 and forced San Antonio to spend much of the final period chasing the game. Holiday finished with 11 points and seven assists off the bench, while Sidy Cissoko chipped in 16 points and four threes.

Wembanyama still tied the game at 109 with a dunk with two minutes left. Avdija answered with two free throws, then delivered the biggest shot of the night on Portland's next possession. Lillard drove into the lane and found Wembanyama waiting near the basket. With the shot clock winding down, he kicked the ball back to Avdija, whose three went in off the glass to put Portland up five.

Wembanyama was terrific with 44 points and 16 rebounds, but Portland had enough scoring across its rotation to withstand it. The Blazers shot just 39.4% from the field, yet they made 19 3-pointers to San Antonio's 10. They saved their best quarter for last, scoring 32 points in the fourth.

Portland is now 2-1 in NBA Cup group play with Golden State coming to Moda Center on Friday for the group finale. The bigger takeaway is where the Blazers stand after 18 games. They went into Philadelphia and won by 17, then came home and beat San Antonio in a game that was tied with two minutes left. At this point, Portland belongs in the conversation with the best teams in the NBA.

#2755. Trump’s meddling in Huang scandal reignites voter angst over tech’s outsized political power

When voters headed to the polls and flipped Congress this month, they sent a clear message to Washington: They wanted change.

Voters wanted curbs on the technology industry's outsized power in Washington and CEOs cozying up to President Donald Trump to sway politics in their favor.

The Monday reinstatement of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang at Trump's urging may be exactly the opposite of what voters voiced with their ballots.

"This whole ordeal only reinforces what Americans already believe about a broken political system that stands up for Big Tech CEOs and not the American people," Sacha Haworth, founder and CEO of The Tech Oversight Project, told CNBC.

Republicans are warning that Trump's unwavering support for the embattled artificial intelligence chip kingpin could further alienate voters.

"The data were so clear in the election that people don't trust big tech. The corruption message, I think, resonated a lot for Democrats in the election, and I think this sort of thing isn't helpful to Republicans in Congress who are going to be running again in two years," said Brendan Steinhauser, a GOP strategist and CEO of The Alliance for Secure AI Action, who had warned that AI data centers could be a "sleeper issue" in the lead-up to the 2026 midterms.

Before and after Election Day, voters were clear about their concern, which centered on data centers.

A Pew Research Center survey published in October found that 44% of voters considered AI very important to their vote. An NBC News exit poll found that 56% of voters said AI and data centers were crucial or a major factor in their vote for Congress. And in states including Texas, data center opposition forced GOP lawmakers who once welcomed development to change their positions.

There's a lot for tech CEOs to cheer under Trump, who was welcomed with open arms following a period of tough enforcement under President Joe Biden, and many reasons to align with the administration, said Sarah Kreps, a political scientist and professor at Cornell University who studies the intersection of technology and national security.

Chief among the reasons for celebration are the market's AI-driven gains since Trump's 2025 inauguration and his light-touch approach to tech regulation. The Nasdaq has gained nearly 21% from Jan. 17, 2025, the last trading day before the inauguration, and Tuesday's close.

"In the end, this looks like a calculated risk that the people and interests Trump prioritizes benefit more from a stable, rising market than the administration loses from the optics," Kreps said of Huang's reinstatement, adding that the Republican Party will be left to "bear the consequences" in 2028.

Outside the rarefied air of Silicon Valley and the White House, there is scant support for Huang's comeback after a series of extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy that critics say could have jeopardized national security and undermined the U.S.'s advantage in the AI race.

Trump sought to allay those fears this week when he told CNBC that he assured the board that Huang posed no national security threat. Instead, Trump used concerns over the CEO giving his talents to China as a major rationale behind the reinstatement push.

"The bigger threat would be losing the brain power of somebody like Jensen," Trump told CNBC this week. "That would be a threat to me. That would be a far bigger threat than anything we're talking about."

Haworth said the reinstatement raises concerns over how Huang's access to Trump benefits the company's business interests, especially as the U.S. investigates chip smuggling into China.

At the same time, Secretary of Commerce Howard Lutnick's perceived slowness to clamp down on illegal advanced AI chip sales to China, as well as his family's financial ties to Nvidia via Cantor Fitzgerald, the financial firm his sons now run, have drawn further congressional scrutiny.

"Voters are struggling with the high cost of living. They're angry about the corruption they see and a system that has been working more and more for the wealthy and well-connected, and making it harder and harder on working families," Rep. Suzan DelBene, D-Wash., chair of the Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee told CNBC.

"Corruption is an affordability issue. When they see money and favors going in places that don't help support working families, that's an affordability issue for them," DelBene said.

As Republicans try to rebuild after being routed in the midterms, the kind of leniency Trump has shown to Huang could backfire, GOP strategists told CNBC.

"Voters are tired of 'rules for thee, but not for me.' Most Americans have to live with the consequences of their decisions," said Brittany Martinez, executive director of Principles First, a center-right grassroots advocacy organization. "If you're a billionaire with the president's ear, apparently the White House will help you get your job back. That's exactly the kind of double standard that makes people wonder whose side this administration is on."

Marc Short, former chief of staff to Vice President Mike Pence and chair of Advancing American Freedom, the conservative think tank Pence founded, told CNBC that the Trump administration's involvement in Huang's reinstatement "sends a very terrible signal."

"I think it's consistent with the administration taking ownership in 41 separate private companies, to now feel that they can determine to the board who should be the CEO of one of America's biggest companies," he said.

#2753. Austin Reaves returns to Lakers practice, might play Friday at San Antonio

After missing two weeks because of a bruised left heel, Lakers guard Austin Reaves returned to live play in practice Wednesday and could be on track to return in time for the Lakers' early season litmus test against the Spurs at San Antonio on Friday.

Lakers coach JJ Redick deadpanned that Reaves' practice performance Wednesday, the team's last session before traveling on Thanksgiving Day, was "the best I've ever seen him play." Reaves was more understated.

"It felt good to play basketball again," Reaves said. "Wind was a little bad, but kind of to be expected being out [for] two weeks. But felt good. We'll see how I wake up tomorrow. We're still going in the right direction, but felt really good today."

Reaves, the Lakers' second-leading scorer, missed five games in which the Lakers (15-3) went 4-1 and remained atop the NBA standings to set up Friday's marquee matchup between the top two teams in the league. He was injured on Nov. 11 during the third quarter against the Milwaukee Bucks when on a dunk attempt, twisting in the air and landing so hard on the inside edge of his heel that he noticed an indent on his shoe insole. He tried to keep playing, but a painful baseline drive forced him to ask out of the game.

"I didn't think I broke my foot," Reaves said Wednesday, "but it sure felt like I broke my foot."

While managing the pain during his return to play, Reaves has worked with the training staff to find a comfortable sneaker and insole combination. Reaves said through the seemingly million options presented to him, he found one that worked for Wednesday's practice.

Read more: Lakers eagerly await Austin Reaves' return before epic showdown against San Antonio

"He looked really good today," center Walker Kessler said. "It was definitely good to have him back out there."

After signing a maximum contract extension to establish himself as one of the Lakers' top stars, Reaves got off to a strong start to the season. He is averaging 24.7 points, 4.7 assists and 3.3 rebounds while shooting a career-best 51.3% from the field and 40.9% from three. The Lakers had won six consectutive games heading into the Milwaukee matchup when Reaves got hurt. Even when Reaves was sidelined, they stretched the streak to nine, tying the longest in Redick's coaching tenure.

"I was just frustrated because it's one of those things, it's like an uncontrollable," Reaves said. "... One of those things that just, it sucks. I was rolling, playing well. We were rolling as a team, wanted to continue to be out there and play games. Hate missing games. But it's one of those things, like I said, there wasn't much that we could really do, but we took care of business."

The Lakers' offense has stalled without Reaves, dropping from first in scoring and efficiency to third. Luka Doncic is especially struggling with his shot. The league's leading scorer, averaging 36.6 points per game, has made 12 of 51 (23.5%) shots from three during the last four games.

With Reaves out, Doncic got help from reserve guards Quentin Grimes and Collin Sexton. Grimes started in Reaves' place and delivered a game-saving, career-high 47 points against the Charlotte Hornets. Sexton served as a primary ballhandler during critical fourth-quarter stretches without Doncic.

"[We] found ways to win," Reaves said. "I think that's a big part of the NBA is if you can find ways to win when you're not playing as good as you are capable of, that'll help win in a lot of games. So very happy, proud just to continue to see the togetherness and the growth that we continue to have."

The Lakers stuck together to set up Friday's showdown that is stocked with intriguing storylines. The NBA Cup group stage finale is a win-and-you're-in matchup for the knockout round. Most valuable player candidates Doncic and Victor Wembanyama, averaging 29.9 points, 12.3 rebounds and 3.9 blocks a game, face off for the first time this season. Questioned for a perceived weaker schedule, the Lakers get their first major opportunity to assert themselves into the title conversation against the reigning Western Conference champions.

The Spurs (15-3) have the best net rating in the NBA, outscoring opponents by 12.3 points per 100 possessions. The Lakers, suddenly a top-10 defensive team after holding the Utah Jazz to only 94 points in Monday's win, are sixth in net rating (plus-6.8 points per 100 possessions).

Despite all the makings of an epic early-season showdown, Redick emphasized that Friday's blockbuster matchup is only the beginning of a difficult four-game road trip. After San Antonio, the Lakers play at Memphis on Sunday, Houston on Monday and New Orleans on Wednesday.

"I think these four games will be a great barometer of where we're at," Redick said. "To your point whether it's guys being in and out of the lineup or play-style changes or tweaks we make or rotation changes, it just always evolves.

"And whether we lose the game, whether we win the game, we're either going to be contenders on 'First Take' or we're not going to be contenders and we're fake. That stuff is just noise. We're going to focus on the process of trying to get better and it's a good test to see where we're at."

#2752. Bullish hopes spring as traders bet against a higher VIX: Options Action Live

With stocks mounting a comeback Wednesday, options trading across a few key volatility products suggests big swings in stocks may dampen in the near term.

More than 80,000 options traded on the Barclays S&P 500 VIX Short Term Futures ETN (VXX), almost three times the 30-day average, with more puts trading than calls, according to LiveVol data. The fund aims to track the moves in Cboe's VIX Index, which surged to 35 early Wednesday but slipped to below 28 as the S&P 500 rallied to within 25 basis points of unchanged.

At the same time, options volume on VIX itself was 50% above average, with an even more distinctive bias towards puts, suggesting traders see stocks firming, or at least, the size of the market's moves shrinking. Of the 850,000 options traded on VIX, more than 515,000 were puts, with twice as many puts likely initiated by buyers than calls, Cboe data show.

#2751. Rams DB Facing Calls for Suspension After What He Did to Packers’ Christian Watson

Los Angeles Rams safety Karen Kinchens was ejected for his hit against Packers WR Christian Watson Getty

Green Bay Packers WR Christian Watson took a vicious hit from Rams safety Karen Kinchens on Wednesday Getty

The Los Angeles Rams and Green Bay Packers kicked off the NFL's first Thanksgiving Eve game on Wednesday night. Not long before halftime, the game turned ugly.

With 1:54 left in the first half, Rams safety Kamren Kinchens launched himself into Packers wide receiver Christian Watson along the sideline and led with his helmet (video here). Watson stayed down for several minutes and was ruled out at the start of the second half with a concussion.

Kinchens was ejected. But many fans don't think that's enough.

GettyLos Angeles Rams safety Karen Kinchens was ejected for his hit against Packers WR Christian Watson

What made it worse for a lot of viewers was what happened after the hit. While Watson was still down, Kinchens was seen celebrating the play.

Medical staff brought out a gurney. Packers teammates, including quarterback Jordan Love, knelt nearby while Watson was looked at. He eventually got up and walked to the locker room under his own power.

Fans flooded social media calling for the league to step in and severely punish Kinchens for the brutal hit.

"That should be a season ending tackle for the defender suspension for the remaining season," one fan wrote.

"Bro was on the sidelines, dude should be suspended," another fan added.

"No place for that in the game — send him packing for the season," one user posted.

"F*** Kam Kinchens suspend that dirty f***," another fan wrote.

"Kamren Kinchens should be suspended for the rest of the season, playoffs included and should be cut by the Rams tonight. Criminal behavior man, prayers to Christian," one fan said.

"One of the dirtiest hits we've ever seen. Kamren needs to be suspended for the season," another scoffed.

"Did you see Rams Safety Kamren Kinchens ejected for his hit on Christian Watson? He should get suspended and fined. Ridiculous hit," remarked a third fan.

GettyGreen Bay Packers WR Christian Watson took a vicious hit from Rams safety Karen Kinchens on Wednesday

Kinchens was first flagged for unnecessary roughness on the play. But after a video review, the NFL office in New York stepped in.

According to the Netflix broadcast, the league said Kinchens left his feet and made helmet-to-helmet contact. NFL executive vice president Troy Vincent made the final call to disqualify him.

Netflix rules analyst Terry McAulay agreed that the ejection was warranted.

However, not everyone saw it that way. Some questioned on social media whether Kinchens actually made helmet-to-helmet contact.

The hit came on a fourth-and-1 play. Watson held onto the ball, but the catch didn't count because Packers wideout Matthew Golden was flagged for offensive pass interference, which offset the Kinchens foul.

It was a big loss for Green Bay. Watson had four catches for 65 yards and a touchdown before he was hurt.

Kinchens could also be hit with a fine, and fans will be watching to see whether the league goes further than that.

#2750. What we learned as Kings can't overcome brutal first quarter in loss to Warriors

What we learned as Kings can't overcome brutal first quarter in loss to Warriors originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO, Calif. — The Sacramento Kings were doomed from the start.

After falling behind by 25 in the first quarter Wednesday night to the Golden State Warriors, they fought back to make the game respectable in the third quarter. But the Warriors ultimately pulled away — even Stephen Curry out with an oblique injury — and won comfortably.

De'Anthony Melton made eight 3s while scoring 24 points, and the Warriors won 129-110 at Chase Center. The Kings fell to 2-16 and will have three days off, including Thanksgiving on Thursday, before hosting a back-to-back against the Minnesota Timberwolves on Sunday and the New Orleans Pelicans on Monday.

Wednesday's loss was the Kings' final game of NBA Cup group play. Sacramento went 0-4 and won't make it out of the group stage after benig outscored by 49 points.

The Kings were missing Zach LaVine, who was out with knee soreness coming out of Monday's loss to the San Antonio Spurs.

The game got off to a terrible start for Sacramento, and they couldn't recover.

The Warriors scored 88 points in their win Sunday over the Boston Celtics, and they had half that total in the first quarter on Wednesday thanks to the Kings' horrendous defense. It was the most for Golden State in any quarter all season.

Golden State went 9-of-13 from deep in the opening frame, taking advantage of open looks repeatedly, while the Kings missed all nine of their attempts from distance. That 27-0 advantage from 3-point range led to Sacramento trailing 44-19.

The Warriors were without Curry, but Melton did his best to impersonate the NBA's best shooter. He had 12 points while making four 3s in his first eight minutes, while Santos matched him with 12 points in the opening period.

The Kings had 10 first-quarter points from Domantas Sabonis, but none of his teammates had more than four. Sacramento's bench was outscored 19-0, and they allowed 15 points off their eight turnovers.

The Kings weren't going to let their first-quarter performance define the game.

They opened the second half with far more energy than the first, and that showed up with its 3-point shooting. Sacramento began the third on a 25-10 run to cut the lead to four, while making 6 of 9 from distance.

However, Golden State ended the quarter on a 19-11 run and took a 12-point lead into the fourth.

The Kings came into the game with the NBA's second-worst plus-minus in third quarter, but they outscored the Warriors 36-29 in the third Wednesday; Sacramento held a 70-57 advantage across the second and third quarters.

The Kings' top players approximated their usual scoring. Sabonis had 27 points, with 10 rebounds and six assists. Keegan Murray had 18 points, and Darius Acuff Jr. added 22.

But the bench scoring favored Golden State 61-19.

Sacramento came into the game with the lowest-scoring bench in the NBA, averaging just 23.4 points per game while the Warriors ranked second at 44.7. Murray had to play 42 minutes. Malik Monk had just five points in 13 minutes.

Precious Achiuwa finished with 11 points, his highest-scoring game since Oct. 29. He missed three games recently with a lower leg injury. Emanuel Sharp started in place of LaVine and finished with nine points on 3-of-6 shooting from 3. He fouled out with 1:54 remaining after playing 25 minutes.

#2749. Saudi national in OpenAI bugging case is out on bond after DOJ tries to block release

Abdulrahman Alfozan, the Saudi national accused of bugging an executive conference room at OpenAI's headquarters, has been released from custody, his attorney told CNBC on Wednesday.

The confirmation of Alfozan's release came a day after a federal judge in San Francisco said the defendant would be allowed out of jail on a $1 million bond, despite prosecutors' warnings that he posed a serious flight risk.

Alfozan, a former OpenAI engineer, is expected to be fitted with an ankle monitor, his attorney, David Rizk, previously told CNBC.

Alfozan was scheduled to be released from detention Wednesday morning at 8 a.m. PT. Shortly before that deadline, U.S. Attorney Craig Missakian of the Northern District of California urged the court to maintain Alfozan's detention pending trial, "given the significant risk that the defendant will flee to Saudi Arabia if he is released from custody."

In an order posted on the case docket later Wednesday, Judge Vince Chhabria said the court only learned of the government's last-minute bid to block Alfozan's release after he had already been let out.The government's motion "is therefore denied as moot," Chhabria's order stated. The judge set a hearing for Dec. 3 on the bid to revoke the magistrate judge's order releasing Alfozan.

Pointing to Alfozan's "ample resources, significant family and professional ties to Saudi Arabia, and few remaining ties to the United States," Missakian had argued in a court filing that the release conditions imposed by Magistrate Judge Alex Tse "are insufficient to safeguard the defendant's appearance for future proceedings in this case."

Alfozan "presents a serious risk of flight that no set of release conditions can reasonably mitigate," Missakian wrote.

His office sought to pause the magistrate judge's release order until a ruling on a motion to revoke it. The Department of Justice did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on Alfozan's release.

Tse in a court hearing Tuesday ruled in favor of Rizk's motion for his client's release, which said prosecutors have admitted finding "no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."

In a text message to CNBC after that hearing, Rizk said, "We are simply grateful that the Court made the right decision to release Mr. Alfozan and now we look forward to defending the case."

But Missakian's filing Wednesday morning pushed back, saying "the government's investigation of this seven-week-old case is ongoing."

Prosecutors have so far found "hundreds of audio files" on Alfozan's personal computer, "including dozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, at least one of which involved representatives of a foreign government and many of which involved discussions of what may be trade secrets," according to the filing.

"To be sure, what the defendant did or intended to do with those audio files remains under investigation, but the absence of that evidence at this point has no bearing" on whether Alfozan is a flight risk, Missakian argued.

Alfozan was arrested Oct. 9 and is accused of concealing a recording device in the artificial intelligence company's conference room to secretly record discussions. The device was found in a USB port in a television in the room on Sept. 30, prosecutors have said.

Jury selection for Alfozan's trial is scheduled to begin Jan. 20.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan contributed to this report.

#2747. Crypto talent will return ‘when AI implodes,’ Arthur Hayes says

Startup talent that has concentrated in artificial intelligence will return to the crypto industry "when AI implodes," according to Arthur Hayes, chief investment officer at Maelstrom Fund.

"A lot of talent has gone into the AI space, so I definitely think there'll be a resurgence in good founder talent coming back to crypto," Hayes said in an online interview with CNBC on Tuesday.

He added that some founders may also return once they "recognize" that their products could eventually be "usurped by Anthropic or OpenAI."

The scale and capabilities of the largest frontier AI labs have made it difficult to "build a real AI business," Hayes said, arguing that "most" founders simply "raise a bunch of money and then light it on fire."

He expects the AI trade to face "serious headwinds" in the second half of 2027, when leading labs begin confronting sizable bills tied to compute leases and other non-cancelable commitments.

Anthropic expects about $451.6 billion in compute and infrastructure obligations through the end of 2030, with roughly $70.7 billion due by the end of September 2027, according to its prospectus.

"Do they have the money? We're going to find out," Hayes said.

Broader enthusiasm around the AI trade has also cooled amid questions about future infrastructure spending. Even exuberance from Jensen Huang's return as Nvidia CEO on Monday proved short-lived, with the shares falling 5.9% on Tuesday and Anthropic dropping below its IPO price four days after its debut.

The co-founder of now-shuttered crypto exchange BitMEX also thinks bitcoin could test $50,000 if the Nasdaq continues to fall. The cryptocurrency has shown little sign of recovery after dropping as low as $55,142.16 on Saturday — its lowest level since July.

Bitcoin hit an all-time high of $126,279.63 in October last year and traded as high as $97,915.90 in January this year.

It has now fallen for seven straight weeks — only the third such streak in its history — with crypto exchange Bitfinex attributing the weakness to equity-market sentiment rather than interest-rate expectations.

Hayes said he would like to see bitcoin rebound from around $55,000 even as the Nasdaq continues to fall. That divergence, he said, would suggest markets were shifting from a broad sell-off toward pricing in a "reflation" scenario, with the Federal Reserve eventually providing more liquidity.

Neither bitcoin nor gold has yet decoupled from the Nasdaq, with Hayes describing the current market moves as a "correlation-led liquidation."

"Stuff goes down, you sell crypto first because it's easy, and then you sell everything else," he said.

Hayes said a bitcoin rally could push up funding rates and futures-basis returns, increasing the income Ethena earns from its hedged crypto positions. That could make USDe, Ethena's synthetic dollar, more attractive and expand its circulation, he said, adding that he expects the ENA token to rise materially.

"Those are the two things that I'm keeping an eye on, and when I'm ready to pull the trigger on buying risk again, those will be the things we buy a lot of," Hayes said.

#2746. NBA announces $11 billion investment plan to develop, modernize arenas for NBA Europe

Is European basketball ready for modern American-style NBA arenas? With adjacent entertainment districts around them?

The NBA announced plans Wednesday for a €10 billion (nearly $11 million at current exchange rates) initiative to pay for "the development and renovation of basketball arenas – and the sports and entertainment districts around them – across Europe." To make this happen, the NBA assembled a group of heavy-hitter global investment firms including Apollo Sports Capital; Ares Management; Arctos, a business of KKR; Blackstone; HPS Investment Partners, a part of BlackRock; J.P. Morgan; Sixth Street and Stonepeak, among others.

"We've seen firsthand how state-of-the-art arenas and the related developments around them can transform basketball teams and their surrounding communities," NBA Commissioner Adam Silver said in a statement announcing the initiative. "As we prepare to launch a new league in Europe alongside FIBA, we see a significant opportunity to help clubs develop facilities which both build on the unique traditions that define European basketball while also elevating the fan experience and attracting many more live events."

This is about arenas for NBA Europe teams, the league the NBA and partner FIBA plan to at least soft launch next October. That said, this initiative is entirely separate from the team investment process.

League officials have been open from the start that they see opportunity in Europe, not just because of the fan base, but because the arena infrastructure there does not maximize profitability — the NBA believes European clubs are leaving money on the table. Part of the plan to make NBA Europe profitable is to change that.

In its announcement, the NBA put it this way: "The investors will look to partner with participating clubs, ownership groups, local developers and other service providers to develop world-class arenas that improve the fan experience and, where appropriate, mixed-use districts around them. Depending on local needs and opportunities, these projects could include housing, workplaces, restaurants, retail and public spaces, helping to revitalize communities where people live, work and play. "

The NBA has long championed the idea that new world-class arenas drive economic growth in the cities where they are placed, not only through ticket sales and jobs created, but also by revitalizing parts of a city if there are housing and entertainment districts (with restaurants, theaters and more) around them. However, multiple studies suggest this is more a redistribution of wealth and spending from other parts of a city to the new district.

Europe does not have many arenas like the ones NBA teams often play in. The target cities for NBA Europe include Paris, Rome, London, Manchester, Lyon, Madrid, Barcelona, Athens, Berlin and more. However, those cities have not been finalized as the bidding process continues.

#2745. Preview: Warriors welcome Kings, Acuff Jr. to Chase Center

Let's talk turkeys and trap games, Dub Nation. The night before Thanksgiving, the Sacramento Kings roll into Chase Center at 2-15, losers of nine of their last 10, with absolutely nothing to lose. That's the most dangerous guest you can have at the table. They're starving for wins!

The Warriors (9-8, 11th in the West) have won seven straight at home and come in as the betting favorites, so on paper this is a comfortable Cup Night. Paper doesn't box out, though. Golden State is 1-4 in the five games Stephen Curry has missed, and that one win came Sunday when they squeezed past Boston 88-87 to snap a five-game skid.

Sacramento also beat Boston by one on Saturday, so this is the battle of Pacific Division teams who have recently seen Jayson Tatum bow his head in shame.

Golden State grabbed 19 offensive rebounds against Boston in an ugly contest where every extra possession mattered. Brandin Podziemski led the way with 18 points, seven rebounds and four threes. Charles Bassey went 6-for-7 for 13 points with nine rebounds, three assists and three steals, and Gui Santos added 13. Yaxel Lendeborg chipped in 12 points and 8 boards. With Steph Curry, Jimmy Butler, the center post designated for Kristaps Porzingis, and Moses Moody in street clothes, this is the blueprint for now. We're talking winning ugly, physical, and with everybody eating.

#2741. Fed’s preferred inflation gauge accelerated to 3.8% in October, raising the 2027 stakes for the central bank

Inflation moved further away from the Federal Reserve's target in October, reinforcing expectations that another interest rate hike is in the cards for December and raising questions about how much more work the central bank will have to do in 2027.

The personal consumption expenditures price index, the Fed's preferred inflation gauge, showed a seasonally adjusted 3.8% annual inflation rate, in line with the Dow Jones consensus estimate and nearly twice the central bank's goal. The reading was 0.1 percentage point higher than September.

Excluding food and energy, core PCE was 3.3%, the Commerce Department reported Wednesday. That was 0.1 percentage point above the forecast.

While the Fed officially follows the headline figure, policymakers consider core to be a more reliable longer-term gauge of inflation trends.

A separate report also showed much stronger economic growth for the third quarter, with gross domestic product rising at a 4.3% annualized rate, according to a revised Commerce Department estimate. The strong top-line gain for the economy adds further fuel to the notion that Fed monetary policy is not restricting economic activity.

Consumer spending rose 0.3% for the month, which matched the pace of price increases but was 0.1 percentage point lower than forecast. Income, on the other hand, surged 0.7%, much more than the 0.4% estimate. The personal saving rate rose to 4.1%.

Stock market futures added to losses following the release, while Treasury yields also were sharply lower. Even with the higher-than-expected core inflation figure, expectations eased a bit for Fed rate hikes in 2027. Traders continued to price in two more increases but lowered odds for a third hike to about 1-in-3, compared to 50-50 on Tuesday, according to the CME Group's FedWatch.

"Inflation is too hot and it will not be brought under control as long as economic demand is also too hot," said Chris Rupkey, chief economist at Fwdbonds.

The report comes with the market fully pricing in a quarter percentage point interest rate hike when the Fed meets Dec. 8-9. Less certain is how much more rates will need to increase to get inflation back down to the Fed's 2% target. Inflation has been running above target for some 5½ years.

Inflation had been drifting lower but spiked again following the late-February attacks on Iran led by the U.S. and Israel. Tariffs also have continued to exert pressure, as has strong demand associated with the artificial intelligence buildout. The White House on Wednesday announced a fresh round of tariffs on 14 trading partners including China, the European Union and Japan.

Policymakers generally consider factors such as tariffs and supply shocks to be temporary, but some fear the impact has begun spreading through the economy and could become embedded.

"America's inflation problem is bigger than an energy shock," said Heather Long, chief economist at Navy Federal Credit Union.

The large upward increase in GDP came from revisions to investment as well as consumer and government spending.

Consumer spending, which accounts for about two-thirds of all economic activity, rose 4.2% in the third quarter. A metric known as real final sales to private domestic purchasers, which Fed officials regard as a key indicator of underlying demand, jumped 5.8%, reflecting an upward revision of 0.9 percentage point.

In other economic news Wednesday, the Labor Department reported that initial jobless claims slipped to 189,000 for the week ending Nov. 21, down 13,000 from the prior week and back to around the lowest level since the late 1960s. Wall Street had been expecting 200,000.

Austin Reaves, left, and Luka Doncic talk during a game against the Utah Jazz in Los Angeles, Wednesday, Nov. 18, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Reaves sustained the injury after landing awkwardly following a missed fast-break dunk in the third quarter of a game against the Bucks.

"No, I had no idea," he said. "Tried to play through it, and then I drove baseline, planted on it, and was like, I can't do this. So walked to the back and was in some pretty bad pain. I didn't think I broke my foot, but it sure felt like I broke my foot."

And basically, the main message was, let's get this back to normal before we put you back out there and put yourself at risk again so later on in the season you won't have to deal with a flare up or something."

But it's one of those things, like I said, there wasn't much that we could really do. But we took care of business."

Austin Reaves #15 of the Los Angeles Lakers reacts during a game against the Utah Jazz at Crypto.com Arena on Monday, Nov. 23, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

When asked how Reaves looked Wednesday, Redick didn't hesitate.

When reporters prodded him to see if he was being sarcastic, he doubled down, "Yeah, not joking," he added.

Austin Reaves (15) of the Los Angeles Lakers smiles during a game against the Utah Jazz at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles on Wednesday, Nov. 18, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers sure hope that Reaves can return Friday and that Redick is right considering the challenge they have ahead of them.

The Lakers and Spurs are tied for the best record in the NBA at 15-3 and Luka Doncic and Victor Wembanyama are currently in a two-man race for the MVP Award.

When asked if it's important to him to return against the Spurs, Reaves balked.

"I mean, I want to play," Reaves said. "…I didn't get hurt the night of Milwaukee and look at the schedule and be like, 'God, I need to be back before the San Antonio game.' If I'm back, good, we'll go to war. But I got to listen to my body and take care of myself. But it should be fun if I'm healthy and get out there."

#2739. Ukraine tells Putin to ‘stop complaining’ over oil refinery attacks as Russia promises retaliation

Ukrainian Foreign Minister Andrii Sybiha has told Russian President Vladimir Putin to "stop complaining" over attacks on Russian refineries, saying Putin could end Kyiv's strikes on the oil facilities by bringing an end to the Kremlin's nearly five-year-long war.

His comments come shortly after Russia's president told a meeting of government officials and senior oil executives in the Kremlin on Tuesday that Ukraine continues to attack refining facilities despite U.S. President Donald Trump's appeal to refrain from doing so.

"I would like to note that retaliatory action from our side is inevitable. Everyone must be aware of that," Putin said, according to Russian state news agency Tass.

In September, Trump called on Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy to stop targeting Russian oil refineries, saying the attacks were "hurting the world" as fuel supply disruptions from the wars in Ukraine and Iran propelled U.S. diesel prices to a record high. Moscow welcomed Trump's push for Ukraine to halt strikes on Russia's diesel-producing facilities at the time.

"If Putin doesn't like Ukraine's strikes on his oil refineries, he can order an end to the war," Ukraine's Sybiha said Tuesday via social media.

"If he doesn't want his military vessels hit, he can order an end to the war. Not to mention the 432,000 Russian soldiers he has lost on the battlefield this year without coming any closer to the goals of his invasion. Clearly, he doesn't care about them. Stop complaining. End the war," he added. CNBC could not independently verify the figures.

In July, the Center for Strategic and International Studies estimated that about 1.4 million Russian troops were killed, wounded or went missing between the start of the full-scale invasion in Feb. 2022 and June 2026, with more than 30,000 casualties a month in the first half of this year.

Ukraine has stepped up attacks on Russian critical targets in recent weeks, damaging Putin's prized Crimea bridge, striking a Russian cargo ship in the Baltic Sea and destroying one of Russia's newest ships at a Libyan port.

Kyiv has also targeted Russian energy infrastructure. Zelenskyy said earlier this month that Ukraine's long-range strikes had disabled more than 55% of Russia's oil-refining capacity, describing this as a "justified response to Russia's refusal to de-escalate and its strikes on our energy infrastructure."

Ukrainian drones reportedly struck two major Russian oil refineries in Russia's Samara region overnight, triggering fires at both facilities.

Satellite images published in late October also showed black smoke rising from multiple fires at Rosneft's Novokuibyshevsk oil refinery in southern Russia following a suspected Ukrainian drone attack.

Putin on Tuesday said Russia's fuel market situation remains "challenging" and called on government officials and oil executives "to continue reacting promptly to the supply-demand relationship for fuel across the whole territory of Russia," according to Interfax.

Ukraine's allies have warned the country faces another winter of blackouts and freezing temperatures amid escalating Russian energy strikes.

#2738. Sean McVay has NSFW reaction to Rams flattening Packers on Thanksgiving Eve

By the time Sean McVay and Matthew Stafford got a good look at what they had left to work with on the sideline Wednesday night, they might as well have been in a garage trying to get a race car back on the track after blowing a tire and snapping a belt.

Oh, and somebody had already borrowed half their tools.

Los Angeles Rams head coach Sean McVay points to his defense during a game against the Green Bay Packers at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, Calif., Nov. 25, 2026. Jon SooHoo/UPI/Shutterstock

The Rams went into their game against the Packers without Puka Nacua and Davante Adams, the two receivers Stafford generally looks for when he needs somebody to go make a play. They lost cornerback Jaylen Watson weeks ago.

Then left guard Bill Murray went down. So did right tackle Warren McClendon Jr. Whatever comfort McVay took in having a couple of tight ends to help out his makeshift offensive line lasted only as long as Davis Allen and Tyler Higbee did.

Over on defense, safety Kamren Kinchens got himself thrown out for unnecessary roughness on a hard hit on Packers wide receiver Christian Watson.

Somewhere in all that, McVay and his staff found a way to run the ball down Green Bay's throat for 241 yards in a must-have 26-13 win.

Matthew Stafford of the Los Angeles Rams calls a play against the Green Bay Packers at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, California, Wednesday, Nov. 25, 2026. Getty Images

And when Stafford needed a receiver, there was second-year wideout Konata Mumpfield, who caught four passes for 69 yards.

Meanwhile, a defense that had every reason to feel as shorthanded as the offense spent most of the night getting itself off the field. Three takeaways. Eleven first downs allowed. All of 244 yards for the Packers, three more than the Rams managed just handing the ball off.

Talk about figuring it out on the fly. Wednesday night and darn near the whole season.

It got McVay thinking about his old college coach, Terry Hoeppner, who had three pieces of advice for him back in the day: Have a plan. Work the plan. Plan for the unexpected.

He's getting tired of the last one, albeit a master at dominating it.

"We've had a lot of that third s––t come to life to plan for the unexpected this year," McVay said.

Consider their five-game winning streak, including Wednesday's win over the Packers, evidence of the Rams' ability to survive and thrive.

By any means necessary. Which sometimes means drawing up formations on the sideline.

Los Angeles Rams running back Blake Corum (24) eludes Green Bay Packers cornerback Brandon Cisse (2) on Wednesday, Nov. 25, 2026, at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, Calif. Jon SooHoo/UPI/Shutterstock

"The biggest thing is, how do we figure out what's the best way to use the group that we have to play the opponent that we're going against? That was what we felt like it was," McVay said.

Imagine telling McVay beforehand he'd go jumbo against Green Bay, then start losing the very guys who make it possible to play that way. Now imagine telling him the Packers would know exactly what was coming and Corum would still be running through them.

#2737. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2736. The Lakers’ wing depth, adaptability are becoming strengths this season

Entering 2026-27, perhaps the most consequential unknown resided on the wings for the Los Angeles Lakers. They knew they could count on Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves to provide All-Star-caliber (and better) production in the backcourt. They were banking on Walker Kessler, at worst, providing high-level rim protection and interior play-finishing, based on his tenure with the Utah Jazz.

Filling in the gaps at small forward and power forward, as well as minutes off the bench at those spots, was a more dubious proposition. Jake LaRavia was coming off a very poor second half in 2025-26. Matisse Thybulle had battled injuries in recent years while Ziaire Williams was yet to establish firm footing through five NBA seasons. Quentin Grimes' role and performance had been a little sporadic the past few seasons.

Yet five weeks into 2026-27, all of them are donning different caps and playing darn good basketball for the 15-3 Lakers — the product of a team-first, resilient mindset.

"I think they're all really grateful to be here, to be Lakers. They're all really grateful to be on this team and be part of the group. I know [Matisse] has expressed that to me on a couple of occasions. For [Ziaire], Matisse, [Quentin], they've experienced adversity in their careers in different ways," head coach JJ Redick said after Monday's win over the Utah Jazz." I think that gratitude piece is like…they're just all in a situation where they're putting the team first and they're putting their egos aside. That stuff matters. I think it's one of the reasons our group has had early success because we said on day one, we wanted this season to be about the team and not any one individual one of us and, so far, it's been that."

Averaging 2.3 steals and 1.0 blocks per game while shooting 40.5% from deep, Thybulle has been a defensive menace and knocked down open 3s when the ball swings his way. Williams has assumed a lot of lofty point-of-attack duties and excelled as a spot-up shooter (42.1% beyond the arc) and cutter (73.5% on 2-pointers).

Grimes scaled up his offensive role while Reaves was sidelined for two weeks and recorded three 20-point outings over that span (including a career-high 47 against the Charlotte Hornets). He's also shooting 40.3% from deep. LaRavia has rebounded from a bumpy end to last season to shoot 43.2% beyond the arc, relentlessly attack the offensive glass and be a reliable help defender.

Dončić, Reaves and Kessler may be leading the way but Los Angeles wouldn't have charged to the top of the league without their versatile quartet on the wings.

"I think just everyone is willing to sacrifice for the same goal. We just have a lot of adaptable players, I think," Jake LaRavia told reporters postgame on Monday. "A lot of it's lineup-based, matchup-based, so whoever the coaches think should start that night is who's going to start. I think, as players, we're all willing to be kind of thrown in and out of the lineup and it doesn't really change much for us as players. You just keep the same mindset and keep the same goal and just go out there and play hard."

#2734. Liesman: Why Greenspan could bet on productivity — and Warsh needs to hike

In a discussion on CNBC's Halftime Report Wednesday, the question came up: Why can't Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh hold the line on interest rates the way his icon Alan Greenspan did at the beginning of the dot-com era?

Greenspan famously leaned against inflation hawks on his committee in 1995 and 1996, holding the line on interest rates amid strong growth, because he believed in the productivity promise of the internet revolution.

It would be nice if Warsh could follow the same playbook amid the artificial intelligence build-out and keep rates steady until the productivity arrives. Unfortunately, a quick look at the numbers shows just how different the situation is for Warsh when it comes to inflation, the magnitude of the capital-investment boom and the monetary and fiscal stimulus boosting the economy.

Bottom line: the Fed looks like it has to hike — and maybe substantially — to be anywhere close to the position where the Fed was in Greenspan's time at the dawn of a major technological revolution.

It may be that the massive capex spending on AI will mean more productivity and less inflation down the road. But the front-end build-out is proving to be more inflationary, and more challenging, to a Fed already dealing with high inflation. In many ways, that's the opposite of what Greenspan had to handle.

The Kings lost by 16. Then they traveled down Interstate 80 to San Francisco and fell to the Stephen Curry-less Golden State Warriors on Wednesday, 129-110, after falling behind by 25 points in the first quarter.

Sacramento is now 2-16 at Thanksgiving. And beyond getting second-year center Maxime Raynaud back from a knee injury soon, reinforcements don't appear to be coming.

"It's tough. We've dug ourselves a super deep hole," Keegan Murray said Wednesday. "So obviously, it's tough to figure out where my head's at. We just gotta figure out how to win games.

Sacramento outscored Golden State by 13 points combined through the second and third quarters. They cut a 29-point lead to four in the third, but the Warriors cruised to the win anyway thanks largely to shooting 46 percent from 3-point range, taking advantage of the Kings' terrible perimeter defense.

"Obviously, it's tough to win in this league with our team right now; we haven't figured that out at all," Murray said. "We can't put four quarters together to put ourselves in that position, so mindset-wise, we just got to take these couple days and just figure it out."

The Kings didn't have a counterpunch to the Warriors' decision to blitz point guard Darius Acuff Jr. with double teams. The talented rookie finished with 22 points but only had four in the first half when the game got away. He finished with another inefficient shooting night, making just nine of 24 shots.

Acuff has been just about the only bright spot from an otherwise terrible Kings season to date. But as much promise as he's shown, the 20-year-old is going to learn things the hard way, like how to manage opponents double-teaming him to force Sacramento's secondary and tertiary options to beat them.

"I think more than anything, you're going to need three outlets when you're going to blitz Darius," Kings coach Doug Christie said. "… I think we were a little late on everything in that first half, even on rotations. We have to be aggressive, and that was something that we went over in practice. It's something that we looked at in film and talked to them about."

The Kings rank dead last in offensive rating at 107.1 and dead last in defensive rating at 119.6.

They have a difficult game Sunday, when they host the 14-5 Minnesota Timberwolves before completing the home back-to-back against the 5-14 New Orleans Pelicans on Monday.

Wednesday continued what seems to be a years-long trend of the Kings struggling against teams who are missing their best players. And that was the case Monday and Wednesday by losing to the Spurs and Warriors with Wembanyama and Curry sidelined.

De'Anthony Melton torched the Kings by making eight of 12 from deep, leading to 24 points, while Gui Santos added 22 off the bench. Golden State won the battle of bench scoring, 61-19.

"San Antonio and Golden State both play good basketball with their guys moving the ball, getting good looks," Murray said. "Golden State shoots more threes without Steph, and it's more of just kick off and catch and shoot. And obviously, they have guys like Melton get hot. Teams like that have won in the past that are short-handed — just know how to win games."

#2732. SpaceX wins FCC approval to deploy 1 million orbital data center satellites

The Federal Communications Commission has authorized Elon Musk's SpaceX to put 1 million artificial intelligence computing satellites in orbit, Chairman Brendan Carr announced on Wednesday.

Carr said in a post on X, the social network owned by SpaceX, that the company's Starmind constellation, and "competitor constellations will use solar power to extend America's lead" in AI.

Musk said in a reply, "Much appreciated. This will be a great asset to America and the world."

The FCC, in its authorization filing, noted that SpaceX's application "received a substantial number of comments from the public, including petitions to deny filed by Kuiper Systems LLC (Amazon Leo), Viasat Inc. (Viasat), and the American Astronomical Society (AAS)."

In a March petition urging the FCC to deny SpaceX's application, Amazon had labeled the company's Starmind project as a "lofty ambition rather than a real plan."

The e-commerce and cloud computing giant is building a competitor to SpaceX's Starlink satellite internet service, and said it would take SpaceX "centuries" to build the constellation, adding that the company provided scant details about how it would "deliver on these grand claims."

The petition resulted in a rebuke from Carr, who said Amazon "should focus on the fact" that it's falling short of its own satellite deployment milestone.

Amazon declined to comment on the FCC's approval.

Other companies have also set their sights on orbital data centers. Google last year announced Project Suncatcher, which it describes as a "moonshot to put AI in space," and launched its first chips into space on a SpaceX rocket and Planet Labs satellite in October.

In March, Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin petitioned the FCC to let it launch up to 51,600 satellites as part of a low Earth orbit satellite constellation dubbed "Project Sunrise." The constellation would work in tandem with Blue Origin's planned TeraWave satellite internet service, announced in January.

Astrophysicist and author Adam Becker was critical of the FCC move in an email to CNBC on Wednesday.

"Any serious attempt at this is going to further pollute the night sky and runs a serious risk of a cascading chain reaction of space junk collisions, which would be a disaster for all of humanity," he wrote. "Musk and Carr are either profoundly stupid themselves, or hoping the rest of us are too stupid to notice this is nonsense."

SpaceX did not respond to a request for comment.

A senior attorney with Earthjustice, Jan Hasselman, criticized the FCC for issuing its decision on a holiday week, and approving the SpaceX proposal without any environmental review, which he said was required by the National Environmental Policy Act.

The FCC said in its authorization that it dismissed "comments and concerns raised regarding topics over which the Commission has no authority, including environmental concerns," and concerns about its impact on "optical astronomy."
