#2731. Maple Leafs Must Have a Backup Plan for Simon Edvinsson

The Toronto Maple Leafs may view Simon Edvinsson as the ideal solution to their defensive needs.

Edvinsson is young, plays a premium position, and possesses the size and mobility that every contender covets. If the Detroit Red Wings ever make him available, there will be no shortage of teams willing to enter the bidding.

That's exactly why the Maple Leafs cannot afford to make him their only plan.

The reality is that acquiring Edvinsson was never going to be easy. The Maple Leafs just don't have the sort of trade capital that the Red Wings would be looking for to make Toronto the winning bidder.

And that situation leaves Toronto in a difficult spot.

The organization still needs help on the blue line regardless of whether Edvinsson ultimately becomes available. If the Leafs spend too much time focusing on one target, they risk tipping their hand more than they would like.

That's why having a backup plan may be just as important as pursuing Edvinsson himself.

There is a temptation to believe that one massive trade can turn the Maple Leafs' fortunes around.

Unfortunately, Simon Edvinsson is not going to become a savior for Toronto.

While there is no question that he can improve things for the organization, he won't automatically make the Leafs a Stanley Cup contender. Edvinsson can be one part of a broader strategy for the Maple Leafs.

That's the realization that about another dozen clubs have come to, as well. They see Simon Edvinsson as a great piece to have in their organization. And that's why the asking price is likely high.

That possibility should force Toronto to keep its options open.

The biggest issue here would be to think that paying up to get Edvinsson will magically solve numerous issues. Yes, getting the 23-year-old defenseman will help, but it would only be a start.

The Maple Leafs cannot allow themselves to fall into that trap.

If management believes the blue line requires reinforcements, it should be simultaneously exploring multiple solutions rather than putting all of its attention toward one difficult trade.

The good news for Toronto is that Edvinsson is unlikely to be the only defenseman available.

Every trade season produces unexpected names.

Some players become cap casualties. Others suddenly emerge because their teams fall out of contention. A few become available simply because organizations decide they need to make changes.

That reality should work in the Maple Leafs' favor.

While Edvinsson may represent the ideal fit, he is not the only fish in the sea. The problem is the perceived need to do something right now to "fix" the Maple Leafs.

Toronto's ultimate goal is not necessarily acquiring Simon Edvinsson. The goal is improving the defense corps enough to preclude the seemingly inevitable destiny that is a rebuild.

If another defenseman can help accomplish that second goal, the Leafs need to be prepared to pivot quickly.

The pursuit of Edvinsson makes plenty of sense. His age, skill set, and long-term upside make him one of the most attractive targets potentially available.

The Maple Leafs can certainly continue pursuing Simon Edvinsson. But if they want to maximize their chances of upgrading the roster, they would be wise to see what else is out there.

Just about every team that pursued Edvinsson will pivot almost immediately. That's why the Maple Leafs would do well to get a head start on everyone else, in case things don't go their way.

#2729. ‘Ready for any storm’: UAE minister optimistic on economy as IMF lifts forecast

The UAE is "very optimistic" about achieving 7%-8% growth next year, the country's economy and tourism minister Abdulla bin Touq Al Marri told CNBC after the latest data from the International Monetary Fund showed the country would avoid a recession in 2026.

The IMF last week revised the UAE's real GDP forecast to 0.2% growth in 2026 from a 3.9% contraction predicted a month ago. "The UAE is coming back, and that adjustment within one month shows that there is a huge potential aspect when it comes towards the end of the year," the minister told CNBC's Dan Murphy.

He said the tourism industry was already picking up with occupancy rates returning to "almost near normal" and international carriers including British Airways and Air France rerouting flights to the UAE. The minister expects economic growth to be higher than 0.2% this year.

The Iran war has disrupted economic activity in the UAE, with missile and drone attacks from Tehran and maritime disruptions weighing on energy trade, tourism, aviation and private-sector confidence.

Earlier this month, U.S. President Donald Trump said that renewed attacks on Iran were "highly unlikely" if negotiations produce a "very strong agreement." However, on Monday, Trump threatened that Tehran could face fresh U.S. strikes next year if the two countries failed to strike a deal, as per Fox News.

"We can we cannot promise the world for any storms that will land on our shore, but we can always promise that the UAE's economy is ready for any storm," Al Marri said.

He added that next year the UAE's economy will have a "different" model than the one before the conflict and investments will be focused on building factories and "things that we really need in times of crisis."

The World Bank in October said it expects the UAE's economy to recover next year, driven by rising oil production and exports and a rebound in tourism, trade, transportation, financial services, and construction.

It warned that the improvement in economic activity in the UAE largely depends on the duration and intensity of the Iran war, with a faster de-escalation likely to accelerate the recovery in services exports and private-sector activity.

#2724. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2722. Hedge funds are no longer just for the rich. They’re wooing everyday investors

For decades, hedge funds were the preserve of billionaires, pension funds and deep-pocketed institutional investors. Now, the industry is increasingly courting a new pool of capital: everyday investors.

The shift, dubbed by some as the "retailization" of hedge funds, comes as alternative asset managers seek new avenues for growth and investors look for ways to shield their portfolios from increasingly volatile equity markets.

Unlike traditional equity funds, which typically rely on stocks rising to generate returns, hedge funds can employ strategies including short selling, arbitrage and derivatives to seek profits across different market conditions.

For an industry already managing record levels of assets, individual investors represent a largely untapped growth opportunity, experts who spoke to CNBC said.

"Hedge funds are entrepreneurs. Their objective is to make as much money as they possibly can, both in returns and for their organization," said Don Steinbrugge, CEO of hedge fund consulting firm Agecroft Partners.

"So if they have the ability to go after a new market and grow their business, they're going to do that."

The push is coming from both hedge fund managers seeking to broaden their investor base and traditional asset managers looking to capitalize on growing demand for alternative investments, said Ken Heinz, president of hedge fund research firm HFR.

"Heightened geopolitical uncertainty, greater market volatility, inflation concerns, and the higher correlation between equities and bonds have raised questions about the effectiveness of conventional portfolio diversification," said Ricky Siao, a hedge fund specialist at Swiss private bank UBP.

The investment environment has become more challenging for investors who have historically relied on traditional allocations to equities and fixed income, he added.

As a result, investors are increasingly seeking exposure to strategies such as arbitrage, event-driven investing and quantitative macro strategies to reduce their dependence on equity market returns.

The democratization of hedge fund investing is taking shape through a growing array of investment vehicles, often with lower fees, smaller minimum investments and more frequent opportunities to withdraw money.

Some of the biggest names in the hedge fund industry are already moving in that direction.

Billionaire investor Bill Ackman's Pershing Square raised $5 billion in April for a publicly listed closed-end fund, giving everyday investors access to its investment expertise without the performance fees traditionally associated with hedge funds. The fund charges a 2% annual management fee.

British hedge fund giant Man Group, meanwhile, entered the U.S. ETF market last September and doubled its lineup to four actively managed funds just three months later.

The broader shift is also changing how hedge fund strategies are packaged for individual investors.

"What has changed most is the wrapper, not the strategy itself," said Yi Ling Ong, managing partner and chief operating officer at Golden Horse Fund Management.

In other words, hedge fund strategies are increasingly being packaged in more accessible vehicles such as mutual funds and ETFs, often with lower investment minimums and fewer restrictions on withdrawals.

"Investors may still be getting exposure to long/short equity, credit, or macro strategies, but delivered through vehicles that offer quarterly or monthly liquidity and standardized reporting."

In Europe, hedge fund managers have adapted strategies to fit regulated UCITS funds, which are accessible to individual investors. In the U.S., mutual funds and ETFs have similarly broadened access to alternative investment strategies.

In Asia, digital wealth platforms are helping bridge the gap between institutional and individual investors.

Singapore-based Endowus, which began offering hedge funds on its platform in 2022, has lowered minimum investments for semi-liquid funds to $50,000, while removing minimums altogether for some daily-liquid alternative funds, according to Chief Investment Officer Hugh Chung.

While most hedge funds on the platform remain restricted to accredited investors, a growing number of alternative funds are becoming available to non-accredited investors, Chung said.

India is seeing a similar shift through Specialized Investment Funds, or SIFs, which allow investors to access more sophisticated strategies through structures that more closely resemble traditional mutual funds, said Rajesh Singla of Alpha AMC.

Assets under management in India's Specialized Investment Funds have more than quadrupled since March to 426.7 billion rupees as of end-October, according to data from the Association of Mutual Funds in India.

The products carry a minimum investment of 1 million rupees, significantly below the entry thresholds typically associated with traditional hedge funds.

The shift is also making hedge fund-style investing cheaper.

Traditional hedge funds have long been associated with the "2 and 20" fee model, which typically charges a 2% annual management fee and taking 20% of investment profits.

While those fees have come under pressure over the years, retail-friendly alternatives such as hedge fund-like ETFs typically charge a single annual management fee of between 0.4% and 1.25%, according to Ong.

But lower fees does not necessarily mean better outcomes.

Retail investment vehicles often face tighter regulatory and liquidity requirements, which can limit managers' ability to replicate strategies used by traditional hedge funds.

Steinbrugge said those constraints can force managers to avoid less liquid securities that may offer some of the greatest opportunities for outsized returns.

"What investors are really choosing between is not just price, but structure," Ong said.

The industry's expansion into the retail market is also raising questions about whether individual investors fully understand the complexity of the strategies they are buying into.

Easier access does not eliminate the risks associated with leverage, short selling or illiquid investments.

"The key risk is that investors may underestimate the complexity and risk associated with these strategies simply because they are now more accessible," Singla said.

Liquidity is another concern, particularly for less liquid alternative investments being marketed through retail-friendly structures.

Some interval funds, which are increasingly being used to distribute private credit and other alternative investments to individuals, may offer to repurchase only 5% of their outstanding shares each quarter, Steinbrugge said.

That could leave investors unable to withdraw as much money as they want during periods of market stress.

Still, industry watchers largely see the rise of retail-friendly products as complementary to traditional hedge funds rather than an existential threat to them.

While the proliferation of more liquid, transparent and lower-fee products could intensify competition and pressure traditional fee structures, Siao does not expect retailization to fundamentally reshape the industry in the foreseeable future.

"I would expect this to be an incremental evolution rather than a fundamental transformation of the hedge fund business model," he said.

"Over time, the industry will likely remain segmented," Ong said. "Retail vehicles will address accessibility and scale, while hedge funds will continue to focus on depth, flexibility, and outcomes that are difficult to replicate in simplified formats."

#2719. South Korea upgrades growth forecast, raises benchmark rates to highest in two years

South Korea's central bank on Thursday hiked benchmark lending rates to their highest in two years, as inflation continues to run above target while a booming economy allows it room to tighten policy.

The central bank raised rates by 25 basis points to 3.25% for its final meeting of the year, in line with expectations from economists, data on the Bank of Korea's website showed.

Inflation is expected to remain above its 2% target level for a considerable time, the BOK added in its statement, saying this was due to the pass-through of elevated cost pressures and as demand-side pressures also gradually strengthen, driven by stronger economic growth. Inflation in the country stood at 2.8% in October.

The BOK also upgraded its growth forecasts for 2026 to 3.9% for 2026 and 4.1% for 2027, up from the 3.3% and 2.9%, respectively, forecast in August.

South Korea's third-quarter GDP grew by 3.4% year on year, beating expectations, allowing the central bank room to raise rates.

"The projections of high growth rates for next year and beyond also reflect the significantly increasing weight of the IT sector as a growth engine for the Korean economy," BOK Governor Shin Hyun-song said at a press conference following the decision.

Operating profits in the semiconductor sector could reach 700 trillion won, Shin told reporters, according to local news agency Yonhap.

Shin also signaled that more rate hikes, although he did not give a fixed timeline.

"While the domestic economy is expected to continue its robust growth, inflation is forecast to remain above the target level for a considerable time ... The Board, therefore, sees it warranted to continue its policy response until there is confidence that inflation will converge stably to the target level."

Gareth Leather, senior Asia Economist at Capital Economics said there is reason to believe that the BOK's tightening cycle was drawing to a close.

The BOK has raised rates by 75 basis points in five months, as higher energy prices from the Iran war and the AI boom has pushed up inflation in the country.

Leather said that the country's strong growth, as well as a strengthening won could ease concerns over inflation.

While rapidly rising property prices could strengthen the case for a prolonged tightening cycle, Leather said that the BOK was "more likely to favour macroprudential measures over further rate hikes to address its financial stability concerns."

After the decision, the country's benchmark Kospi rose 1.88%.

#2716. Beware higher correlation but nab high U.S. credit yields, fund managers say

The artificial intelligence boom could lead to a recession, whether through a collapse in AI investment or widespread job losses as the technology advances, a hedge fund manager said.

"I do worry that if we don't have an AI-led recession in a few years, we're going to have an unemployment-led recession," Sheru Chowdhry, founder and chief investment officer at DSC Meridian Capital, said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Joanna Ossinger at the iConnections Global Alts Asia conference in Singapore last week.

Chowdhry's warning highlights a risk that extends beyond a potential bursting of the AI bubble. Rapid advances in the technology could lead to widespread job losses, with implications for the housing market and consumer spending, he said.

"If this continues to go at the pace it is, I think in a few years a lot of us will probably not have jobs," Chowdhry said, adding that investors would have to rethink their approach to sectors such as housing and discretionary spending.

Even so, the fund manager remains bullish on computing power and sees opportunities in data centers, despite concerns about AI valuations.

The current AI investment cycle reminds Chowdhry of the dot-com bubble and the telecommunications infrastructure buildout of the early 2000s.

"This cycle reminds me of the dot-com bubble and the telecom build we had going on in the early 2000s," he said. "Every cycle is different, but it kind of rhymes in some ways."

Chowdhry also questioned whether semiconductor companies could sustain their profit margins in the coming years.

However, he sees opportunities in data centers, pointing to access to power and grid connections as major bottlenecks in the AI buildout.

"If you can identify data centers, I feel like that's going to be the picks and shovels of this cycle," he said.

In particular, data centers that can secure permits and access to power within the next two years present a significant investment opportunity, he added.

Beyond AI infrastructure, Chowdhry also pointed to attractive yields in U.S. credit markets.

He cited yields of around 8.5% on U.S. high-yield bonds and 9.5% on leveraged loans, describing the current market environment as an opportunity to "get paid to wait."

Investment portfolios may be more vulnerable to market volatility than they appear, according to Dan Gelernter, director of AI at R.G. Niederhoffer Capital Management, who spoke on the same panel.

Average monthly correlations across investable assets rose from around 5% between the 1960s and 1990s to roughly 11% to 12% by 2020, according to research cited by Gelernter.

His own analysis suggests that the figure has climbed further to around 15% to 16% in 2026.

"Most portfolios are more highly levered, less liquid, more highly correlated than they appear to be, and that problem will get worse the more markets move," Gelernter said.

Within the AI trade, Joo Kian Ong, a partner at AP3X Capital, said Samsung Electronics would be a good long position, with SK Hynix or Micron on the other side as potential shorts.

#2714. Inside India newsletter: Roaring economy, foreign investors’ return and now record auto sales

Hello, this is Priyanka Salve, writing to you from Singapore.

Welcome to the latest edition of "Inside India" — your one-stop destination for stories and developments from the world's fastest-growing large economy.

Despite 2026 being a challenging year with high global energy prices and supply chain disruptions, the Indian economy has fared surprisingly well. The auto industry, a key pillar of manufacturing and employment, has breached several records this year and is poised to report its best-ever sales.

This week, I spoke to experts to decode what this stellar performance means for one of the world's most promising consumer markets.

Any thoughts on today's newsletter? Share them with the team.

India's economy expanded at 7.8% in the June quarter, beating expectations, even as growth in several major economies cooled. Foreign portfolio investors who had been exiting Indian markets in droves for most of the year have also started returning in historic numbers.

And now, the world's third-largest auto market is set to for a record this year as better road connectivity, tax cuts, and easier financing options have fueled bumper sales over the last several months. That comes at a time when major auto markets across the world, from China to the U.S. have been seeing a slump in sales.

"10 years ago, despite having the money, people did not buy cars, but now even if they don't have the money, they take financing and buy," Amar Sheth, vice president of the Federation of Automobile Dealers Associations, told CNBC, pointing to the shift in consumer mindset.

They are becoming "more optimistic" about the future and, rather than waiting, consumers are willing to take a loan and purchase something useful to them now, he adds.

The industry body that Sheth represents said on Monday that automakers in India were poised to sell more than 30 million vehicles in the financial year ending March 2027, dubbing the sector as a "reflection of the wider India growth story."

From April to October, total vehicle retail sales in India reached 18.7 million units, up 10% from a year earlier, with car, tractor and commercial vehicle sales growing more than 15%, as per FADA data.

The optimism of the industry body is also shared by major automakers; several of whom have announced capacity expansion plans this year to meet the growing consumer demand.

Japanese automakers Maruti Suzuki and Toyota plan to raise capacity by 1.2 million units and 200,000 units, while Korea's Hyundai Motors will add capacity for 150,000 units, as per data shared by automotive industry intelligence provider Mobility Global. Leading Indian companies Tata Motors, Mahindra & Mahindra and JSW have also announced capacity addition plans, the data showed.

India is the only big auto market in the world that is growing while most others such as France, Germany, and Korea are "either declining or are flat," Puneet Gupta, director India and ASEAN at Mobility Global, told CNBC, explaining the reason behind car companies investing in the country.

Gupta said that the Chinese automotive market hit an inflection point in 2007, when the GDP per capita crossed $2,500, and now India is "at a similar point." He added that government support for the auto industry would be crucial in replicating the boom seen in the Chinese auto industry.

The catalyst for India's auto sector boom traces back to last September, when the government announced a wide range of tax cuts to spur domestic consumption.

The auto industry contributes 7.1% to the country's gross domestic product, 49% to manufacturing GDP, 15% to goods and services tax collection, and supports 30 million jobs, as per government data.

While the tailwinds from the tax cuts have faded, experts now believe that replacement demand and the growing reach of financing options to smaller cities will unlock the next phase of growth.

Consumption has been strong this year as the government has partly shielded people from rising energy costs while corporates, with their strong balance sheets, have been slow to pass on price hikes, Gaura Sengupta, chief economist at India's IDFC First Bank, said.

So far this year, wage growth has been good, the labor market has held up equity markets haven't corrected sharply, keeping the wealth effect intact, she said, underscoring the reasons driving consumption along with easy access to credit.

Despite the economy facing external shocks, producers are also "feeling confident to add capacity" as they have seen domestic demand sustain, Sengupta added.

However, experts said that the central bank's recent tightening of monetary policy and price hikes by automakers could slightly take the edge off the auto sales boom in the last few months of the financial year.

While urban demand is unlikely to get affected, rural sales growth could moderate, they added.

"Overall retail should close FY27 in high single digits," Diwakar Murugan, senior automotive analyst at Omdia, told CNBC, adding that this year's record sales are not a one-time spike but a "structural reset."

China has changed tactics but is still thwarting India's rise, a U.S. panel saysChina is "actively working to suppress India's rise," even as the relationship between the two major Asian powers has thawed in the past year, according to a U.S. panel that sees an opening for greater outreach to India.

U.K.-India trade deal faces a stiff test over Scotch whisky import taxesWithin months of taking effect, the India-U.K. trade deal is facing an early test, as New Delhi resists extending preferential tariffs to bulk Scotch whisky imports.

Investors give a thumbs down to Tata Steel-APL Apollo $1.67 billion deal, plunging sharesTata Steel, one of the world's leading steel producers, on Monday said it will buy a 26.4% stake in India's largest structural steel pipe and tube maker, APL Apollo, for 155.1 billion rupees ($1.67 billion). However, investors reacted negatively to the deal.

Nov. 27: Mahanadi Coalfields IPO listing.

Nov. 30: GDP data for July-September 2026.

#2713. Explained: Why Hardik Pandya trade between MI and RR collapsed at the last moment before PBKS move

Explained: Why Hardik Pandya trade between MI and RR collapsed at the last moment before PBKS move originally appeared on Cricket News. Add Cricket News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

RR were close to securing Hardik Pandya from MI ahead of IPL 2027.

RR owners and head coach Kumar Sangakkara reportedly travelled to Bengaluru for negotiations.

Talks broke down after MI demanded Jofra Archer be included in a proposed swap involving Yashasvi Jaiswal.

RR pulled out of the deal, opening the door for Hardik's move to PBKS in exchange for Arshdeep Singh.

MORE:Can You Find The IPL Connections? Play Now!

Mumbai Indians skipper Hardik Pandya was believed to be in contention to join Rajasthan Royals prior to IPL 2027, but RR ended up pulling out of talks at the last moment, which cleared the path for him to join Punjab Kings.

As reported by The Times of India, RR emerged as genuine bidders, with the RR owners and head coach Kumar Sangakkara making a trip to Bengaluru to have talks for a possible trade with MI.

As per the proposed trade, RR opener Yashasvi Jaiswal would have been exchanged for Hardik Pandya.

However, MI wanted England fast bowler Jofra Archer also in the deal as a condition for letting go of Hardik. RR did not find this condition acceptable and thus decided to pull out of negotiations.

With RR pulling out of talks, it lost the chance of signing an experienced Indian all-rounder and IPL-winning captain for the 2027 season. Pandya joined Punjab Kings in a trade deal alongside left-arm pacer Arshdeep Singh.

MORE:IPL 2027 mini-auction: Purse and budget for each team detailed

While RR decided to pull out of the game, Punjab Kings continued to show interest in acquiring Hardik Pandya and even decided to include their fast bowler Arshdeep Singh as part of the deal.

The transfer was concluded on November 11, with Hardik joining PBKS while Arshdeep joined MI.

Hardik's salary rose to ₹18 crore from his previous ₹16.35 crore MI salary, whereas Arshdeep's salary remained the same at ₹18 crore.

The transaction helped both parties to fulfil their requirements, as Punjab acquired an all-round player while Mumbai added some pace to their attack.

MORE:Explained: Why has MI's purse reduced ahead of IPL 2027 mini-auction?

Not being able to get Hardik could turn out to be a costly choice for Rajasthan Royals, considering what leadership skills he would bring along.

Hardik guided Gujarat Titans to victory in IPL 2022 and again to the finals in 2023. He also managed to clinch four titles with Mumbai Indians, making him a good catch for RR.

The franchise is expected to continue with Riyan Parag as captain for IPL 2027. Although he was able to guide his team to the playoffs in 2026, he is yet to make a name for himself as an effective player and leader.

RR might be justified in not accepting Mumbai Indians' offer for the deal if they demanded Jofra Archer from RR too. But Hardik not only offers his all-around skills but also a good captaincy record and experience of big games.

On the other hand, Punjab Kings seem to have made an intelligent choice in signing a talented all-rounder. What started as a great choice for Rajasthan Royals ended up being an even greater one for Punjab.

#2712. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2711. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2710. Tom Brady says ‘kind of crazy’ NFL move has elevated one star to ‘elite’ status in the league

Tom Brady is probably the one player who has seen all there is to see in the NFL, but he is still surprised by a "kind of crazy" move that has elevated one star to elite status in the league.

The usual suspects among the top stars in the league rarely change, like the Patrick Mahomes and Josh Allens of this world.

However, the true surprise comes when a player comes out of nowhere to upend expectations, and a single move triggers it.

Tom Brady identified that move for one player in the league who elevated himself to elite status after it happened.

The New York Giants were staring down the barrel of a wasted season when Jaxson Dart went down with a season-ending injury.

Dart was the Giants' quarterback of the future, given the keys to the present team so he could develop and make mistakes along the way.

However, once he went down, and Jameis Winston was clearly not up to the task, JJ McCarthy arrived from the Minnesota Vikings.

McCarthy had an immediate impact on the Giants, and despite Dart's injury, they currently sit at the top of the NFC East Division with a 6-3-1 record.

#2709. North Korea's Kim calls for nuclear arsenal expansion, says status 'will never change'

North Korean leader Kim Jong Un called for bolstering the country's nuclear arsenal and expanding the use of tactical nuclear weapons across multiple domains, state media reported Thursday, as Pyongyang reaffirmed its determination to retain its nuclear arms.

Kim's comments followed a working visit Wednesday to facilities involved in nuclear material production and weapons research, according to the Korean Central News Agency.

At the Nuclear Weapons Institute, he urged greater attention to "operationalizing" tactical nuclear weapons for a range of missions.

Kim directly linked the development of those weapons to North Korea "sharing a border with its most dangerous, hostile state," an apparent reference to South Korea.

The visit was part of Pyongyang's efforts to counter what Kim described as "the reckless expansion of adversary states' nuclear forces and military alliances," according to KCNA. He also singled out South Korea as a threat to North Korea's security and sovereignty.

Kim reiterated that North Korea's status as a nuclear-armed state was irreversible, dismissing "denuclearization rhetoric" and saying its nuclear status "will never change."

"[Our nuclear policy] is a core element of safeguarding the sovereignty of the Republic, enshrined in the supreme law of the state," he added.

The report did not identify the sites Kim visited or disclose any production figures.

The announcement followed an assessment last week by the International Atomic Energy Agency that another extension to Yongbyon's original uranium-enrichment facility had been externally completed by late October.

The nuclear watchdog has lacked access to North Korea since 2009 and monitors its activities using satellite imagery and open-source intelligence.

Citing what he portrayed as growing threats from Washington and its allies, Kim accused the U.S. of encouraging neighboring allies to acquire nuclear-powered submarines, pursuing nuclear sharing and the joint use of nuclear weapons.

His comments come after South Korea announced efforts to acquire nuclear-powered submarines after President Donald Trump backed the plan in October 2025, according to a White House joint fact sheet. Nuclear-powered propulsion does not itself mean that a vessel carries nuclear weapons.

Trump had expressed interest in meeting with Kim at the APEC summit in Shenzhen last week, according to Reuters, but no meeting took place and North Korea did not publicly respond to the proposal.

Trump denied that Kim had ignored him, adding that communications could "start up right away," in an interview with the New York Post Tuesday. He said the two leaders had not been in contact "recently."

"We've been off to other things," Trump added.

North Korea has continued to accelerate its nuclear weapons development and rebuff South Korean and U.S. attempts to resume dialogue since negotiations between Trump and Kim collapsed in 2019.

— CNBC's Jenny Lee contributed to this report.

#2708. Even the price of toys is on the rise. How Americans are scaling back holiday plans

As Amanda Brecht planned how her family would spend Thanksgiving this year, she had to make some difficult decisions.

Because of high gas prices, the 30-year-old decided she wouldn't drive across her home state of Michigan to celebrate with extended family. Instead, Brecht and her husband will spend the holiday with just their three kids. And with food prices rising, they'll be skipping appetizers and serving turkey breast instead of a whole bird.

"Thanksgiving is definitely going to look different this year," Brecht said. "Money doesn't grow on trees. You can only do so much."

With inflation accelerating, Americans like Brecht will see higher prices almost everywhere they look this holiday season. At a time of year synonymous with food, gifts and big spending, many consumers are instead pinching pennies, paring back their plans and making tough choices about how they'll celebrate.

Some are raiding their savings accounts and running up debt on credit cards. They're reporting downbeat outlooks about the economy in surveys. And they've become more selective with spending as inflationary pressures mount.

"Families are facing some tough choices this year," said Alexander Smith, an associate professor who researches behavioral economics at the Worcester Polytechnic Institute. "They might have to cut back on holiday spending in whatever way that they can."

A classic Thanksgiving feast will be harder to pull off as grocery prices rise.

The price of a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner increased 6% to $58.63 in 2026, according to an annual survey from the American Farm Bureau Federation. Faith Parum, the organization's deputy chief economist, said the price increase was a result of higher costs for labor and transportation.

Grocers including Walmart and Aldi have gained traction on social media by offering Thanksgiving meal bundles as a value-focused workaround to higher prices. Walmart's offering promises to feed 10 for $35, and includes a turkey and ingredients to make mashed potatoes, green bean casserole, cranberries, marshmallow-topped sweet potatoes and a pumpkin pie.

Still, some consumers will feel disappointed by having less choice in their menus due to higher prices, according to Joe Bruseulas, chief economist at tax firm RSM.

"We're missing the point here if all we're doing is focusing on the fact that Walmart will sell you a homogeneous dinner meal" at a discount, Bruseulas said. "People feel horrible that they have to go to that because the price of everything has just risen so significantly over the past five years."

Inflation is affecting more than Thanksgiving staples like turkey or stuffing. Food prices on average have jumped around 24% over the last five years, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Some items have seen even sharper increases: Coffee and ground beef, for example, have each climbed by more than 40% in the same period despite pulling back from recent highs.

At the same time, millions of Americans lost SNAP benefits under policy changes included in President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill." Food banks across the U.S. have seen foot traffic skyrocket this year as the reduction in federal nutrition aid coincides with rising prices in grocery aisles.

The Salvation Army said some of its food pantries in October provided double or triple the amount of groceries than they did in the same month a year ago. Demand is now so high that some food banks in Feeding America's national network are worried about running out of supplies.

"We want to make sure that our food banks are as full as they possibly can be going into the holiday season," said Linda Nageotte, Feeding America's president and operations chief. "Far too many of them have empty shelves."

High energy prices as a result of the war with Iran are also affecting Americans' holiday plans.

Slightly fewer Americans will travel at least 50 miles around the Thanksgiving holiday in 2026, marking the first year-over-year decline since the country was in lockdown from the pandemic in 2020, according to AAA. Stacey Barber, a vice president at the auto association, attributed this year's pullback in part to higher fuel costs.

AAA said that about nine out of every 10 Thanksgiving travelers will do so by car. While gas prices have come down from a May peak, they are still up about 18% year over year and could set a new record for the month of November, according to the organization's data.

Travelers are paying about 31% and 23% more for roundtrip domestic airfare around Thanksgiving and Christmas, respectively, than they did a year ago, according to booking platform Hopper. Airlines have blamed the ticket price hikes on rising jet fuel costs in the wake of the war.

"There will be a significant shell shock when they see the price of traveling," said Hayley Berg, lead economist at Hopper.

Berg said price increases would feel more noticeable on routes previously serviced by Spirit, the budget airline that shut down in May. Travelers are booking holiday flights earlier than they did in past years, a sign that prices are top of mind, she said.

Staying home for the holidays isn't a silver-bullet solution, either, as heating costs jump.

The average American household is expected to spend 7% more on home heating this winter than they did last year, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association. For the typical family, that equates to an increase of about $66 and a total bill for the season above $1,000.

To be sure, some families will feel it more than others. Heating oil, which is used mainly in homes in the Northeast, has jumped around 70% year over year. However, natural gas, which is one of the most common heating types in the U.S., costs about the same as last year.

Some U.S. states, like Massachusetts and New York, beefed up their home energy support programs to account for higher prices. But Mark Wolfe, NEADA's executive director, said federal aid offerings haven't been meaningfully expanded this year.

"Some of these increases are caused directly by the decision to go to war," Wolfe said. "Why isn't the federal government helping families pay these higher bills?"

The spirit of giving will take on a different meaning for consumers this year.

Retail sales during the all-important holiday season are expected to grow almost 5% this year and surpass $1 trillion for the first time, according to Bain & Company. But the consulting firm said most of that growth would come from inflation, rather than from consumers buying more products.

And Citi on Wednesday said that increased holiday spending would be mainly driven by higher earners this year. The bank said that's a result of the "K"-shaped economy, a term used to describe a scenario where better-off Americans splash out while others struggle.

Toy prices have climbed more than 9% over the past year and now sit at their highest levels since 2019, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Manufacturers have raised prices in response to higher costs for transportation and plastic, according to Chris Byrne, an independent toy industry analyst. The artificial intelligence boom has driven up prices for the chips that power gaming devices and other electronics.

Byrne said that's causing shoppers to focus more on the quality of gifts rather than quantity.

"The toy industry always reflects the culture at large," Byrne said. "It's almost true economically as well. As inflation is rising, people are being a little bit more cautious about what they're buying."

Just 16% of respondents to a Morning Consult survey said they planned to spend more in the 2026 holiday season than they did a year ago. Respondents who were older or had lower earnings were more likely to say their budgets were shrinking, the survey found.

Some are doing away with gifts altogether. A poll from consulting firm TSG and the Electronic Transactions Association found 5% of Americans did not plan to buy any presents in 2026, up three percentage points from last year.

One shopper, Wendy Cooper, set a limit of one gift for each of her three adult children this year. For her teenage daughter, the 56-year-old Atlanta resident has scoured Five Below, the trendy value retailer, for budget-friendly presents.

"It's super stressful," Cooper said. "You have to weigh: Do you guys want lights on and a place to live? Or do you want gifts?"

Across the board, the Bureau of Labor Statistics' consumer price index reaccelerated after the U.S. first struck Iran in late February. Annual inflation rates have begun rising faster than wages in the same period, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Even if Americans keep spending for the holidays, economists caution that high prices during this busy shopping period can further drag on their economic outlooks. In the CNBC Voter Poll early this month, about three-fourths of participants rated the economy as poor or not good. Consumer confidence gauges had dropped significantly this year, but have regained ground in the runup to the holiday shopping season.

From her home in Northern Michigan, Brecht is doing everything she can to make Christmas feel special without breaking the bank.

The graduate student has been delivering DoorDash orders in her spare time and listing clothes on resale platforms to save up money for her kids' holiday gifts. Brecht is watching closely for discounts and will look for potential presents at thrift stores in the coming weeks.

If necessary, Brecht said she would turn to credit cards for some holiday purchases. She isn't alone: TSG and ETA's survey found that about one-third of Americans said they expected to go into debt during the holiday season, a roughly 74% increase from 2025.

"I don't really know how we're going to do it," Brecht said. "I'm just praying for good Black Friday deals."

#2707. Javy Guerra Set to Return as A's Bullpen Coach in 2027

The rest of Mark Kotsay's staff came a little clearer into focus on Wednesday, as Javy Guerra announced on LinkedIn that he'll be the Athletics MLB bullpen pitching coach in 2027. This is a role that he took over at the All-Star break after Scott Emerson was fired as the team's pitching coach and Dan Hubbs was promoted from bullpen coach to pitching coach.

When the A's announced Casey Weathers (pitching coach) and Trevor Amicone (hitting coach) would be joining Kotsay's staff, Athletics Roundtable reached out to the organization to see if Hubbs would be returning to his previous role, and a representative said they would be making an announcement on the whole coaching staff at a later date.

Both Hubbs and Guerra are listed on the team's website as the A's bullpen coach, though Guerra has made his announcement. Either the club is adding a new role or a secondary bullpen coach, or Hubbs is not slated to be part of the coaching staff.

The A's have been adding new voices this offseason, with Weathers and Amicone bringing a different set of skills to the team's coaching staff, and the green and gold announced on Wednesday that they have hired Ian Walsh to be the team's new Director of Pitching and Max Gordon to be the team's new Director of Hitting.

According to the press release, the pair will be leading development efforts of the A's minor league pitchers and hitters, and will oversee a staff that aims to produce high-end talent by working in conjunction with the baseball operations staff to develop and maintain a year-round program of evidence-based training.

They will also partner with the major league staff to contribute to organization-wide pitching and hitting philosophies.

The reason this is important is that the players up and down the system will be hearing a consistent message throughout the minor leagues, as well as when they get to the big leagues.

During the season, Kotsay mentioned during one press conference that he hoped top pitching prospect Jamie Arnold was working on his changeup because there had been a number of arms that had reached the A's big-league roster and didn't have a working change just yet.

With these added layers being put in place this winter, the hope is that the A's development pipeline will begin producing more players that are ready to go when they reach the big leagues.

#2706. ‘Your booth is waiting’: Denny’s welcomes Jensen Huang back as Nvidia CEO

Jensen Huang is back at the helm of Nvidia — and Denny's has a booth waiting for him.

The American diner chain welcomed the billionaire's return as Nvidia CEO by revealing that it had reinstated a commemorative plaque marking the birthplace of the artificial intelligence chip giant after learning of its removal on Nov. 13.

"Some things you just don't take off the menu. Jensen Huang is one of them," Denny's said in a post on X.

The plaque marks the booth at a Denny's restaurant in Silicon Valley, California, where Huang and his co-founders discussed the idea for Nvidia, which was founded in 1993.

The restaurant chain said it learned on Nov. 13, a day after Huang's resignation, that the plaque had been removed.

"We put the plaque right back up where it belongs," Denny's said, without specifying who had removed it or why.

Huang's ties to the diner chain, however, stretch back well before Nvidia's founding. He worked at Denny's as a teenager, washing dishes and serving customers before going on to build one of the world's most valuable companies.

The restaurant chain also disclosed that it had contacted Nvidia's communications team on Nov. 10 to express its pride in their shared history.

Huang's return came less than two weeks after his sudden resignation rattled investors and raised questions about corporate governance and succession planning at the chipmaker.

While his return has put those issues in the spotlight, Denny's offered a simpler message to its former employee.

"Jensen, your booth is waiting and the coffee's on. We're always open, 24/7," the restaurant chain said.

#2705. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2703. Norse Atlantic flights suspended after warning finances 'critical'

Some Norse Atlantic flights were cancelled on Wednesday and Thursday, raising concerns about the future of the low-cost long-haul airline shortly after it warned that it was in a "critical financial situation."

Shares of Norse closed 53.3% lower on Wednesday after plunging as much as 86% during the session.

The airline had been set to operate two Pakistan International Airlines flights Wednesday under a wet-lease agreement, in which one airline provides the aircraft and crew to another. The agreement had only been signed in early October.

But PK786 from London to Islamabad and PK710 from Manchester to Lahore were cancelled because of "an unexpected operational suspension," the Pakistani flag carrier said on X.

Norse Atlantic has yet to make an official statement on its operational status. Checks by CNBC showed the airline was still selling tickets as at 1:30 a.m. Oslo time on Thursday.

PIA said it would operate the routes using its own Boeing 777 aircraft from Wednesday.

Norse's own flight Z0 783 from London Gatwick to Orlando was also shown as canceled Wednesday on live air traffic website Flightradar24. London Gatwick's website listed the flight's status for Thursday as "enquire airline."

In a bid to stave off financial troubles, Norse cut 75 jobs in May as part of an effort to reduce costs by $50 million, after Eivind Roald replaced founder Bjorn Tore Larsen as CEO in November last year.

The Norwegian carrier said Wednesday that its situation depended on securing financing for short-term liquidity needs and that it was in discussions about a potential strategic takeover.

"Norse is also engaged in discussions with selected parties on short term financing, however, there can be no assurances that such discussions will result in a viable solution," the airline said.

India's largest airline, low-cost carrier IndiGo, entered an agreement with Norse Atlantic in early 2025 to lease six 787-9 aircraft as it launched flights to Europe and Britain ahead of the arrival of its own widebody planes.

The agreement concluded at the end of October this year, with IndiGo citing airspace constraints, elevated fuel costs and currency pressures.

At its peak in 2024, Norse Atlantic had 4,822 scheduled flights. That number dipped to 4,695 in 2025 and 2,565 in 2026, according to aviation analyst Cirium. The airline had 122 flights scheduled for November this year.

Norse Atlantic CEO Roald told CNBC in May that he was not "overly concerned" about access to fuel and said the airline had no indication at the time that the crisis would have an immediate impact. The airline said in October that it was continuing to operate at reduced capacity because of "prolonged high fuel prices."

South Korean beauty stocks jumped Thursday as the country's skincare brands dominated Amazon's bestseller rankings ahead of Black Friday.

Shares of K-beauty giant APR climbed 3.77%, while cosmetic contracts manufacturers Kolmar Korea and Cosmax gained 5.46% and 2.91%, respectively.

APR shares, in particular, have rallied sharply this week, up 28%, defying a broader market sell-off in Seoul markets, with the benchmark Kospi index down 2.65% during the same period.

A cosmetic sector index tracked by Meritz Securities had gained 12% this week until Wednesday, according to a Thursday note from the brokerage.

The rally reflects renewed optimism over earnings growth following strong performances by K-beauty brands across major e-commerce platforms in the U.S. and Europe with Black Friday sales already underway, as well as increasingly attractive valuations following months of share price declines, according to Meritz.

K-beauty products topped Amazon's skincare bestseller rankings across six major markets — the U.S., the U.K., Germany, France, Spain and Italy — as of Nov. 25, according to data compiled by Meritz Securities. Other Korean beauty brands, including Biodance, Illiyoon and Anua, also ranked among the top 10 in the U.S., the data showed.

As of Thursday morning, Korean beauty products occupied several of the top spots in Amazon's U.S. beauty and personal care category, the e-commerce website showed.

Meritz Securities expects APR shares to maintain their positive momentum through the year-end, with a more substantial rebound in early 2027.

The brokerage named APR and Cosmax as its top picks in the cosmetics sector. Korean beauty brands have established an "overwhelming presence" in the bestseller rankings of major e-commerce platforms during Black Friday sales, prompting the market to reassess the growth's potential, the brokerage said.

#2700. Warriors' offense, sparked by De'Anthony Melton, kicks into gear in win over Kings

Golden State Warriors guard Brandon Williams would know better than anyone that what De'Anthony Melton is doing is not quite normal. A month ago - posed a question as to whether Melton ever shot like this in high school - Williams curled up his nose in mock confusion.

"Melt?" said Williams, Melton's former teammate at Crespi High in Los Angeles, after the Warriors' win over the Clippers on Nov. 1. "F- no."

Melton is the inverse, now, of what he was. He rose to prominence in Southern California high school ball as a sort-of first-option Gary Payton II, a flasher and cutter and mid-post operator who would do everything on a basketball court without much being able to shoot. Years later, the Warriors signed Melton in 2024 because he could shoot. Nobody can quite appreciate that turnaround like Williams.

"For him to come here, and start raining threes," Williams said, "I'm like, 'This must be a Warriors thing, bruh.'"

This was a Warriors thing again on Wednesday night, in a 129-110 win over Sacramento. From Melton, who tied a career high with eight threes in a 24-point eruption. From - well, everyone, as a hapless Kings defense decided to duck under screens time and time again. And a lid that had sealed off the rim for Golden State for five straight games popped off, as a 27% clip from the three-point line in five previous games flipped into a 20-for-44 night from downtown against the Kings.

Melton, who'd gone 1-for-12 across his previus three games, caught fire early and nailed a couple more fourth-quarter triples. Reserve Georges Niang, in an utter dry spell, hit three threes. And Gui Santos, banged up for much of the start to this season, finished with a season-best 22 points.

3-Point Goals: 14-40, .350 (Hunter 3-6, Sharp 3-6, Acuff Jr. 3-12, Murray 2-6, Sabonis 1-1, Achiuwa 1-2, Black 1-5, Monk 0-2).

Turnovers: 18 (Acuff Jr. 5, Sharp 3, Achiuwa 2, Cardwell 2, Murray 2, Black, Karaban, Monk, Sabonis).

Steals: 6 (Achiuwa, Hunter, Monk, Murray, Sabonis, Sharp).

3-Point Goals: 20-44, .455 (Melton 8-12, Niang 3-6, Podziemski 3-6, Santos 2-4, Green 2-5, Payton II 1-2, Richard 1-2, Bassey 0-1, Horford 0-1, Williams 0-2, Lendeborg 0-3).

Blocked Shots: 7 (Bassey 2, Horford, Lendeborg, Melton, Podziemski, Williams).

Turnovers: 15 (Lendeborg 4, Melton 4, Bassey 2, Green 2, Horford, Niang, Williams).

Steals: 9 (Lendeborg 3, Santos 2, Melton, Payton II, Podziemski, Richard).

Golden State will release an update Friday after re-evaluating star Stephen Curry, who now has missed six games while rehabbing a torn oblique abdominal muscle. Their consistent struggles with health, though, have gone much deeper than simply missing Curry - or even Jimmy Butler, Moses Moody and Kristaps Porzingis. Slowly, and not a moment too soon, these Warriors are returning to some partial semblance of full strength.

At the start of the second quarter, backup point guard Brandon Williams touched the floor in his first action since a hamstring strain suffered in a Nov. 9 win over the Pelicans. For years, through chronic knee issues earlier in his youth and heaps of thankless buckets in the G League, Williams has kept his motor running by simply wanting to hoop. He is a self-made Southern California grinder, getting up hundreds of shots in his old high school gym this summer while waiting for a veteran-minimum deal that took a long while to come.

For two weeks, he sat on Golden State's bench, nursing that hamstring and stewing at the sight of an offense that failed to generate much of any dribble penetration without him.

"After it happened - the first couple days, I didn't really do too much, so I'm just itching to play basketball," Williams said Tuesday, recounting the hamstring injury. "And everybody here knows I love basketball. So if I get on the court, I probably - may be doing a little bit too much, and they'll drag me off of it."

The very instant the Warriors lifted the red flag on Williams on Wednesday night, he hit the gas, the trait that changed the dynamics of Golden State's non-Curry units early this season. Just 30 seconds into his return, he down-shifted into eighth gear for a driving layup. And 20 seconds after that, he hit full turbo, turning purple Sacramento jerseys into mere blurs in an and-one transition finish as Melton scrunched his nose in gleeful disgust from the bench.

The rust, now, didn't shake this fast. After a rip-roaring 44-point first quarter - their highest output of the season - a suddenly high-octane Golden State offense began spinning its gears, as Williams found no traction on congested drives to the rim. An 0-for-5 stretch for the human teleporter beget a backslide for the Warriors and a sudden magnet-ball third quarter for Sacramento, as the Kings hit seven threes in the first eight minutes of the second half to cut the Warriors' lead, which once was 29, to four.

Williams' ability to single-handedly fight the tide, though - an element Golden State hasn't had with Curry down in a stretch of sheer offensive futility - loomed large here. He flew past a Kings turnstile for a late third-quarter layup, sneering as Sacramento called a timeout. Two assists came in quick succession for a Niang three and a Payton layup, and Golden State suddenly had a cushion again that it wouldn't relinquish.

#2698. Judge blocks FCC early-renewal process for Disney-owned ABC stations, for now

A judge has temporarily blocked the Federal Communications Commission from continuing its early renewal of a set of broadcast station licenses for Disney-owned ABC.

Judge Loren L. AliKhan of U.S. District Court in Washington granted Disney the preliminary injunction it sought and denied the FCC's attempt to dismiss the suit.

"This is not a close case," wrote the federal judge. "Given the extreme, indeed existential, nature of the possible penalty Plaintiffs face—loss of their broadcast licenses—the court concludes that early license renewal proceedings create a risk sufficient to chill a broadcaster of ordinary firmness."

The broadcast network had filed the lawsuit and followed the FCC beginning its early review of the ABC licenses. The government agency cited concerns around ABC's diversity, equity and inclusion efforts as the reason behind the start of the early renewal — years ahead of their scheduled expiration.

"Today's decision is an important victory for the First Amendment," said an ABC spokesperson.

An FCC spokesperson said in a statement that the part of the decision granting ABC's motion "is wrong on the facts and wrong on the law," the Associated Press reported.

In her ruling, AliKhan said that early license renewal proceedings came over a year into the FCC's enforcement push against ABC, starting with an investigation into Disney's employment practices in March 2025 and leading to the renewal proceedings in April this year.

ABC on Tuesday had said that it continued to "face irreparable harm" from the administration, which "has been acting" through the FCC, which is run by President Donald Trump appointee Brendan Carr.

AliKhan, a Joe Biden appointee, noted in her ruling that ABC had to change how it handled guest appearances for top shows "The View" and "Jimmy Kimmel Live!" with no candidate for public office appearing on the former and a senatorial candidate's appearance on the latter uploaded to YouTube instead of being broadcast.

"The prospect of license non-renewal has also factored into plaintiffs' calculus of when and how they cover President Trump and political topics," said the judge.

ABC had alleged that Trump and Carr have been making comments "intended to chill their protected speech," and that such comments have escalated since the 2024 presidential election.

The White House and the FCC didn't immediately respond to requests for comment from CNBC.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan contributed to the story

#2696. Vance says he was assured Jensen Huang was not under DOJ investigation

Vice President JD Vance said Wednesday that Attorney General Todd Blanche told him Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang was not under investigation.

"I was told by the Department of Justice and multiple officials in our national security apparatus that he was under no investigation," Vance told reporters in Ohio on Wednesday after being asked what assurances he had gotten that Huang was not under investigation. "Not even that he was about to be prosecuted; that he's under no investigation."

Vance spoke two days after President Donald Trump told CNBC in an interview that an investigation had been done.

Trump was asked what Blanche had told him about potential criminal liability for Huang and the question of whether Nvidia chips had ended up in China in violation of U.S. export laws.

"They did a pretty good investigation, and they felt that he, himself, was exonerated," Trump said Monday.

Reuters reported Wednesday that the Department of Justice did not conduct a formal investigation into Huang before Trump pushed for his reinstatement as CEO.

Blanche had concluded there was no evidence Huang had broken any laws based on investigations into Nvidia clients Super Micro Computer and Megaspeed, according to the report.

The DOJ did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the Reuters report. And the White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on Vance saying Huang was under no investigation when Trump had said there was one. Nvidia didn't immediately respond to a request for comment about Vance's remarks.

Blanche on Tuesday dodged questions about whether there had been an investigation.

"What the president said is that he talked to a lot of people going into the decision that he made to encourage the board to reinstate him," Blanche said after being asked whether the DOJ had investigated Huang and, if so, what were the results of the probe.

Huang was reinstated as CEO of Nvidia on Monday, after the chipmaker's board of directors said it had received "input from senior levels of the United States government" that Huang was not a national security risk. Trump took credit for Huang's reinstatement in the interview with CNBC shortly after Nvidia's announcement.

Huang resigned following The New York Times reporting multiple affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing and who was an executive for the Singapore-based cloud provider Megaspeed.

The reporting added fuel to allegations that Nvidia should have known its artificial intelligence chips were being diverted to China and being used by Chinese companies, violating U.S. export laws.

"The product they create is a very important part of us staying ahead of the SI arms race with China. We really do care about ensuring those chips do not fall into the wrong hands," Vance said, using the Trump administration's preferred acronym for AI, which the president calls "super intelligence."

"We actually see this is an opportunity for Nvidia to focus on complying with American export controls, and we think Jensen Huang is going to take that very seriously," Vance continued.

"We received the assurances that he was not under any significant — he was under no investigation — and the accusations against him were based on anonymous reporting, not on actual evidence," Vance said.

On Tuesday, Trump left the door open to pardon Huang in an interview with the New York Post, but Vance said on Wednesday that the CEO doesn't need one.

"I don't think he needs a pardon at all," Vance said. "I haven't seen any evidence again that he committed a crime. And if there is evidence that he committed a crime, then that's up to relevant authorities to actually bring a prosecution against him."

Huang is scheduled to appear for a closed-door interview with House committees about his dealings with Megaspeed on Tuesday. At the same time, Nvidia will hold its annual conference where it showcases new technology in Washington.

— Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2695. Rams vs. Packers final score: L.A. wins their biggest game of the year

The Los Angeles Rams beat the Green Bay Packers by a final score of 26-13 in the first ever Thanksgiving Eve NFL Game. Blake Corum powered the Rams to this win with 184 yards on the ground with 32 carries. It was an incredible performance by the third-year player. L.A.'s defense also forced a fumble and two interceptions to support their offense, and notch this crucial win for the Rams and their playoff odds.

The Rams jumped out to a 6-0, and then 13-3 lead in the second quarter when Kyren Williams found the endzone for the first touchdown of the day, but the Packers scored 10 unanswered point of their own to tie the game at 13-13 going into halftime.

Christian Watson was on pace for a massive game with four receptions for 65 yards and score, but a hit by Kam Kinchens ended Watson's night with a concussion. Kinchens was ejected for the hit on Watson as both players saw their nights end early.

The Rams drove down the field to open the second half and when Blake Corum found the endzone for his first touchdown of the day, the Rams had a 20-13 lead after the extra point. L.A.'s defense forced a Green Bay punt which gave the Rams a chance to take a two-score lead, but Matthew Stafford threw an interception to give life back to the Packers.

Jordan Love then threw an interception of his own to give the Rams the ball back, but the Rams failed to add any points off the turnover, keeping the score 20-13 and a one possession game.

With about six minutes to go in the fourth quarter, L.A. tacked on their third field goal to take a 23-13 lead and a two-score lead. With under four minutes to go, and with the Pack driving, Love threw his second interception of the game setting up the Rams to chew clock and forcing the Packers to burn timeouts as L.A. tried to put the game away.

The Rams added a final field goal to take a 26-13 lead with 30-seconds remaining in the contest, and that was enough for the Rams. Sean McVay beat Matt LaFleur for the first time in his career. L.A. earned their most impressive win of the 2026 season, and now they get some time off before they get back to work and look to move their win streak to six games when they face the Kansas City Chiefs on Thursday Night football.
