#2693. U.S. proposes new tariffs on 14 trading partners, including China and the EU

The U.S. is preparing new tariffs on 14 economies, including China, the EU, Japan and India, following investigations into what the Trump administration says are excess manufacturing practices that disadvantage U.S. commerce.

The administration launched those and other trade investigations in March, weeks after the Supreme Court unraveled a major piece of President Donald Trump's tariff-heavy economic policy by striking down his "liberation day" duties. Trump has since been accused by critics of his tariffs of trying to revive the canceled duties using other legal authorities.

The latest action targets foreign economies that create, sustain or contribute to structural excess capacity and production in manufacturing sectors that the U.S. finds unreasonable and burdensome or restrictive to its commerce, a senior U.S. official told reporters Tuesday evening.

The new tariffs are "targeted," not blanket rates, and will vary for each economy, and many will not exceed the rates established in previously reached trade deals, said the official, who spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the forthcoming duties.

The rates, which mostly range from 15% to 20%, are "not really additive" and generally will not stack on top of existing duties, U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC's "Squawk Box" Wednesday morning.

"In effect, it'll look more like a top up," Greer said. "When it all nets out, it really will just top up a few percentage points."

An exception is China, which faces a 20% rate under the new tariff policy, "so it will go up some," Greer said. But at the same time, both countries are moving to remove tariffs on some of each other's goods in what Greer called "stability-generating activity through this action."

The senior U.S. official said Tuesday that the 20% rate will come on top of the so-called forced technology transfer-related tariffs of roughly 25%, which were implemented during Trump's first presidential term. U.S. tariffs on Chinese goods will therefore reach about 45%, give or take for different goods, the official said.

Average U.S. tariffs rates on Chinese goods stood at about 36.5% as of July, according to the Congressional Research Service.

Greer also said on CNBC that the new policy likely will not take effect for months.

"We've talked to our trading partners about this. They know what we're proposing," he said. "I think a lot of this is baked in, and I don't think any of these would actually take effect earlier than a couple months from now."

The administration says the tariffs target what it calls "overcapacity" — excess industrial production, often supported by government subsidies, that allows cheaper goods to flood global markets and undercut U.S. producers.

China has disputed the allegations. "The so-called 'overcapacity' does not really exist," China's foreign ministry said Wednesday before the official reports were released by the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative, according to Reuters.

The overcapacity levies are being imposed under Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974. The Trump administration invoked the same statute to launch investigations into 60 economies over alleged forced-labor issues.

Those investigations, which also began in March, led to broad tariffs of 10% to 12.5% on goods from dozens of countries covering 99.4% of U.S. imports.

The Section 301 actions came weeks after the Supreme Court struck down the near-global tariffs that Trump had enacted under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act.

Shortly after the court's ruling, Trump invoked Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974 to impose a 10% global tariff. That tariff expired after 150 days, and was immediately replaced with the forced-labor-related duties.

An ongoing lawsuit accuses the Trump administration of citing forced-labor issues as a "pretext" in pursuit of reviving Trump's worldwide tariff regime.

#2692. Victor Wembanyama’s nickname ‘around the league’ revealed in statpadding discussion

Victor Wembanyama is known by a particular nickname for some of his outspoken takes about his fellow NBA stars, according to an NBA insider.

Victor Wembanyama has come into the NBA with a very unique, but firm identity. Even as he vies to become the next face of the league, the young Frenchman has shown that he will never back down from speaking about the things he believes in.

Wemby has surprised fans by vocally speaking out against issues that top athletes often don't. He has criticised sports betting, and the rampant promotions of it that have become prevalent in sports. The 22-year-old has spoken out against drinking and party culture. He recently called out some of his peers for 'statpadding' too. All of that has apparently led to a hilarious development.

Naturally, when a superstar is vocally critical of his colleagues, it tends not to go over too well. In his short time in the league, Wembanyama has blazed his own path, and that has not gone unnoticed.

#2690. How Trump’s latest tariff moves hand India an edge over rival Asian exporters

The latest U.S. tariff rates announced Wednesday have signaled that Washington is willing to offer India an edge over its competitors if New Delhi agrees to finalize a long-pending trade deal.

The Office of the United States Trade Representative has proposed a tariff of 17% on Indian exports if New Delhi "were to reach an Agreement on Reciprocal Trade" with Washington. It added that without a trade deal, India could face tariffs of up to 25% under Section 301 investigations encompassing excess production capacity that harms U.S. interests.

Meanwhile, the USTR has proposed tariffs of 18% on Bangladesh, Cambodia, Thailand, and 20% on China and Vietnam, subject to their respective trade deals, handing India a competitive advantage over rival exporters.

The U.S. expects the announcement of a lower tariff rate will encourage New Delhi to sign off on a trade deal before the G20, Atman Trivedi, partner at Washington-based DGA Albright Stonebridge Group, told CNBC.

India has got a "better bargain with Washington than its main competitors," and it may be enough for the Indian government "to declare victory and secure domestic backing for the deal," Trivedi said.

In February, U.S. President Donald Trump had announced a deal with India, claiming that New Delhi had accepted to "Buy American" at a much higher level after the U.S. agreed to slash tariffs to 18% from 50% earlier.

The India-U.S. trade deal has been pending with both sides indicating there was little room to grant concessions, while the Supreme Court annulling Trump tariffs allowed New Delhi to demand preferential tariffs that give it an edge over competitors.

"Today's announcement increases the urgency of reaching an agreement," Ronak D. Desai, visiting fellow at Hoover Institution, Stanford University, told CNBC. He added that while the prospect of a 17% tariff may strengthen the economic case for the deal, the threat of a 25% tariff could stir the sensitivity of the Indian government.

"The Modi government will be particularly sensitive to any perception that India is conceding under American Pressure," Desai said.

India's commerce ministry on Wednesday said it "does not have structural excess capacity in any sector," denying the findings of the USTR investigation, but added that it remains committed to "working closely with the United States towards the early conclusion of the India-U.S. Bilateral Trade Agreement."

Despite India facing steep tariffs, which were raised to 50%, New Delhi's trade surplus with the U.S. rose to $57.3 billion in 2025 from $45.2 billion in 2024.

Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi and Trump are likely to meet in December for the G20 leaders' meeting in Florida, and several experts believe the two leaders could seal the trade deal on the sidelines of the summit.

#2689. South Korea's Kospi leads gains as tech rebound lifts Asia markets

Asia-Pacific markets rose Thursday, tracking Wall Street gains overnight, with tech stocks rebounding from steep losses in the prior session.

Investors appear to have looked past a fresh set of U.S. tariffs unveiled by the White House on Wednesday, targeting more than a dozen major trading partners, including China and the European Union.

South Korea's Kospi gained 1.73%, while the small-cap Kosdaq index advanced 2.32% after the country's central bank hiked benchmark lending rates by an expected 25 basis points, as inflation continues to run above target while a booming economy allows it room to tighten policy.

The central bank raised rates to 3.25%, a 2-year high, in its final meeting of the year, data on the Bank of Korea's website showed.

Japan's Nikkei 225 rose 1.21% while the Topix edged 0.71% higher. Australia's S&P/ASX 200 was up 0.15%. Hong Kong's Hang Seng index rose 1.46% while mainland China's CSI 300 added 0.12%.

Asian technology stocks rebounded sharply Thursday, led by Japanese chip-related companies.

SoftBank Group surged more than 11%, while chip-testing equipment maker Advantest rose 7.3%. Lasertec and Disco both gained more than 6%, while Tokyo Electron climbed 4.6%.

In South Korea, memory chip giants SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics advanced more than 4% and 2.1%, respectively. The gains followed a bruising session Wednesday amid renewed concerns over the sustainability of artificial intelligence spending.

Cryptocurrencies also traded higher, with bitcoin rising 1.58% to $60,944.60, while ether gained 1.15% to $1,702.96.

The gains come amid lingering concerns over the outlook for digital assets, with the co-founder of now-shuttered crypto exchange BitMEX Arthur Hayes telling CNBC on Tuesday that bitcoin could test $50,000 if the Nasdaq continues to slide.

#2688. Nick Saban Reveals Where Alabama Stands in National Championship Race

TUSCALOOSA, Ala. — Nick Saban remains one of the most influential voices in college football, even after stepping away from the Alabama sideline.

However, the legendary Crimson Tide coach recently offered an assessment that could give Alabama fans something to debate around the Thanksgiving table.

With the Iron Bowl approaching and the College Football Playoff race intensifying, Saban identified three programs he believes possess the ingredients to win a national championship.

During a Friday, Nov. 20, appearance on ESPN's "The Pat McAfee Show," Saban discussed the characteristics that separate legitimate national championship contenders from the rest of the field.

The seven-time national champion identified Georgia, Utah and Notre Dame as three particularly complete teams.

His reasoning centered on quarterback play, defensive performance and the ability to establish a productive running game.

Saban believes those three programs have demonstrated the balance necessary to navigate the postseason.

However, the former Alabama coach stopped short of placing his old program alongside them.

The omission stands out as Alabama prepares for one of its most important games of the season.

Although Alabama did not make Saban's three-team shortlist, the former coach did not dismiss the Crimson Tide's championship chances.

Instead, he acknowledged that several additional programs could realistically compete for the national title.

"This is no disrespect to Alabama," Saban said during the ESPN appearance.

He subsequently included the Crimson Tide among a larger group of approximately seven or eight teams capable of winning the championship.

Nevertheless, Saban suggested that Alabama and other contenders have weaknesses that separate them from his preferred three.

The distinction offers an interesting perspective from the coach who built Alabama into college football's dominant program.

Saban won six national championships during his 17 seasons in Tuscaloosa before retiring following the 2023 season.

Now, his position as an ESPN analyst allows him to evaluate the Crimson Tide alongside the rest of the country.

Saban's assessment arrives as Alabama prepares to host Auburn on Saturday at Bryant-Denny Stadium.

The Crimson Tide enter the rivalry matchup with a 9-2 record and remain firmly involved in the College Football Playoff conversation.

Meanwhile, Auburn brings a 7-4 record into Tuscaloosa under first-year coach Alex Golesh.

Alabama has won six consecutive Iron Bowls, including victories in each of Kalen DeBoer's first two seasons.

However, the Tigers have an opportunity to complicate Alabama's postseason outlook with an upset.

The Crimson Tide must also demonstrate that their offense can consistently produce against quality competition.

That concern connects directly to Saban's assessment of the championship race.

Although Alabama possesses the talent to compete with the nation's best teams, questions remain about its overall consistency.

Saturday provides another opportunity for DeBoer's team to address those concerns.

The comments also arrive during a season in which Saban's legacy has received renewed attention.

ESPN released its six-part documentary series, "Saban," in October, providing an extensive look at his coaching career and personal life.

The series features interviews with former players, coaches and family members, including his wife, Terry Saban.

Additionally, it explores the demanding standards that helped Saban build championship programs at LSU and Alabama.

His comments about the current playoff race reflect many of those same principles.

Rather than focusing exclusively on records or rankings, Saban continues to emphasize complete football teams.

That philosophy helped define his coaching career and remains central to his analysis.

Alabama still has opportunities to strengthen its postseason position, beginning with Saturday's Iron Bowl.

A convincing victory over Auburn would provide another positive result before the final College Football Playoff selections.

However, the Crimson Tide must eventually demonstrate the consistency Saban identified in Georgia, Utah and Notre Dame.

For Alabama fans, the former coach's assessment presents an interesting question during Thanksgiving week.

Does the Crimson Tide belong among the nation's most complete championship contenders, or does Saban see weaknesses that could become costly in December?

The answer will ultimately come on the field.

Alabama and Auburn kick off Saturday at 7:30 p.m. ET on ABC.

#2687. The Nasdaq is in freefall. Here's one stock to look at

I asked Claude after Friday's close what I should do about the tanking stock market. Its advice was surprisingly useful: avoid panic selling and look for opportunities. A 40 VIX, which it touched Friday, might provide those opportunities. Specifically, a higher VIX (higher options premiums) signals fear, but it also creates an opportunity to sell elevated premium in some premium stocks, like Amazon.

Amazon's cloud business is growing at its fastest pace in nearly five years. AWS revenue rose 39.3% YoY in the third quarter, and management reported a $578 billion backlog. Trailing 12-month operating cash flow rose 29% to $168.3 billion. However, infrastructure investment accelerated, resulting in more than $42 billion in negative cash flow. The competing forces of accelerating growth and the cost of supplying it could keep the stock in its range over the next four weeks. Its shares are only off 14% from its recent highs, comparatively better than the Nasdaq Composite's near 19% decline.

AWS demand supports the lower end of the range. The backlog gives Amazon a basis for adding capacity even as investors scrutinize the cash cost. Cash spending could temper an upside rerating.

The next leg higher may require evidence that new capacity can earn attractive returns at this scale, and that evidence may be in short supply over the next couple of months.

Sell the Dec. 18, 2026 AMZN 235/285 strangle for an indicative $5.90 credit per share.

Max reward — $590 per strangle if AMZN finishes between $235 and $285 at expiration, before fees.

Breakevens at expiration — $229.10 and $290.90.

Max risk — Unlimited on the uncovered call, but one should think of the trade as risking getting short at a net price above $290, a nearly 13% premium to today's closing price. The downside loss would reach $22,910 per strangle if AMZN fell to zero. The credit is roughly 2.3% of a $260 reference share price over four weeks, or nearly 30% on a simple annualized basis. That comparison uses the share price as its denominator. Actual return on capital depends on margin and the cost to close or settle the trade.

Buying in bear markets, as the Nasdaq just touched, is never easy. But those opportunities don't present themselves too often. What are you going to do with it?

#2686. Rams shut down Packers in second half for a 26-13 win in NFL's first Thanksgiving Eve game

The Rams and Packers played to a tie in the first 30 minutes of Wednesday night's game in Los Angeles, but the second half was all Rams.

Blake Corum ran for 151 yards and a score in final 30 minutes and the Rams defense shut out the Packers to pave the way to a 26-13 win. The victory is the fifth in a row for the Rams and they are now 7-4 with a Thursday night home game against the Chiefs on deck in Week 13.

The win moves the Rams ahead of the Packers in the race for NFC playoff spots and Thursday's game between the Lions and Bears will have further impact on the chances for all four teams to advance to the postseason.

Corum's touchdown came at the end of the Rams' first possession of the second half and he finished the game with a career-high 32 carries for 184 yards.

That scoring drive ate up more than eight minutes. The Packers had a quick three-and-out, but briefly showed some life when linebacker Ty'Ron Hopper picked off Matthew Stafford to set the offense up in Los Angeles territory. Jordan Love turned the ball right back over when he hung a deep pass that safety Jaylen McCullough snagged out of the air. The Rams would then ride Corum and Kyren Williams on a pair of drives that covered more than 11 minutes.

The Packers' last chance came with 6:13 left and they moved the ball to midfield before Love threw his second interception of the game. Cornerback Emmanuel Forbes got that one and the Packers were out of timeouts well before the two-minute warning.

Sticking with the ground game helped to limit Packers defensive end Micah Parsons's impact in his return from last season's torn ACL. He had a sack late in the first half, but Stafford only threw eight times after the break and the Packers' run defense could not get their offense the ball often enough to get back into the game.

Love was 17-of-29 for 198 yards and a touchdown, but clearly missed wide receiver Christian Watson after he left the game with a concussion. They only picked up two first downs after halftime and they'll hope that an extended break before their Week 13 trip to New Orleans is enough time for him to return to action.

The Rams have several injuries of their own to monitor as guard Bill Murray joined tight ends Davis Allen and Tyler Higbee in making early exits from the game. They'd like to have all those players back next week, but they showed on Wednesday night that Corum can sometimes be enough all by himself.

#2685. Wealthy Europeans are looking beyond the stock market amid the AI sell-off

Wealthy Europeans are looking to alternative asset classes to build exposure to artificial intelligence themes amid turbulence in public markets.

Goldman Sachs Alternatives' inaugural survey of high-net-worth and ultra-high-net-worth investors found 47% of European respondents saw alternatives as the best way to gain exposure to AI and future tech growth, compared to just 24% of U.S. investors.

The survey found a significant gap between the U.S. and Europe in terms of investor knowledge of alternatives, which include assets like private equity, property and infrastructure. Goldman said 82% of European respondents are familiar with the alternative investments market — compared to 50% of U.S. investors.

Goldman polled 1,000 investors with at least 1 million in investable assets in their local currency across the U.K., France, Germany, Italy and Switzerland. The survey was carried out between June 29 and Aug. 18 this year.

The report on the findings, published Wednesday, did not specify the proportion of respondents who already have a stake in some kind of alternative investment — but wealthy Europeans appeared particularly bullish on the space.

Private equity made up the largest proportion of current alternatives holdings. Among wealthy Europeans with alternatives exposure, 62% said they invested in private equity. The second most commonly held asset class was private real estate, which was backed by 51% of European alternatives investors. Hedge funds were held by 37% of European alternatives owners, and private infrastructure was held by 30%.

One in four respondents who did not already own an alternative investment said they were likely to invest in the market in the coming 12 months. Among those already in the space, 26% said they were likely to increase their allocations over the next year, while just 5% said they planned to decrease their exposure.

Of the European investors who already owned alternatives, 54% said their assets had performed as expected, while 44% said their assets had exceeded their expectations to the upside. Only 2% said their alternative investments had performed worse than expected.

"Recent reforms gave individual investors unprecedented access to alternative asset classes like private equity, venture capital, and private credit," Kyle Kniffen, global head of alternatives for third-party wealth at Goldman Sachs Asset Management, said in Wednesday's report.

He added that wealthy investors "look ready to jump at the opportunity."

"Our survey shows satisfaction among current alts owners is high and adoption looks set to increase," Kniffen said.

Alternative investments have increasingly become a staple of high-net-worth individuals' investment portfolios.

Over the past year, JPMorgan Asset Management's private wealth alternative assets in the Asia-Pacific region have jumped 40%, while BNY Wealth says alternative investments now account for 60% of single-family office portfolios — recently surpassing traditional assets for the first time.

But investing in alternatives carries risks. Private equity, for example, can lock up capital for long periods, and although the number of exits ticked slightly higher last year, the value of those deals fell.

Concerns about private credit, meanwhile, have dominated headlines, with JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon warning on Oct. 14, 2025, that "cockroaches" were likely to be lurking in private markets.

— CNBC's Lee Ying Shan contributed to this article.

#2684. Spot-on Sucic! Croatian ace reveals Calhanoglu and Zielinski penalty inspiration after firing Inter past Stuttgart

Petar Sucic converted from the penalty spot for the second consecutive match to spark Inter's 2-0 Champions League victory over VfB Stuttgart at San Siro. Speaking after full-time, the Croatian midfielder praised injured spot-kick specialists Hakan Calhanoglu and Piotr Zielinski while highlighting Inter's improved defending against crosses.

Croatian midfielder Petar Sucic continued his clinical run from 12 yards by converting a decisive penalty to set Inter on course for a 2-0 Champions League victory over VfB Stuttgart on Wednesday night. Having also scored from the spot in Bergamo days earlier, Sucic dictated the midfield tempo at San Siro before Lautaro Martinez added a second goal to seal Inter's third straight European win.

"I managed to score from the penalty spot in two consecutive games, I'm happy to have contributed to the team's victory," Sucic said in his post-match interviews. "The group has a very strong mentality and is always very united, the season is long and we can have problems and injuries."

With regular penalty takers Hakan Calhanoglu and Piotr Zielinski sidelined through injury, Sucic stepped up once again with composure. Asked whether the absent duo had offered him tips from the spot, the midfielder hailed their pedigree while backing his own self-belief.

"If Calhanoglu and Zielinski gave me advice on penalties? They are among the best in the world, I have a lot of confidence in myself and I'm happy because I scored two goals in two games," Sucic explained.

Sucic pointed out that Inter corrected the late defensive mistakes against crosses that cost them in Bergamo, while expressing satisfaction with operating in a deep playmaking role under Cristian Chivu.

"I think we did a lot of good things in Bergamo too and were ahead, but in the end we conceded to their crosses. Stuttgart also tried today, but we were much better at defending in this situation," he noted. "Playmaker? I'm happy, I think I can play in all midfield roles and the coach used me that way too. I'm ready to give my best, I just want to help the team."

While securing an automatic top-eight finish in the Champions League league phase remains the target, Sucic urged Inter to stay grounded ahead of a demanding schedule.

"The top eight? That's the goal, but let's look at the game per game and let's see where we are," he added, with Inter next travelling to face Borussia Dortmund on Wednesday, December 9.

#2683. S&P warns Nvidia credit rating could be cut over 'rising' management and governance risk

Nvidia's credit rating could be cut by one notch over the next 90 days if the company continues to have "deficiencies" in its management and governance framework, S&P Global Ratings said in the wake of the Jensen Huang's ouster and reinstatement as Nvidia CEO.

"News regarding the CEO and questions regarding potential export control violations have exposed vulnerabilities in Nvidia's management and governance framework, especially in regard to its board structure, composition, and effectiveness," S&P said in a Wednesday note.

Nvidia currently holds an AA rating, the third-highest level.

S&P has placed the chipmaker on "CreditWatch with negative implications," though any potential downgrade would be limited to one notch, to AA-, unless investigations or disclosures lead the agency to conclude that Nvidia's reputation or future prospects have also been harmed.

A downgrade can pressure bond prices, push yields higher and raise borrowing costs. Nvidia priced $25 billion of investment-grade senior notes in June, with maturities from 2028 through 2056, shortly after S&P upgraded the company from AA- to AA.

Nvidia has about $32.5 billion of bonds in the U.S. high-grade corporate bond index, according to Bloomberg.

S&P's Wednesday note follows Moody's Ratings changing its outlook on Nvidia to stable from positive on Nov. 18, citing governance concerns, and leaving that outlook unchanged after Huang's return on Monday.

In its note, S&P cited the "long tenure" of certain board members as a reason their independence may be "compromised" and said Nvidia's management and governance risk was "rising."

The ratings agency added that it remained unaware of any remedial action taken by the board to address what could be "meaningful" management and governance deficiencies.

Chairman Harvey Jones and early investor Tench Coxe have served on Nvidia's board since 1993, while Brooke Seawell joined in 1997. Mark Stevens served from 1993 to 2006 and rejoined the board in 2008.

S&P also flagged questions around geopolitical risk management and internal controls arising from Nvidia's exposure to China.

"While Nvidia management has delivered significant returns for its stakeholders over decades, weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning have brought questions around the "key man" risk to the forefront," S&P's Andrew Chang and David Tsui said.

Chang and Tsui noted that Huang's resignation and subsequent reinstatement did not resolve questions about the board's oversight of executive conduct.

The agency also intends to meet with Nvidia's board and management to discuss plans to address those deficiencies, particularly around "board independence, effective management oversight and a credible leadership succession plan."

Huang returned less than two weeks after Jones said Nvidia's directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back, sending Nvidia shares up as much as 36% during Monday's session.

Nvidia shares are down about 12.5% since Monday's close.

#2682. Here is where the Seahawks are in the NFC playoff picture after Wednesday night

The Seattle Seahawks have fallen in the NFC West standings as a result of the Los Angeles Rams' 26-13 Thanksgiving Eve win over the Green Bay Packers, but there is a silver lining here for the reigning Super Bowl champions.

Green Bay has dropped to 6-5 after losing its third game over the last five weeks. Seattle is now (temporarily, at least) ahead of the Packers in the Wild Card chase. While the Seahawks are behind the Rams by a half-game, they currently own the division record tiebreaker on their fierce rivals. This means that the Seahawks control their Wild Card fate, starting with Week 12 against the San Francisco 49ers.

12. Tampa Bay Buccaneers (3-7, 1-0 vs ATL+NO)

14. New Orleans Saints (3-7, 0-1 vs TB+ATL, 1-5 NFC)

If the Seahawks defeat the 49ers, Seattle is the 7-seed heading into Week 13. A loss? They drop to No. 9 and behind the Packers on the conference record tiebreaker. It's not a dagger to their postseason hopes, but their margin for error would be about as thin as dental floss.

Seattle has a huge opportunity to get back into a playoff spot on Sunday and stay somewhat attached to the 49ers in the NFC West title race.

#2681. SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts

SpaceX said Wednesday it will donate one share of the company's class A stock to every active Trump Account, and CEO Elon Musk would personally match the gift.

"SpaceX is building the infrastructure of the future and working to make life multiplanetary," the company said in a statement. "We believe ownership of the company creating these systems should extend to the next generation of Americans for their long-term benefit."

The White House announced in October that nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created, including more than 60 million through auto-enrollment. It's unclear how many are considered active.

Musk said in a social media post on X on Thursday that the share from SpaceX would be transferred next month, without providing further details. "The share directly from me will be transferred when my lockup expires in June next year," he added.

Eligible children must have their account claimed to enable contributions from family members, friends and employers, and to receive the government's one-time $1,000 seed contribution. Auto-enrolled children can receive philanthropic contributions without a parent or guardian claiming an account, according to TrumpAccounts.gov.

Representatives for SpaceX and the U.S. Department of the Treasury did not immediately respond to a request for further details about the planned gift.

SpaceX Chief Operating Officer Gwynne Shotwell had previously agreed to make a personal gift of over 2 million SpaceX shares to Trump Accounts, with eligible account holders expected to receive them in December.

In October, Elon Musk posted on X that he would give a share of SpaceX to every kid in America next year.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Since the July 4 launch, families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts. Total deposits now sit at more than $9 billion, CNBC first reported.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move officials have said is meant to encourage more philanthropic gifts.

Contributing stock can benefit donors, who are able to avoid capital gains taxes triggered by selling appreciated stock. But families can face restrictions related to selling that donated stock. The policy also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

Under U.S. Department of the Treasury guidelines, families cannot refuse stock gifted to Trump Accounts and generally cannot sell it until the earlier of five years or the end of the calendar year in which the beneficiary turns 17.

SpaceX shares have been under some pricing pressure as the company negotiates a possible merger with Elon Musk's automaker Tesla. The companies have established two special committees to handle merger negotiations, and hired advisers for a potential mega-deal.

CNBC's Sam Meredith, Kevin Breuninger, Kate Dore and Jess Dickler contributed to this story

#2679. What to know: Cricket's Sandpapergate wounds reopen as teammates exchange blame

BRISBANE, Australia (AP) — Cricket is imploding again 8 1/2 years after the cheating scandal dubbed "Sandpapergate."

Longtime Australia teammates are calling each other out over their conflicting versions of what happened. There have been accusations of scapegoating, of lies and cover-ups. Pot shots are coming from outside and within the inner-sanctum.

Three Australian players received unprecedented bans over a plot to illegally tamper with the ball during a test match — the traditional, five-day version of cricket — in March 2018 against South Africa in what was already an acrimonious series.

An autobiography published by retired batter Usman Khawaja in early November alleged that everyone in the Australia squad and staff knew of the sandpaper plan but let captain Steve Smith, vice-captain David Warner and Cameron Bancroft — who was literally caught red-handed — take the fall. Smith and Warner accepted 12-month bans from Cricket Australia and returned in 2019. Bancroft was suspended for nine months, and never re-established his spot in the Australian team.

Warner and Bancroft, among others, supported Khawaja's claim. Pat Cummins, the incumbent Australia captain who was among the bowlers in that match, described Khawaja's claim as "complete and utter nonsense." His three longtime partners in Australia's so-called bowling "cartel " have also distanced themselves from the plot.

The initial Cricket Australia investigation, conducted within a matter of days, leveled the blame on three members of the team. Questions are being raised now about why all players, coaches and support staff weren't interviewed as part of the investigation.

Some critics have torched Khawaja's decision to reopen the biggest scandal in decades in Australian cricket and questioned his motives. Backers have praised Khawaja's courage for speaking out and acknowledging the win-at-all-costs mentality that typified the team of that era, even if he went public only after retiring. Khawaja said the Australians should have accountability as a team.

Intense media coverage has renewed focus on Warner's central role in the sandpaper plot. Bancroft, for the first time publicly, stated it was Warner's idea.

Just like their counterparts in baseball, cricket players are capable of devising devious ways of getting extra movement on a thrown ball. It's sometimes known as "ball management." What made Sandpapergate such a scandal was the sheer brazenness of it.

Tampering usually involves either making one half of the ball rough or unpicking the stitches in the seam. Teeth, fingernails, bottle caps, zippers, dirt and grit have been used to gouge the ball. Wet candy, mints, gum and glue have been used on the cricket ball. Saliva was typically used to shine one side of the ball — to make it swing through the air — until that was banned in 2020 during the health crisis of the COVID-19 era.

Some famous players — it's a long list but here are a few names, all former captains: Mike Atherton of England, Sachin Tendulkar of India, Faf du Plessis of South Africa — have been accused of or sanctioned by the International Cricket Council for ball tampering. Their punishments amounted to fines or comparatively minor suspensions.

The abrasive sheets of paper have always been used to rub the face and edges of cricket bats, which are made of willow. Cummins says there's still sandpaper in the locker rooms to use on bats. He said it was also used in practice sessions to scuff the outside surface of the leather balls so that batters could prepare to face deliveries that swing through the air unconventionally.

But using it on the field, during the game, that's extraordinary. And that's what got the Australian team into this prolonged mess.

Some wonder why this case was treated so harshly. Cricket Australia administrators recognized the sport's long-established status in the national consciousness, and realized the Sandpapergate scandal brought the game into disrepute and shattered the team's standing in the community.

Michael Kasprowicz, a former test fast bowler and Cricket Australia director, told Australian Associated Press that the hard line taken was probably more about perception than tampering.

"I know from a board point of view at the time, it wasn't about damaging the ball. It was about damaging the brand," he said.

Bancroft was the initial face in the scandal, after South African television broadcast footage of him with the sandpaper on the field.

He published a roughly 4,500-word account of his recollections after Khawaja, Warner and Cummins made public statements.

"It was not just the three of us ... It was the whole team," Bancroft said. He felt "freed" after finally telling his story, he said, adding that the "final straw" was a news conference staged by Cummins that "made me feel angry, betrayed and alone."

Warner responded to the initial fallout from Khawaja's book by supporting his longtime friend and saying Cricket Australia's investigation into the scandal was "rushed and botched. "

But Cummins, in a 70-minute stand-up news conference, accused Khawaja of being "casual" and "flippant."

"When he says we all knew, if by that he means that we knew sandpaper was going out in the field, he's 100% wrong," Cummins said.

In a clearout after the 2018 series, head coach Darren Lehmann, who has denied any knowledge of the plot, was replaced by Justin Langer, who was succeeded by Andrew McDonald in 2022. Veteran wicketkeeper Tim Paine replaced Smith as test captain before Cummins took over in 2021.

Cricket Australia has a new CEO, Todd Greenberg, who has ruled out reopening the Sandpapergate investigation but plans to establish an independent forum for the players. He said the sport's administrators aren't taking any side of the debate.

Former Australia captain Greg Chappell, who was embroiled in the so-called "Underarm" controversy in the 1980s, wants the "bickering" to stop.

"What worries me is that it's dividing people who love the same game," he wrote in a column for cricinfo.com, "and I fear it will open chasms inside a team that has to take the field together."

#2677. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2675. JJ Redick Calls Out ESPN’s Overreactions To Lakers: ‘We’re Either Contenders Or A Fake Team’

Ahead of the Los Angeles Lakers' upcoming fixture against the San Antonio Spurs, their head coach JJ Redick spoke to the media at the post-practice scrum at the training facility, where he indirectly addressed Stephen A. Smith and Ramona Shelburne's recent criticism of the Lakers.

When a reporter asked him about his thoughts ahead of facing a top team in the Western Conference, Redick pointed out that their plans are not just focused on facing one team on this road trip but on several top teams in the West.

"This entire road trip is a massive challenge for us. Four games, and all of them are going to present different tests. We're playing against the Western Conference Finals champion from last year, who are off to a great start," he said.

"Then you get Memphis, Houston, and New Orleans. So, there's a lot on this trip. It's not just one game. I think these four games will be a great barometer of where we're at."

"But to your point, whether it's guys being in and out of the lineup, changes in playing style, tweaks we make, or rotation changes, things are always evolving."

"Whether we lose a game or win a game, we're either going to be contenders on [ESPN's] First Take, or we're not going to be contenders, and we're fake. The overreactions game-to-game are what they are. That stuff is just noise. We're going to focus on the process of trying to get better. It's a good test to see where we're at right now," Redick concluded on the subject.

Stephen A. Smith, who is the executive producer of ESPN's First Take and also appears often on the show, has recently claimed that the Lakers do not have the right foundational core to compete for a championship.

In simple terms, he believes that since no team has won a championship with a core of white players in the modern NBA, the Lakers do not have NBA history on their side to compete for a ring this season without adding more Black players to their roster.

Moreover, Ramona Shelburne also appeared on ESPN and claimed that the Lakers' recent form was not a reliable sample size to test their performance since she believed they had one of the easiest schedules in the NBA until that point.

While shutting down all the media narratives as 'noise,' JJ Redick has emphasized the fragile and extreme nature of such media narratives that swing very easily, especially considering the subject is the Lakers, one of the most storied franchises in NBA history.

He did not directly name Stephen A. Smith or Ramona Shelburne because it seems he did not want to engage with him directly over this. But it is clear that the Lakers are locked in on letting their performance on the court do the talking.

At 15-3, the Lakers are second in the Western Conference and riding a two-game winning streak. However, their upcoming four-game road trip against the Spurs, Grizzlies, Rockets, and Pelicans will be a significant test.

If they navigate this stretch successfully, they can strengthen their championship credentials and silence critics. And with Austin Reaves expected to be back in the next game, it has certainly boosted their chances of doing so.

But it remains to be seen if JJ Redick and the Lakers can prove the media critics wrong.

#2674. This is where the Rams sit in the NFC playoff picture after their win on Wednesday

The Los Angeles Rams won on Wednesday night to move to 7-4, and they are now officially in a playoff spot for the first time this season after five straight wins. They came into Week 12 tied with the Seattle Seahawks and the Green Bay Packers, but the previous tiebreakers favored Seattle so L.A. was on the outside losing in. The win over the Packers knocked Green Bay down and L.A. is a half-game ahead of Seattle — for now.

The Seahawks play the San Francisco 49ers on Sunday. If Seattle wins, they would move back ahead of the Rams based on their superior division record. The Minnesota Vikings at 7-3 are just a half-game in front of the Rams at this point, too. If they win, they stay ahead of L.A., as well.

If either Seattle or Minnesota lose, the Rams hold onto their playoff spot. The Seahawks would fall to 6-5 and a game behind the Rams. A Vikings loss would drop them to 7-4 in a tie with the Rams but also give them their third NFC loss, meaning the Rams would be ahead of them in the conference record tiebreaker. If both outcomes happen, the Rams would jump from ninth in Week 11 all the way to sixth after Week 12.

#2672. Kantserov Keeps Rolling, Blackhawks Beat Kraken For Perfect Road Trip

Rookie Roman Kantserov extended his goal streak to three games with another two tallies, as the Chicago Blackhawks (9-14-2) defeated the Seattle Kraken (11-10-1) 3-1 at Climate Pledge Arena on Wednesday.

Kantserov now leads the Blackhawks and all NHL rookies with 15 goals and 20 points this season, scoring six times and recording eight points during the team's perfect 3-0 Northwestern road trip. The Russian winger is also sixth in the NHL in goals, ahead of notables like Connor McDavid and Auston Matthews.

After Cole Smith opened the scoring early in the third period, Kantserov tallied the eventual game-winner less than two minutes later, firing a one-timer from a sharp angle into Seattle's net on the power play. The Blackhawks have now scored on the power play in seven straight games and have converted on 11 of 20 attempts during that stretch.

Kantserov added his second goal with an empty-netter to seal the win in the final seconds.

Patrick Kane extended his point streak to six games with an assist on Kantserov's power-play strike, but unfortunately, his night ended not too long after.

With the extra attacker on the ice for the Kraken, Kane blocked a shot in the defensive zone and dropped to the ice, clearly in pain and unable to put much pressure on his right skate. He immediately went to the locker room and did not return for the final 3:01.

Seattle snapped Spencer Knight's shutout bid with a late goal from Jordan Eberle, but otherwise, the 25-year-old was flawless on Wednesday, turning aside 29 of 30 shots. It was arguably Knight's best performance of the season. He was credited with 1.98 goals saved above expected.

Knight outdueled Kraken starter Joey Daccord, who suffered just his second loss in eight career games against the Blackhawks. Daccord stopped all 11 shots faced in the first two periods, but got beat twice in the third. He allowed two goals on 19 shots across 60 minutes.

When the Blackhawks boarded the plane for Calgary last week to begin their road trip, they hadn't won away from the United Center in their first 22 games this season. They'll now return to Chicago with three road wins in their pocket after sweeping the bottom of the Pacific Division.

Here are highlights from the Blackhawks 3-1 win in Seattle, moving to 5-1-1 in their last seven games and 9-14-2 in the 2026-27 regular season.

Knight was up to an early task, denying Mackie Samoskevich on a penalty shot just 1:02 into the game. Ian Cole and Artyom Levshunov collided at their own blue line, and Samoskevich took advantage of the open ice for a breakaway. Kantserov hooked Samoskevich on the backcheck, but no harm, no foul. #2670. Landeskog Calls Out Canadiens’ Late-Game Antics After Avalanche Blowout

Gabe Landeskog had no issue with the Montreal Canadiens wanting to defend their teammate. He just didn't like how far they went to do it.

"I've been on the other side of that. I've done kind of similar things that they've done," Landeskog said after the Avalanche's 6-0 win over the Canadiens on Wednesday at Ball Arena. "Try to get a response from somebody, and when it's not there, you can't really start cross-checking people, start slew-footing people."

Landeskog was referring to the final stages of a lopsided game that turned increasingly chippy after Kaiden Guhle left with an injury following a hit from the Avalanche captain. The Canadiens spent much of the remainder of the night looking for retribution.

"Well, listen, it was a battle. We had two battles all night," Landeskog said of his run-ins with Guhle. "First one he won. He knocked me on my ass. Almost got a high ankle sprain. And I got him back the second time. I wasn't trying to hurt him. I hope he's okay. He knew I was coming. Just kind of finished my check. So the whole time I saw that between me and him, hope he's okay, and that's that."

Kaiden Guhle left the game Wednesday night due to this hit from Gabriel Landeskog#NHL pic.twitter.com/cYzV4TxNFs

The Canadiens' two toughest players, the Xhekaj brothers, were the ones attempting to escalate things.

Florian Xhekaj tried to fight Landeskog late in the second period, but the Avalanche captain didn't take the bait. Instead, he drew a penalty, and Colorado scored on the ensuing power play.

Arber Xhekaj was involved in plenty of the late-game chaos, too. With four minutes remaining, he hit Brent Burns from behind and drew a crowd. Nazem Kadri and Martin Necas were the first players in.

Meanwhile, Florian was near the Avalanche bench and slashed Landeskog while he was sitting on the bench. Both brothers could face further scrutiny from the league before their next game.

"The Xhekaj brothers wanted to get involved, and we'll see what's going to happen with that," Landeskog said. "But I think for one of the most storied franchises and one of the most respected franchises in all of hockey, and then you got two guys doing that. I think at the end of the day, the way I see it, there's a battle between me and [Guhle] and then those guys want to try to get involved, and I get it, I do. But to a certain degree."

On the scoreboard, the Avalanche absolutely dominated Montreal. Mackenzie Blackwood made 27 saves to earn his second shutout, while Devon Toews scored twice. Logan O'Connor, Artturi Lehkonen, Necas, and Landeskog also found the back of the net.

Cale Makar added three assists in his return to the lineup. Brock Nelson and T.J. Hughes each had two assists, while Lehkonen and Necas also finished with multiple points.

It was one of Colorado's most complete performances of the season. Those types of efforts have become more common in recent weeks, even with superstar center Nathan MacKinnon out of the lineup.

But the Avalanche's response to Montreal's physicality might have been the most telling part of the night.

Following the late penalty on Arber for his hit on Burns, head coach Jared Bednar sent his top power-play unit over the boards. Colorado already had a 5-0 lead but wasn't interested in taking its foot off the gas. Landeskog, fittingly, scored the game's final goal. That decision was not an accident.

"I want to be respectful. I do. If it was a hooking penalty or tripping penalty, I would have given, like Parker Kelly and OC a crack and put different guys that don't play the power play," Bednar said. "But if part of their thing was we're gonna take some liberties on your guys, that's our response. If they're gonna take liberties on our guys, and guys are borderline getting hurt, then we're gonna put our unit out and keep doing some work."

#2669. Steelers could try to not pay Aaron Rodgers the balance of his $22 million salary

If/when the Steelers release quarterback Aaron Rodgers, they’ll have to decide whether to willingly pay the balance of his $22 million base salary, or whether to fight.

With his Week 12 paycheck already vested, the question becomes Week 13 through Week 18. Six weeks, totaling $7.33 million.

It won’t be easy to cut him off. First, the Steelers would have to release him for reasons unrelated to skill, injury, or salary cap. The argument, if the Steelers make it, would be that Rodgers’s appearance on Pat McAfee’s show constitutes personal conduct detrimental to the interests of the team.

A grievance would undoubtedly be filed, if the Steelers pursue that approach.

But even without the guarantee, Rodgers’s presence on the Week 1 roster qualifies him for the balance of his base salary as “termination pay” under the Collective Bargaining Agreement. To circumvent that, the Steelers would have to show that Rodgers has become ineligible for termination pay.

Here’s the key language, from Article 30, Section 3(b) of the CBA: “An otherwise qualified player will not be entitled to Termination Pay if (i) the Club terminated the player’s contract for Conduct Detrimental to the Club, and (ii) the Non-Injury Grievance Arbitrator determines that the player’s conduct was so severe that the maximum discipline for Conduct Detrimental of a fine equal to one week’s salary and suspension without pay for four weeks, pursuant to Article 42, Section 1(b)(xvii) of this Agreement (regardless of whether the Club imposed any such discipline prior to terminating the player’s contract), constitutes an inadequate remedy for the player’s conduct.”

It won’t be easy to satisfy that standard. But there’s language in the CBA that potentially applies to Rodgers’s public comments from Wednesday.

Here’s Article 6, titled “Public Statements": “The NFLPA and the Management Council agree that each will use reasonable efforts to curtail public comments by Club personnel or players which express criticism of any club, its coach, or its operation and policy, or which tend to cast discredit upon a Club, a player, or any other person involved in the operation of a Club, the NFL, the Management Council, or the NFLPA.”

That’s the same clause that fueled the NFL’s attack against the NFL Players Association’s report cards. The process constitutes comments from the NFLPA that express criticism of the teams, their coaches, the operations, and their policies.

None of this means the Steelers will attempt to cut off his final $7.33 million. But it’s an option. And it’s something to be considered as the Steelers try to figure out the next move.

#2668. Nationals adding towering LHP to bullpen on 1-year, $5 million contract

Nationals adding towering LHP to bullpen on 1-year, $5 million contract appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Washington Nationals have reached an agreement with left-handed reliever AJ Puk on a one-year, $5 million contract, MLB.com's Mark Feinsand reported on Wednesday night. The team has not yet confirmed the deal.

The 6-foot-7 southpaw is coming off an injury-plagued period and will join the Nationals looking to return to his previous form. He missed the entire 2026 regular season after experiencing multiple setbacks during his recovery from an internal brace procedure performed in June 2025. His most recent major league appearance came on April 17, 2025.

The 31-year-old initially suffered a flexor strain midway through the 2025 campaign, which resulted in a stint on the 60-day injured list. He eventually underwent the internal brace procedure, ruling him out for the remainder of that season. While attempting to work his way back in 2026, he experienced left shoulder discomfort during a Triple-A rehabilitation assignment. He was later diagnosed with a capsule strain, ending his hopes of returning that year.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

Despite his recent availability issues, Puk has demonstrated considerable effectiveness when healthy. Between 2022 and 2024, he recorded a 3.38 ERA across 194 1/3 innings while striking out 242 batters. His 31.1% strikeout rate during that stretch ranked tied for 19th among 185 qualified relievers.

His 2024 campaign was particularly productive. Splitting the season between the Miami Marlins and Arizona Diamondbacks, Puk finished with a 3.15 ERA and collected 88 strikeouts over 71 1/3 innings. He also produced a career-high 1.7 WAR, according to FanGraphs. His slider generated swings and misses on 47.8% of opposing hitters' attempts.

Selected sixth overall by the Athletics in the 2016 MLB Draft out of the University of Florida, Puk has also dealt with injuries throughout his professional career, including Tommy John surgery in 2018.

Washington is also bringing back left-handed pitcher Tom Cosgrove on a minor league contract worth $950,000, with an additional $150,000 bonus if he reaches the majors. Cosgrove appeared in 18 games for the Nationals, compiling a 7.71 ERA, a 1.5 WHIP, three home runs allowed and 14 strikeouts across 16 1/3 innings.

#2667. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

#2665. LeBron James records first triple-double with 76ers: Where he ranks on all-time list

In case you forgot, LeBron James is great.

The NBA's all-time leading scorer notched his first triple-double with the Philadelphia 76ers Wednesday, Nov. 25 in an NBA Cup matchup against the Miami Heat.

He completed the feat with 1:43 left in the fourth quarter, when he scooped his 10th rebound. With Joel Embiid sidelined to start the game and Jaylen Brown getting banged up just seconds before the end of the first half, James took over. He erupted in the second quarter, burying 3-pointer after 3-pointer. During one stretch in the second period, he scored 14 consecutive points for the 76ers.

"It's not my job to dominate every night for this ball club," James told reporters after the game. "We have a Finals MVP (Jaylen Brown), a regular season MVP (Joel Embiid), an up-and-coming superstar point guard (Tyrese Maxey), a young fella in VJ (Edgecombe), who is up and coming as well. My dues is paid in what I've done, but if they need me to dust off the cape from time to time, I can still accomplish that. But it's not my job. I don't think it's my job, but if they need it, I can do it."

James finished the game with 25 points on 9-of-15 shooting, adding 12 assists and those 10 rebounds. His point and assist totals were both season highs.

This is the 126th triple-double in James' career, which has now reached an NBA-record 24 seasons. Russell Westbrook holds the record for most triple-doubles in NBA history with 209. James ranks fifth behind Westbrook, Nikola Jokic, Oscar Robertson and Magic Johnson. No other player in NBA history had recorded a triple-double after turning 41 years old; James has now done it four times.

In fact, James now has more triple-doubles than Michael Jordan, Kobe Bryant and Larry Bird — combined.

James was ranked the 19th-best player in the league in USA TODAY's preseason rankings of the top 100 players.

The four-time MVP and NBA champion joined the 76ers this offseason as a free agent after leaving the Los Angeles Lakers.

"We all know, and everybody always says it, but Father Time wins," Heat coach Erik Spoelstra said. "But right now, he's giving Father Time hell."

#2664. Players Era Festival game thread: St. John’s men’s basketball vs. Louisville

When the Players Era bracket was revealed this summer, college basketball experts and fans immediately circled one potential matchup as a can't-miss game: St. John's versus Louisville in the event's second round. They got their wish when each team won their first games, and it will be the most-anticipated contest on a loaded Thanksgiving college basketball slate.

Not only is this matchup a battle between two ranked opponents hoping to reach the Final Four, but it is also chockful of storylines and threads linking these two old Big East leaguemates. Of course, it is headlined by Red Storm head coach Rick Pitino facing his old team for the first time since the Cardinals fired him in 2017 after a federal investigation into the program. Almost a decade after the widely publicized split, Pitino and Louisville appear to have mended bridges, and the St. John's head coach is treating Thursday's game like any other. At least, that's what his players see.

A couple of Red Storm assistants will also be reuniting with their former program. Steve Masiello was an assistant at Louisville for six seasons from 2005-11, where he helped coach eventual Cardinals icon Peyton Siva, the all-conference starting point guard for Pitino's 2013 National Championship squad.

Former North Carolina teammates Ian Jackson and Seth Trimble will face off for the first time in their college careers. Jackson and Trimble played together for only one season in 2024-25, finishing as the second- and third-highest scorers for the Tar Heels. Trimble is spending his fifth year with the Cardinals and already leads the team with 16.0 points per game.

There will also be heavy hearts on both sidelines. Each program continues to honor legendary sports information director Kenny Klein, who passed away suddenly this summer at the age of 66. Klein spent four decades leading Louisville's sports information department, then came out of retirement in 2025 to work alongside his longtime friend in Pitino at St. John's.

St. John's is honoring Klein by leaving an empty chair with the initials "K.K." open on their bench at every game this season. Pitino is also planning to return to Louisville on January 2 when the team is set to unveil a banner honoring Klein's memory.

Welcome to the thread for Thursday's matchup against Louisville in the second round of the Players Era 16. Please be respectful in the comment section and do not share any illegal streams of the game.

The Red Storm will play either Tennessee or Iowa State on Friday night, and if they win their next two, they will advance to the Players Era's championship game on Saturday night.
