#2662. LeBron triple-double, Maxey 30-piece lead Sixers over Heat

The Philadelphia 76ers got back in the win column on Wednesday evening, taking down the visiting Miami Heat 111-104 in NBA Cup group play.

Joel Embiid was ruled out for this one after having been doubtful on the injury report since yesterday because of illness. This was the second game in a row Embiid missed due to being sick.

The Sixers were led by tremendous nights from LeBron James and Tyrese Maxey. James, who notched his first triple-double as a Sixer, put up 25 points on 9-for-15 field goal and 4-for-6 three-point shooting with 12 assists, 10 rebounds and a steal. Maxey added 30 points shooting 12-for-21 from the floor. VJ Edgecombe came alive in the second half to chip in 18 points.

The bad news is that Jaylen Brown exited the game just before halftime after seemingly favoring and grabbing at his hamstring. He was quickly ruled out for the rest of the contest with hamstring tightness. Something to be aware of going forward, for sure.

The good news is that the Sixers are now 15-4 and have off tomorrow. They're back in action Friday.

It didn't take long to see how Miami benefits from being third in the association in pace. In the first three minutes, the Heat got out ahead in transition three separate times for easy buckets before the Sixers defense even made it back up the other end. The Sixers aren't that slow, but the Heat were running circles around them early.

Jabari Walker got his third start tonight in Joel Embiid's absence, and it went right to work doing the little things right. He buried a second-chance triple off a LeBron James offensive rebound then got down the defensive end to draw an offensive foul out of Giannis. Jabari had seven points on 3-for-3 field goal shooting in his first six minutes on the floor.

If you read any of the Bell Ringers I usually write, you know I cannot stop talking about LeBron's passing. Well, I'm here to talk about it again. It's not just one pass. It's every single pass he makes. He waits until the exact millisecond to release perfectly targeted passes that set up the Sixers for sometimes wide open buckets.

Tyrese Maxey led the way for the Sixers' scoring in the first quarter with 13 points on 5-for-8 field goal, 1-for-2 three-point shooting. The consistent contribution of points helped steady the Sixers' offense after starting off the game a bit disjointed. Philly trailed Miami by three points after one.

LeBron has this tendency to just absolutely lock in offensively in insane bursts. He goes from the main facilitator to a mode of "hold on y'all, I got this myself" and, in year 24, he's absolutely still got it. James had the ball in his hands for three straight Sixers' possessions and turned each into three points — one and-1, and two threes. He wasn't done. A minute later, he pulled up again from not far over midcourt for the ultimate heat check. Three more points. It ended up being a 14-3 Sixers run that gave them the lead, courtesy of LeBron James, single-handed.

It's been a very quiet night so far from Jaylen Brown. With just four minutes left in the half, Brown has two points on 1-for-6 field goal shooting with not much else to show for himself on the stat sheet. He hasn't made a ton of mistakes, necessarily, but more so has been relatively inconsequential so far and can't seem to get a shot to fall. A far cry from last game, his return from a hamstring strain, where he looked as solid as ever.

Just a quick note (and, yes, I admit in saying this I am incredibly biased)… Giannis gets a VERY friendly whistle. I get it, he's a superstar. But compare the amount of contact he takes that is getting called a foul versus the amount of contact Tyrese Maxey takes with no whistles.

The Sixers — especially considering the absence of Joel Embiid — are doing a great job so far containing Bam Adebayo. At halftime, Adebayo had just five points on 2-for-8 field goal shooting

The Sixers ended the half on a 7-0 run to help them to a 62-53 lead going into the break. That's the good news. That bad news is that Brown left the half a few seconds early after seemingly favoring his left leg and grabbing at his hamstring. He went straight back to the locker room.

The team was quick to rule out Brown for the rest of the game due to hamstring tightness. Dean Wade started the second half in his spot.

Philadelphia has had a tendency to come out of halftime looking a bit sluggish or out of sorts… not tonight. The Sixers ripped off a quick 7-0 run just a minute or so into the second half to push their lead to double-digits. It was the usual suspects, James and Maxey, chipping in with the addition of VJ Edgecombe getting involved.

Edgecombe had a relatively quiet first half, but he seemed energized to come out for the second. The sophomore assisted the Sixers' first two buckets of the third frame and then scored the next two himself. He added a triple and a steal a few minutes later. That's one thing that Edgecombe seems to possess beyond his years: patience. It doesn't matter how few opportunities he gets early on in a game, he never gets discouraged and is always ready whenever he is called upon.

It's a different game entirely when the Sixers are actually hitting their three-pointers. With just a few minutes left in the third, six Sixers have sank at least one three-pointer and three players have multiple. They're shooting 45.8% from long range and it certainly makes holding on to a narrow lead just a bit easier.

It's been very nice to have Dominick Barlow back. In just his third game of the season after making his debut this past Saturday, Barlow continued putting in some really serviceable minutes at the backup center position featuring solid rim protection, grabbing offensive boards, putting back buckets and setting some big screens. He and Walker have been a decent one-two punch at the center position without Embiid available tonight.

The Sixers brought a nine-point lead into the final frame. Their lead was quickly extended back into the double-digits with two back-to-back triples from Anfernee Simons — his first two field goals of the night, making him the seventh Sixer to knock down a three-pointer in this one.

Apologies for the redundancy but the Lebron passes just get more and more ridiculous. It is just beyond anything I've ever seen done so well night in and night out, every single game. Here's an absurdly accurate lob to Edgecombe from half court to extend the Sixers lead to 14.

Edgecombe has absolutely come alive since halftime. From all the statistical contributions to just the extra effort plays that won't show on the sheet, he has his handprints all over this second half and was a big factor in the Sixers holding on to this lead.

The Heat aren't just going away quietly tonight. They've chipped the Sixers lead back down to just single digits with five minutes to play. This Philly team just always has to keep games interesting…

What a night from LeBron James! With his 10th rebound with under two minutes to play, James notched his first triple-double as a Philadelphia 76er.

The Sixers hold on and get back in the win column. They move to 15-4 on the season and will be back in action on Friday night.

#2661. Packers-Rams Final Score: Blake Corum takes control in 26-13 Rams win

The Green Bay Packers' playoff hopes are officially on life support. The team needed a win over the Los Angeles Rams on Wednesday of Thanksgiving week to stay in the NFC's 7th and final playoff position with six games to go in the 2026 regular season. Instead, another offensive collapse in the second half doomed the team, as Jordan Love and company ran just eight plays, held the football for barely three minutes, and picked up no first downs between the start of the second half and the midpoint of the fourth quarter.

Over that time, the Rams took a lead and extended it out to ten points, which was too much for Green Bay to overcome. Blake Corum ran the football more than 20 times in the second half as the Green Bay defense wore down, and the Rams running back finished with over 180 yards on the ground and scored the go-ahead touchdown early in the third quarter to help the Rams coast to a 26-13 win.

Micah Parsons' return for the Packers saw him record a sack, but it was spoiled by his teammates' inability to stop the run and the Packers' offensive miscommunications. Jordan Love threw two interceptions, both of which appeared to be partially due to receivers running the wrong routes. Tucker Kraft committed an early fumble as well, giving the Packers three turnovers for the second straight game. And all told, the Packers had fewer than 20 minutes of possession in the game and ran just 44 plays on offense.

The unit certainly suffered after Christian Watson left the game with an apparent concussion, while right guard Kevin Zeitler injured his ankle in his first start as a Packer. But the Packers' issues were well beyond any injury issues, and they once again point to more fundamental problems with this team as a whole. With the Rams getting the win — going up by a game in the standings and earning a big tiebreaker with the victory — the Packers' likelihood of making the postseason is now awfully slim.

The Packers went 3-and-out on the game's opening drive, and the Rams immediately took advantage of a bad call, with Brandon Cisse getting flagged for a long pass interference penalty. That put them in field goal range quickly, but Xavier McKinney snuffed out a swing pass on 3rd down to force a 3-point attempt from former Packer Brandon McManus, who hit from 43 yards out to give the Rams an early lead.

Despite a running game that lost six carries on its first two carries, the Packers offense got moving on their second drive. Jordan Love hit Kaleb Johnson for 19 yards out of the backfield for the initial first down of the game, but after he found Tucker Kraft for another first down, the tight end coughed up the football as Nate Landman forced a fumble for the game's first turnover. Another inexplicable penalty on the ensuing drive helped the Rams drive deep into Packers territory, as Devonte Wyatt was called for an absurd roughing the passer penalty. Micah Parsons made his presence known for the first time with a pressure on Matthew Stafford, however, helping to limit the Rams to another field goal.

After another run for negative yardage by Johnson, Love hit Watson for a big 25-yard gain on a crossing route over the middle to get the Packers quickly across midfield. Trey Smack cut the Rams' lead to 6-3 a few plays later, hitting a bomb from 54 yards out to get Green Bay on the board.

The Rams responded with a touchdown drive, picking apart the Packers' secondary with short and intermediate. Stafford went 5 of 6 for 60 yards on the drive, with his longest play going to Konata Mumpfield for 24 yards, but it was Kyren Williams who finished it off, finding the end zone from seven yards out for a score. That gave them a 13-3 lead early on in the second quarter.

Love came up with back-to-back explosive plays on the first two snaps of the next drive, however, as the Packers answered. An end-around flea flicker from Savion Williams back to Love went for a gain of 21 to Watson, then Love hit Matthew Golden for a catch-and-run of 39 yards. A Chris Brooks run of 4 yards and a Love scramble for 7 set up goal to go and two plays later from the 7, Love drifted back and found Watson crossing left to right for a touchdown. Watson's 8th score of the season brought Green Bay back to within three points.

After forcing the Rams to punt for the first time all game, the Packers drove into field goal range once again. They did so thanks to Savion Williams picking up a 3rd-and-short on a dive when lined up as a tailback and Jordan Love finding MarShawn Lloyd for a nice gain out of the backfield for another first down. Matt LaFleur kept the offense on the field for a 4th-and-1 at the 30-yard line, and it proved to be a costly decision.

Love lofted a good pass to Watson up the right sideline for a gain of 15 yards, but Kamren Kinchens was ejected for a hit to Watson's head. Watson walked off on his own power, but the penalty and the catch were erased, offset by an offensive pass interference penalty on Matthew Golden. Watson walked to the locker room under his own power, but he would not return to the game.

Instead of sending the offense out again, Trey Smack came on for a 48-yard field goal attempt and he hit it, tying the game with just under two minutes left in the first half. Micah Parsons then got his first sack of the season on first down of the Rams' next drive, scuttling any real chance for Los Angeles to score before the half as the game went into the break tied at 13.

Coming out of the break, the Rams got cruising on the ground. Blake Corum picked up chunks of yardage as Stafford found Mumpfield for a couple of 3rd down conversions to get inside the Packers' 10. On 3rd-and-2, Mumpfield appeared to have a touchdown, but he was ruled to have bobbled the ball and the play was ruled incomplete. That appeared to set up a 4th down attempt, but instead the Packers were called for defensive holding on the play anyway, giving the Rams a 1st-and-goal from the one. After Corum got stopped on his first rushing attempt, he barely snuck over the goal line on second down to give the Rams a seven-point lead.

The Packers' offense got bailed out on the next sequence with the game hanging in the balance, but then gave the ball right back. After going three-and-out following the Rams' 8-minute drive, Los Angeles looked to put together a long drive again but Ty'Ron Hopper dropped into zone coverage and intercepted a Stafford pass, setting up Love and company in a great spot. However, he and Matthew Golden were not on the same page; Golden appeared to run the wrong route and Love's deep pass to the middle of the field got picked off near the goal line to return the favor.

Corum continued to run the ball effectively into the early fourth quarter, even as they saw a pair of starting offensive linemen and tight end Tyler Higbee leave the game with injuries. Finally, however, the Packers managed to get a stop with the Rams at the 40-yard line, leading to a surprising punt rather than a long field goal attempt.

The Packers still could not move the chains, however, failing to pick up a first down for the third time in three second half drives. Tucker Kraft came up a yard and a half short on 3rd-and-9, and Matt LaFleur called for a punt from deep in Rams territory. Corum gave the Packers a big jab on 2nd-and-long with a 13-yard reception, then delivered a knockout blow with a 35-yard run up the gut on 3rd-and-1. That set up another McManus field goal, making it a two-possession game at 23-13.

Green Bay finally started to mount a bit of a rally, picking up their first first downs of the second half with under six minutes to go in the game. Love picked up a 4th-and-8 from near midfield, but he got crossed up once again on a route, this time with Bo Melton, and threw his third interception of the game to truly put the game on ice. Los Angeles ran out all but the last 30 seconds or so of clock on the ground, adding a short field goal for the game's final points.

The win gave Sean McVay his first victory over his friend and former protege, as Matt LaFleur had been 5-0 against LA heading in. Green Bay now likely needs to run the table to make a run at the playoffs, or at least get a significant amount of help, now that they have fallen out of playoff position.

#2658. NBA Cup 2026: 76ers' LeBron James oldest to post triple-double; Rockets' Kevin Durant fifth ever to score 33,000 points

Early in the second quarter Wednesday night in NBA Cup group play, LeBron James pulled up from Broad Street.

He drilled a 35-foot 3-pointer amid a torrid stretch that saw him make a trio of triples and score 14 straight points for the 76ers on Thanksgiving Eve. Before that sequence, the Sixers were trailing the Miami Heat by three points at Philadelphia's Xfinity Mobile Arena. Afterward, they were up eight.

While becoming the oldest player in NBA history to notch a triple double — at 41 years and 330 days old — James continued his recent hot streak from beyond the arc, going 4 of 6 on 3-point attempts. Philadelphia shot 14 of 35 from long range en route to a 111-104 victory.

The Heat (10-7) would have clinched East Group B had they come out on top, but Philadelphia (15-4) denied Miami an eighth consecutive win, despite losing Jaylen Brown to a hamstring injury late in the first half and Joel Embiid being sidelined by illness.

Nick Nurse's squad is now 2-1 in NBA Cup competition; Erik Spoelstra's Heat dropped to that same mark.

James finished with a season-high 25 points and 12 assists, plus 10 rebounds, with maybe his most notable dime taking place in the infancy of the fourth quarter. The 22-time All-Star flexed his elite court vision, timing a halfcourt lob perfectly to connect with VJ Edgecombe for a layup. James later dialed up an alley-oop for forward Dominick Barlow to throw down.

Edgecombe was among the Sixers players stepping up in the absence of Brown and Embiid, as Philadelphia protected its nine-point halftime lead and kept Miami at arm's length over the final two quarters. He had 18 points, and Tyrese Maxey poured in 30.

Kevin Durant is now one of five NBA players to score 33,000 points. The 38-year-old Houston Rockets forward achieved that feat on Wednesday night in a 116-104 home victory against the Phoenix Suns. He joined James, Kareem Abdul-Jabbar, Karl Malone and Kobe Bryant.

Durant did so while piling up 20 points for Houston (14-5), which, despite already being eliminated from NBA Cup knockout-round contention, kept pace in the upper echelon of the Western Conference standings.

The Rockets improved to 2-2 in Cup group play, while the Suns (9-8) slid to 1-2. They, too, have been eliminated from knockout-round contention in West Group A.

Along the way, Houston center Alperen Şengün passed Hakeem Olajuwon for the second-most triple-doubles in franchise history. Şengün, only 24 years old, secured his 15th performance of that kind with 23 points, 11 rebounds and 11 assists.

Late in the game, Suns wing Dillon Brooks got into it with Durant and was ejected after receiving his second technical foul of the night.

Although the Minnesota Timberwolves lost LaMelo Ball to an ankle injury Wednesday, they held on to defeat the Oklahoma City Thunder 113-106.

Jaden McDaniels came through, big time, in the West Group B matchup. In fact, by game's end, he was the third player in Wolves history to log at least 30 points, 10 rebounds and 5 assists in consecutive games. That elite company also includes Karl-Anthony Towns and Kevin Garnett.

After going for 34 points, 10 rebounds and 6 assists on Monday in a win over the Brooklyn Nets, McDaniels put up 32 points, 10 rebounds and 5 assists this time out versus the Thunder (10-9), who are 2-7 in their last nine games and eliminated from Cup knockout-round contention.

Minnesota (14-5), on the other hand, is atop West Group B with a 3-0 record. McDaniels beat the third-quarter buzzer with a ridiculous off-balance shot. A late 3-pointer from Wolves guard Ayo Dosunmu helped seal the deal.

#2655. Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

Only fitting for LeBron James to have his best game as a Sixer against the Miami Heat.

This was also a rare non-clutch win for the Sixers, who basically led from the second quarter on. James and Tyrese Maxey carried the offense for much of the night, while VJ Edgecombe filled in the gaps. (Happy Thanksgiving to all who celebrate! We're all thankful for all of you!)

LeBron James: 25 points, 12 assists, 10 rebounds, , 4-of-6 3PT, 1 steal

Another game where it just felt like Edgecombe was everywhere. The box score shows it, but he was relentless, seemingly playing at a different speed than anyone on the Heat. With Brown out, Edgecombe scored 13 of his points after halftime.

While the LeBron pass was ridiculous, don't sleep on the Edgecombe finish here.

The Center Triumvirate (Walker, Barlow, Bona): 16 points, 16 rebounds, 3 blocks, 1 steal

Piecing together 48 minutes without Embiid has generally felt like an impossible task. This season, it's felt even more difficult.

Against a frontcourt featuring Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo, the trio of Jabari Walker, Dominick Barlow and Adem Bona did a sensational job.

Antetokounmpo is great and he's going to get his, but those three (and Dean Wade) made him work for everything. Adebayo had a tough night, going just 6-of-16 from the field.

Barlow in particular has been outstanding since coming back from his back injury. Since making his season debut last Saturday against the Milwaukee Bucks, Barlow has been incredibly impactful offensively, defensively and on the glass.

#2649. Kalshi daily volume of ‘perps’ trading begins to top its prediction markets

Kalshi is known for its prediction markets across sports, politics and culture.

But now, for the first time, perpetual futures, Kalshi's second financial product, has surpassed the notional trading volume of its event contracts on consecutive days.

On Thursday, perpetual futures — commonly known as "perps" — on Kalshi saw $3.4 billion in notional volume as artificial intelligence laboratory Anthropic stock debuted on public markets while the rest of the market sold off. That exceeded about $3 billion in notional volume for Kalshi's prediction markets on the same day.

Perps are futures-style contracts where there is no expiration and traders do not need to own the underlying asset. Contracts instead continuously track the price of an asset, with funding payments to keep the contract price aligned with that of the market. Funding payments are made periodically between holders of short or long positions.

Perps trading surpassed event contracts one day in September, but on Friday perps topped prediction markets volume for a second day. Now Kalshi expects that performance to continue as it introduces perps tied to more individual stocks. On Monday, for example, Kalshi launched contracts linked to Anthropic.

Kalshi was the first to debut perps in the U.S. after winning federal regulatory approval in May to list perps tied to cryptocurrencies.

Perps trading volume includes any leverage that speculators acquire along with their trade, while event contracts are fully collateralized — for now.

Since the original launch in May, Kalshi has received approval to introduce perps tied to metals, crude oil, stock indexes and individual stocks.

Offshore perps — those available on exchanges not regulated in the U.S. — generated more than $90 trillion in notional trading volume in 2025, so it's not surprising that the product has grown so rapidly on Kalshi, according to Udesh Jha, chief risk officer at Kalshi Klear, the company's clearing house.

"We're just in very early innings. We're launching products, we're growing the participant base."

Perps were Kalshi's first product to mark its transition from a prediction market-focused platform to a full-blown financial exchange where various assets are traded. Last week, reports said that Kalshi is planning to offer trading on equities on its platform in the coming months.

At the same time, the company's perps product has come under fire. Some incumbent exchanges where futures are traded — which saw their stock prices decline following the approval of U.S. perps — believe the product was improperly approved. CME Group is suing the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, the federal derivatives regulator, for approving perps.

Others have simply expressed concerns about the efficiency of the markets. CNBC previously reported that trading volume on Kalshi's most popular perps markets — tied to bitcoin and ether cryptocurrencies — was dramatically greater than open interest, or cash actively in the markets. It's a phenomenon that isn't common with U.S.-regulated traditional futures, nor other offshore perps markets.

Kalshi said at the time it has "zero concerns" about the distinction, and that it appears because less leverage is offered compared to offshore exchanges and there are fewer incentives for traders to park capital on its perps markets.

While Kalshi started with cryptocurrencies, Owen Lau, an analyst at Clear Street, said perps tied to individual stocks likely represent the most promising opportunity for growth in the U.S.

"That's how I foresee the way perps will penetrate further into traditional asset classes," Lau said. "Perps on single stocks and [key performance indicators on individual companies] could change how investors hedge their portfolio or even make a directional bet."

Kalshi's growth has been powered by the fact that right now it's the only exchange that can actively list perps in the U.S., though crypto exchange Coinbase received approval in May to route U.S. users to offshore platforms for trading. That monopoly status, however, will probably prove short lived.

Coinbase has filed to list single-stock perps in the U.S., and President Donald Trump in August said the CFTC is working to bring Hyperliquid — a dominant player in offshore perps activity — to domestic traders.

But Jha at Kalshi isn't worried about the competition.

"What will carry us is ... making sure that we have the right products for the marketplace, making sure we have the right surveillance of the products that we are trading."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2646. Rams run over Packers in NFL's first Thanksgiving Eve game: Winners and losers

Call it Thanksgiving Eve, call it Drinksgiving if you want. But the NFL created another football holiday on Wednesday, Nov. 25, with its first prime-time regular-season game on the night before Turkey Day.

And, in a game with significant playoff implications, the Los Angeles Rams largely took the Green Bay Packers to the woodshed with a 26-13 victory … even if the entire affair was largely secondary to U2's halftime show (more on that later). Despite all the adversity LA has faced this season, the Rams go into the holiday on a five-game heater and looking like they might be rounding into the playoff team everyone expected them to be, at minimum, three months ago. Meanwhile, the Packers, even on a night when they got Micah Parsons back in the lineup, continue to head in the wrong direction.

Music? Postseason stakes? Turkey Day-adjacent football? So many winners to discuss … and a few losers:

As my dear colleague Mike Middlehurst-Schwartz said, "Rams, Packers in attendance for U2 concert." Nailed it, buddy.

Fifty years into their legendary, incomparable run as brilliant musicians and world-changing citizens, the boys from Dublin's Mount Temple Comprehensive School, with a healthy Larry Mullen Jr. back behind the drum kit, haven't lost a step − maybe just a little range in Bono's voice − as they return to the forefront of the global stage once again with their already acclaimed 16th studio album, 'Carnaval De Luz.' However the only new song they played at halftime on Wednesday night was "Torn," the band's collaboration with the late Dolly Parton in what was her final recording − and three years after she performed herself on an NFL Thanksgiving stage in Dallas.

Otherwise, the group, which will kick off a worldwide tour next year, played snippets from some of their most impactful songs of the 1980s: "Sunday Bloody Sunday" (intercut with backup singers doing the Supremes' "Stop! In the Name of Love"), "Pride," and, naturally − in another 360 NFL moment − "Where the Streets Have No Name," the capstone song of the quartet's unforgettable Super Bowl 36 halftime show, which paid tribute to the victims of Sept. 11.

Thanks, fellas. I'll be seeing you again (and again) soon.

One of the league's dominant defensive forces returned to action nearly a year after shredding his knee in Denver. Now he gets 11 days to shake off any further cobwebs − while emphasizing the import of run defense − before the Pack take the field again on Dec. 6 at New Orleans.

The entire ensemble is meant to honor the team's legendary "Fearsome Foursome" defensive line from the 1960s, which featured Rosey Grier, Lamar Lundy and Hall of Famers Deacon Jones and Merlin Olsen. Nice, clean look.

#2644. AI debt markets shudder as Nvidia's top 'banker' returns

AI credit markets are shuddering again with Jensen Huang back as CEO of Nvidia.

Fears about the massive debt being taken on to fund the artificial intelligence buildout climbed Tuesday as Wall Street weighed what Huang's return to Nvidia means for its bankrolling of the AI boom.

Huang is considered one of the top financiers, or bankers, in the AI industry, spearheading deals with neoclouds like CoreWeave and other firms to buy chips, backed in some cases by Nvidia guarantees. His return to Nvidia has revived prospects for the company to continue "circular financing" of AI chips and data centers — raising concerns among credit investors.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child of AI debt concerns, saw its stock sink 13% and the yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 top 17%. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

At the same time, the spread on the neocloud company's five-year credit default swaps jumped 8.5% to over 1,200 basis points. Credit default swaps are insurance against nonpayment and are considered a barometer of perceived risks. Rising CDS spreads mean investors view the debt as riskier.

Huang was reinstated as CEO on Monday after stepping down on Nov. 12 amid allegations of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. His reinstatement on Monday was cheered by investors, who pushed stocks higher, yields lower and spreads on CDS tighter. However, stocks retreated, bond yields rose and CDS spreads widened on Tuesday.

Ben Emons, founder and chief investment officer at FedWatch Advisors, called it a waning of the "Jensen effect."

The moves suggest "markets see government intervention as 'moral suasion' that carries credit and interest-rate risks," he said.

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday he "convinced" the board to bring Huang back.

CoreWeave wasn't the only name seeing heightened concerns in CDS spreads. Oracle's five-year CDS spread jumped 7% to 355.68. Even highly rated hyperscalers Meta Platforms and Amazon.com saw spreads widen by more than 2%. Nvidia's CDS added 3.4%.

Larry Holzenthaler, senior portfolio manager for fixed income at Catalyst Funds, believes the drama that unfolded with Huang's return to Nvidia won't necessarily sit well with credit investors.

"A lot of old school credit people are going to look at what kind of materialized there, the administration's involvement in putting him back in, and I think a lot of credit participants are going to step back and tiptoe away from that a bit," he said. "Credit market investors typically don't like that type of drama or uncertainty."

While Nvidia has commitments across the AI ecosystem, it is the neoclouds that have been under the most scrutiny. Many of the companies, which rent out AI computing power and cloud capacity, are highly leveraged and have relied on Nvidia financing to reduce their exposure to the bond market.

With so much riding on Nvidia's funding, Huang's next move is being closely watched.

During his brief absence, chief financial officer Colette Kress said during the company's earnings call that Nvidia would be "rigorously evaluating future investments" and will remain disciplined in how it commits capital.

That suggested Nvidia was going to be more judicious with financing, says Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"That concern should be put to rest with Jensen [Huang] back," he said.

Investor Steve Eisman, of "Big Short" fame and host of "The Real Eisman Playbook" podcast, believes Huang feels emboldened now that he's back in charge.

"[H]e'll double down on the circular financing," Eisman predicted on CNBC's "Squawk Box" Tuesday.

After Huang's departure, Eisman believed that the ecosystem the CEO created by pouring money into AI companies would likely unwind and leave some of the companies in "big trouble."

"Now that he's back, they're not," he said.

Others are not so certain the level of funding will continue.

"Given scrutiny of disclosure around financing and guarantees … we do not expect Mr. Huang's return to bring a quick reversion to broader customer financing," Stifel analyst Ruben Roy said in a note Monday.

Nvidia said on its earnings call that it has $118 billion in guarantees, as of Oct. 25, to back AI projects.

Concerns about the level of debt to fund the AI infrastructure buildout had already taken hold before Huang's departure and reinstatement.

"These issues have not disappeared just because the White House pressured the weak board to reinstate Jensen Huang," said economist David Rosenberg, founder and president of Rosenberg Research.

John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson Investors, believes having Huang back at the helm "reaffirms his existing go-to-market strategy." However, the credit market is still taking a cautious look at the neoclouds as it waits for more clarity on long-term supply/demand dynamics.

Still, Lloyd is cautiously positive on CoreWeave, which he owns in portfolios he manages, and noted all the data point to a "pretty healthy environment right now." That includes strong residual values on graphics processing units and spot compute rates, he noted.

"The company is doing everything in its power to execute," he said. "They don't need the high yield market at this time."

In the meantime, investigations into Nvidia and Huang are ramping up. Huang is expected to appear before three House committees next week. Nvidia is conducting an independent investigation into his activities, and California Attorney General Rob Bonta has launched a probe. At the federal level, President Trump said Monday that U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

"There's other shoes to drop," Rosenberg said. "I don't think that the book has been closed on this file."

#2643. Dan Hurley calls out empty seats at Duke-UConn, says game 'deserved' sellout

LAS VEGAS — Connecticut coach Dan Hurley criticized the crowd for his team's game against Duke, which the coach said "deserved to be" sold out.

The Elite Eight rematch brought two of the biggest brands in college basketball, with clear national championship aspirations, to T-Mobile Arena in Las Vegas. While the game lived up to the billing, ending in a 77-63 Blue Devils win, not every seat was filled for it.

The announced attendance was 8,507, in an arena that can hold up to 20,000 people. There were empty sections in the upper bowl, along with curtains blocking off other parts.

That was enough for Hurley to say this game should have been played at one of the home campuses, or a site like Madison Square Garden in New York City.

"I promise you, if we played at MSG, it would have been sold out. There would have been 20,000 people. There wouldn't have been sections that were empty," Hurley said. "I mean, we had 8,500 people here, you know? We do want to play a schedule similar to what we're playing this year, every non-conference season. But I think anytime that Duke and UConn can play, it's a big deal.

"Maybe it would be better if we came to Cameron then if they came to Storrs, or if we did it in a place where it would have been sold out like it deserved to be."

Hurley's comments came amid Las Vegas hosting the Players Era tournaments, which have seen several ranked teams playing in mostly empty arenas. The tournaments aren't alone. Several neutral site games have not drawn large crowds, which has caused groans among college basketball fans who want these games played at home arenas.

The Duke-UConn matchup marked the first time they've met in the regular season since 2014. All 12 of their meetings have been at neutral sites, six of which came in the NCAA Tournament. This was the first time they played each other west of Texas.

The game was put together by DBG Sports, and was streamed on Amazon's Prime Video as part of Duke's multi-year broadcast partnership with Amazon.

When asked about Hurley's comments, Duke coach Jon Scheyer said the program is "always about finding unique ways we can do games that maybe other programs can't," adding name, image and likeness plays a role in it. Duke reportedly has NIL opportunities through the Amazon deal.

Scheyer added he doesn't know of any talks about Duke and UConn scheduling a home-and-home series, but he's not backing down from playing top-tier opponents.

"As far as UConn and all that, there's a great tradition there, and there's not talks as far as I know of, but I think we've shown we're willing to play anybody with the schedule that we have and so are they. I'm sure this won't be the last time we play each other," Scheyer said.

#2640. ‘Am I crazy?’ – Bill Simmons thinks the Packers have been ‘screwed’ by NFL referees in 2026

Bill Simmons has publicly brought up one of the major concerns he's observed about how NFL officials have been treating the Green Bay Packers in the 2026 NFL season.

The Packers fell to a 26-13 loss to the Los Angeles Rams on November 25 for Wednesday Night Football. While it was hard to overcome Blake Corum's 200+-yard rushing performance, Packers fans left the game feeling hard-done by the referees once again.

It was a pattern that multiple online fans have been bringing up for weeks that became more apparent after the loss to the Rams. It's gotten to the point where even Bill Simmons feels like it merits a discussion.

After the Packers loss to the Rams, veteran media personality Bill Simmons shared a message on X, questioning if his eyes telling him that the Packers are consistently getting screwed is real or not.

"Have the Packers gotten screwed by more calls than every other team in 2026 or am I crazy?"

There is some truth to Simmons assertion, with specific areas of foul-calling seeing an almost historic swing against the Packers.

The Packers have been the most penalised team in the NFL when it comes to roughing the passer, with another controversial call in this regard made against the Rams.

The nine roughing-the-passer calls against the Packers are the most in the NFL by more than double, with the second-ranked team picking up four roughing-the-passer calls over the season.

Not only that, Packers QB Jordan Love has drawn zero roughing flags this season despite being hit frequently by opposing defenders throughout the season. The disproportionate 'roughing the passer' calls for and against the Packers have become too much to ignore.

The Packers also rank near the bottom for unnecessary roughness and personal fouls this season. Outside of these specific areas, the Packers have not seen any patterns that break the league's overall average.

Teams like the Carolina Panthers and Cleveland Browns rank higher in terms of the absolute most-penalised teams in the NFL, but the debatable nature of what's happening in Packers games and the lack of proportion in 'roughing the passer' is raising more eyebrows.

#2636. What's gone so wrong at Man City? Enzo Maresca's muddled team in danger of complete collapse

The confidence-depleted Citizens' miserable recent run continued in Europe this week, and the manager must find solutions for their malaise quickly

Enzo Maresca looked like the true heir to Pep Guardiola after the first month of the 2026-27 campaign. Manchester City opened with eight successive wins, including a pair of particularly impressive triumphs away at Manchester United and Liverpool, and had the swagger of genuine contenders, despite the cloud of a guilty verdict in their much-debated Premier League financial case hanging over the club.

Continuing with the same slick brand of football Guardiola always championed at the Etihad Stadium, it appeared that Maresca was building something special after a significant squad overhaul in the summer. Unfortunately for City fans, though, the magic has worn off quicker than they ever would have imagined.

City have only won one of their last seven games in all competitions, narrowly beating RB Leipzig 3-2 in Germany, while they only progressed to the Carabao Cup quarter-finals after edging Brighton on penalties, having played out a 2-2 draw in normal time. In the Premier League, Maresca's men were humbled by Aston Villa, Brighton and Nottingham Forest before being held to a 1-1 draw at home by struggling Fulham, which left them six points adrift of leaders Arsenal.

There was a big opportunity for an uplift on Tuesday as City hosted a Napoli team in freefall under Massimiliano Allegri, but they blew it, ultimately scraping a 2-2 draw that damaged their hopes of automatic qualification for the Champions League last-16. Next up, it's the small matter of a trip to the Emirates Stadium, where Arsenal will be looking to take a massive step towards defending their title.

It's shaping up to be a complete mismatch, because City are a mess right now. Their season is in danger of unravelling heading into the festive period unless Maresca can bring back a sense of cohesion. But what exactly has gone so wrong?

Napoli ruthlessly capitalised on City's defensive fragility at the Etihad, with their attackers afforded far too much time and space in the final third

City twice came from behind to rescue a point against Napoli, with Rayan Cherki stepping up as the hero in the 86th minute, gliding past his marker in the box before firing a precise low shot past Vanja Milinkovic-Savic in the opposing net. Teenage midfielder Ayyoub Bouaddi also netted his first goal for the club, but those two moments of brilliance did not paper over the cracks for City's frighteningly fragile backline.

Napoli caught the Citizens trying to play out from the back before opening the scoring, with Billy Gilmour then playing in former Manchester United striker Rasmus Hojlund, who evaded March Guehi with a simple change of direction before finishing into the far corner.

The second was even more shambolic, as Matteo Politano took Nico O'Reilly out of the game with a superb hooked pass for Leonardo Spinazzola. The full-back then had all the time in the world to pick out as a cross as Josko Gvardiol hesitated to close him down, and found the unmarked Noa Lang to lash home a volley at the back post. Napoli carved City open like a knife through butter, much to the dismay of club legend Stuart Pearce.

"There is a vulnerability about City. You can play through them and that is a problem," Pearce told talkSPORT. "They don't look as though they're a side that can actually keep a clean sheet, and that is probably their biggest problem, and the biggest damning stat of this recent run. They're almost too easy to play through at the moment. Teams draw City onto them and then play through them - either on the floor, or sometimes go beyond them and hit 50, 60-yard balls that defenders should not let bounce."

While the defence has dramatically underperformed, City's midfield has not been providing enough protection

Indeed, City have conceded in each of their last 10 fixtures, their worst run since December 2019. Guehi is too prone to costly individual errors, including the mis-control that led to Villa's first goal in their home win against City, and Ruben Dias has looked uncomfortable trying to start attacks under pressure.

Gvardiol, meanwhile, is caught between roles right now; pushing inside from left-back into midfield pockets has left him exposed to turnovers, most notably when he was robbed of possession in the early stages of the 3-1 defeat at the City Ground by Jai Cunha, who proceeded to drive forward and put Forest in front.

O'Reilly has also struggled on that side, Abdukodir Khusanov and Matheus Nunes' lack of defensive awareness has been a problem for City on the right, and Gianluigi Donnarumma is no longer a reliable presence between the sticks, letting the team down with both his distribution and handling.

The structural problems extend into the midfield, too, where City are greatly missing Rodri's ability to screen counter-attacks. Pairing club-record summer signings Enzo Fernandez and Elliot Anderson in the double-pivot simply hasn't worked, because both of them naturally want to push forward, which leaves huge gaps for opponents to exploit. Bouaddi is a more natural fit to partner Anderson, and they combined to make City look more solid for extended periods in the Fulham draw and against Napoli, but Maresca has subsequently had to force Fernandez into a No.10 role that doesn't suit him.

Fernandez is a deep-lying playmaker who arrives late in the box, not a back-to-goal technical wizard in the mould of Cherki or Phil Foden. It's a waste of £126m to put the Argentina international in behind Erling Haaland, and forces Cherki out of the central areas he does the most damage in and onto the wing.

City's usually reliable No.9 is suddenly missing chances and sitting on the fringes of matches as the team toils for fluidity

With the balance off in the middle of the pitch, City's build-up play has been laborious and predictable, and Haaland has been isolated up top. The Norwegian terminator started the season in typically ominous form, scoring 14 goals in 12 appearances, but hasn't netted in any of his last four outings, and didn't muster a single shot on goal in the Napoli game.

When the chances have arrived, Haaland's clinical touch has deserted him. For example in the Fulham draw, he recorded 1.03 expected goals (xG) from four shots without scoring, even blazing over from point-blank range after being teed up by Foden.

Haaland is averaging just 23 touches through his 11 Premier League outings, and recorded only 14 against Napoli despite playing the full 90 minutes while wearing the captain's armband. That lack of involvement doesn't matter when he is bursting the net consistently, but without the end product, the 26-year-old is just a passenger.

"We have to do better and we have to find some ways that make us control the game more and perform in a better way," Haaland said in a post-match interview with beIN Sport. "I have no idea what we were missing, but you could feel it in the last few games that there's something that's not clicking fully and we have to find that out.

"We have to stay positive and we have to get the best out of every single player. With or without the armband, I feel huge responsibility because all I want to do is perform for this club and win games for City. But right now we're not doing that and it hurts."

He added to TNT Sports: "Football is easy and difficult. When you win it's easy and you know what to do. When you don't, you start questioning everything." And therein lies the main reason for City's decline; the likes of Haaland, Guehi and Fernandez haven't become bad players overnight, but a mental block has seemingly set in.

Maresca is facing an uphill battle to motivate his players again as City wait on the final result of their appeal

The difference between Haaland's demeanour this week and after City took down Liverpool 3-0 in early October is night and day. City had a siege mentality at Anfield in the face of universal condemnation after an independent commission found the club guilty of 114 breaches of the Premier League's financial rules.

However, the bleak reality of the situation has since clearly had an adverse impact on the squad. City have appealed the verdict, but the expectation is that an earth-shattering punishment will be issued before the end of the season, whether that's a record points deduction, relegation to the Championship, or even expulsion from the Football League.

With so much uncertainty surrounding the club, there is no meaning to anything that happens on the pitch, at least domestically. All the effort they put into a potential title race will almost certainly amount to nothing, and so the stakes are non-existent in every game. A Champions League challenge won't be possible if the players continue to look so distracted either.

It's only natural that every member of Maresca's squad, and the manager himself, will privately be worrying about their futures. That is the obvious explanation for City's significantly reduced intensity and why they head into Sunday's clash with Arsenal as major underdogs.

Maresca has so far refused to criticise his players, and downplayed their poor form by pointing to transitional periods that City's main rivals went through. "It's not easy to change after 10 years with the same manager: it was difficult for us, for Arsenal, for United," he told Sky Italia after the Napoli draw.

That excuse doesn't fly, though, after a £464-million recruitment drive. City have one of the deepest squads in all of Europe's top five leagues and more than enough quality to be doing better than they are. Maresca has to find a way to lift motivation levels from the floor, or else City will face a crisis long before learning their legal fate.

#2635. Nvidia's billion-dollar board bet: Who's protected as lawsuits mount?

Jensen Huang is back as CEO of Nvidia, after President Donald Trump urged the company's board to rehire Huang. But investigations into the company, its executive leadership and its board continue as outside criticism mounts.

"Clearly they caved to external pressure," said Betsy Atkins, an author and longtime public-company director. "The board failed," she said, when it initially allowed Huang's removal. "The board's role is to think of all stakeholders."

California Attorney General Rob Bonta is investigating whether Nvidia misrepresented information to investors. "There is classic corporate securities law on the books in California that prevents investors and the public from being misled by companies," Bonta said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Tuesday.

Directors are already defendants in a shareholder lawsuit accusing them of failing to oversee Nvidia. A separate securities class action targets the company and Huang, its co-founder.

Now the board’s insurance choices are part of the story. A risk advisor with direct knowledge of Nvidia’s coverage says the company built an approximately $1 billion D&O tower, insurance jargon for directors and officers insurance policies from different companies that combine to cover one massive risk.

Nvidia "loves insurance," the advisor said. “They buy insurance as if they have no balance sheet.” A billion-dollar tower would essentially mean Nvidia "bought the market," purchasing all the coverage it could get, according to an industry expert with 25 years working in financial insurance. The expert asked not to be named out of concern for past or potential future clients.

But Nvidia directed all that coverage to Side A only, according to CNBC's source. Side A protects directors and officers personally when the company cannot indemnify them. Side B covers the company for legal defense costs or settlements on behalf of directors and officers, and Side C protects the company when it's named as a defendant in a suit such as a securities claim.

The risk advisor says Nvidia believed it had "the war chest" to protect itself against a securities class action suit.

That choice is raising questions about whether the board protected itself at the company’s expense. CNBC asked Nvidia for comment but received no response.

An insurance CEO characterized a billion-dollar policy as a lot of coverage, but said a Side A-only allocation was a surprising move because it results in imbalanced coverage, protecting only directors.

That structure is not automatically a governance failure, according to a D&O insurance expert with long experience in coverage for banks, drugmakers and other businesses that often face litigation. Side A-only programs are common among companies with the capital to manage the corporate risk internally.

"For a company with Nvidia’s resources, retaining the company-side risk can make financial sense," the advisor said. It's Side A D&O that's "imperative to corporate governance."

"It is a capital-allocation decision," the expert told me. The company decides that buying insurance for its own balance sheet is not worth the premium. In other words, Side B and Side C are considered too expensive for the coverage the company gets.

The question for shareholders is whether the board’s decision to hold that risk was a sound use of the company’s resources and whether the company can absorb the costs if the claims grow.

A shareholder derivative complaint filed Nov. 11 names Huang, directors and other officers. It alleges that they failed to oversee risks involving Huang’s relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang and made misleading statements. It also alleges that some defendants sold roughly $2 billion in Nvidia stock while holding material nonpublic information. Those remain allegations, not findings.

A separate securities class action, filed Nov. 9, names Nvidia and Huang. It seeks damages for investors who bought Nvidia shares between Oct. 9, 2025, and Nov. 8, 2026, alleging the company and Huang misled investors.

The distinction matters: The derivative suit is brought on Nvidia’s behalf, while the class action seeks recovery for investors.

"Insurance is already at work," says Michel Léonard, chief economist at the Insurance Information Institute, describing standard policy mechanics, not Nvidia’s specific policy. "Once the suit is filed, D&O starts paying defense costs, long before anyone proves anything."

A claim still has to meet the policy’s terms, and defense costs can reduce the remaining limits. Nor does a drop in Nvidia’s market value by itself establish an insurable loss. Plaintiffs would need to prove legally recoverable damages; the policy would respond only to covered losses.

The board has already taken steps it says are aimed at accountability. Nvidia announced an independent investigation on Nov. 8, and independent directors retained outside counsel two days later. Huang resigned as CEO and a director on Nov. 11; the board appointed CFO Colette Kress interim CEO. Nvidia said it had no reason to believe national security or company secrets had been compromised by Huang's extramarital relationships.

Atkins, who joined the board of Wynn Resorts in 2018 after founder Steve Wynn resigned, argues that allegations are not conclusions and that boards should also weigh governance concerns against the value a founder brings to a company’s innovation. Nvidia's investigation is continuing even as Huang resumes leadership.

“Will companies’ hyper-accelerated innovation speed be the same without them? I don’t think so,” said Atkins, author of "Don't Waste a Board Seat: A Decision-Maker's Guide to Choosing Directors Who Create Value."

Nvidia’s 2026 proxy also says the company has indemnity agreements with its directors and officers — separate from its insurance. Those agreements provide for the company to cover certain costs and liabilities when the law permits.

#2634. Report: G.M. Omar Khan called Aaron Rodgers directly to inform him of the benching

As expected, the Steelers view the events that resulted in the benching and eventual release of quarterback Aaron Rodgers differently than Rodgers does.

On one very specific point — as it relates to whether the Steelers properly handled the situation — the Steelers and Rodgers sharply disagree.

Rodgers said Wednesday that he learned he’d be benched and inactive when tight end Pat Freiermuth called Rodgers, after the announcement of the move was made at 11:10 p.m. ET on Tuesday night. However, Tom Pelissero of Netflix reported during the Wednesday night pregame show that G.M. Omar Khan called Rodgers to inform him of the change to Mason Rudolph.

It’s odd, to say the least, that the Steelers announced the move after 11:00 p.m. local time on a Tuesday night. If Rodgers didn’t know, there was no reason to let the world (and specifically the Broncos) know the move had been made.

The mere fact that the Steelers announced it when they did suggests that Rodgers knew about the decision, and that the Steelers believed he possibly would release it in terms that supported his broader version of the events.

By announcing it on Tuesday night, the Steelers pre-empted a late-night social-media post from Rodgers — or a strategic leak to a reporter — painting the situation in the light most favorable to Rodgers. For example, Rodgers could have characterized that he was benched because he was repeatedly blowing the whistle internally about coach Mike McCarthy’s various alleged lapses.

While it’s a minor point, it points to the broader issue of credibility. If Rodgers knew about the decision, any suggestion that he didn’t would be calculated to make the Steelers look inept for telling the world he’d been benched without letting him know about it in advance.

The truth may never be fully known. But it’s clear that, as to the things that happened over the past day or two, there are (as there always seems to be) two versions — Rodgers’s version and the team’s version.

It’s also possible there are three versions. Rodgers’s version, the Steelers’ version, and the truth.

#2632. Rams ride Blake Corum to Thanksgiving Eve win over Packers; Jordan Love throws 2 INTs with Christian Watson out

On his 26th birthday and in the NFL's first Thanksgiving Eve game, Los Angeles Rams running back Blake Corum starred with 184 rushing yards during a 26-13 win over the Green Bay Packers at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, California.

Corum became the first Rams player to carry the ball at least 30 times in a game during the Sean McVay era. Meanwhile, the Packers' offense floundered after losing wide receiver Christian Watson to a concussion late in the first half.

Green Bay quarterback Jordan Love threw a pair of interceptions and could have had a couple more. His team mustered a mere 55 total yards of offense in the second half.

McVay, previously 0-5 against Packers head coach Matt LaFleur, finally beat his former assistant. The Rams (7-4) have won five straight games. Green Bay (6-5) remains last in a crowded NFC North.

Packers edge rusher Micah Parsons, playing in his first game in nearly a year after tearing an ACL and meniscus in his left knee against the Denver Broncos in Week 15 last season, recorded a long-awaited sack.

Watson left the game twice: first with a thigh injury after making a 6-yard catch on the opening play from scrimmage and then for the concussion caused by a massive hit near the sideline late in the second quarter for which Rams safety Kamren Kinchens was ejected.

Forging forward without wide receivers Puka Nacua — who is reportedly out for the season after undergoing MCL surgery — and Davante Adams — who was still sidelined by a hamstring injury — the Rams managed to stake themselves to a 13-3 advantage, scoring on their first three possessions.

Stafford, who finished with 140 yards on 16-of-23 passing, made do with his lesser-known receivers. A 37-yard defensive pass interference penalty catapulted L.A.'s first drive. That ended with a 43-yard Brandon McManus field goal. McManus trotted back out to kick a 41-yarder following a Rams takeaway later in the opening frame. L.A. linebacker Nate Landman, forcing his eighth regular-season fumble in the past two seasons, punched the ball free from the grasp of Packers tight end Tucker Kraft.

A 32-yard connection between Love and Watson put the wheels in motion for Green Bay. Rookie kicker Trey Smack, who came in with just 15 makes on 21 field-goal attempts, got the Packers on the board with a 54-yard kick.

That's when Stafford answered with the kind of efficiency that made him the league MVP last season. He went 5 of 6, at one point keeping the series alive with a clutch dump-off to running back Kyren Williams despite duress on third-and-5. It wasn't long before Williams was surging through the middle for a 7-yard touchdown run to make it a two-score game.

A pair of highlight-reel plays thrust Green Bay right back into the thick of things. LaFleur turned to trickery initially, with wideout Savion Williams taking a handoff and throwing the ball back to Love, who went on to fling a pass downfield to Watson. His toe-tapping 21-yard reception was followed by a 39-yard catch and run from another receiver, Matthew Golden.

Watson was the one who found pay dirt, reeling in a 6-yard touchdown pass from Love midway through the second quarter. That score cut the Packers' deficit to 13-10.

Watson's night came to a premature end on Green Bay's next drive. LaFleur rolled the dice on fourth-and-1 from the L.A. 30-yard line, and it originally looked like that decision was going to be rewarded. Love located Watson for what would have been a chains-moving pickup, except the pass was broken up by a brutal blow from Kinchens near the sideline that leveled Watson.

Kinchens was ejected because, per the Netflix broadcast, he left his feet and made forcible contact to the head/neck area with his helmet.

Offsetting penalties — Golden was flagged for offensive pass interference — brought up another fourth-and-1. That time, Smack was sent out for a 48-yard field-goal attempt.

With that boot, it was 13-13. It remained that way heading into the break.

Wednesday was a game of attrition. Not only did the Packers lose Watson, but the Rams watched a pair of tight ends — Davis Allen (ankle) and Tyler Higbee (chest) — go out, along with left guard Bill Murray (ankle).

Even so, L.A.'s offense kept churning. It relied heavily on Corum. The ascending third-year pro from Michigan carried the rock nine times on a 14-play, eight-plus-minute drive that began the third quarter. Corum scored from a yard out to restore the Rams' lead.

While dominating possession, the Rams eventually established a cushion — that was after Stafford and Love traded interceptions. The latter took place in L.A. territory, as miscommunication between Love and Golden cost the Packers dearly.

A 35-yard Corum run set the stage for the third of McManus' four field goals.

Although Love converted a fourth-and-8 with a strike to wideout Bo Melton down the stretch, he tossed his second pick the next snap. Rams cornerback Emmanuel Forbes Jr. dropped deep into coverage and climbed the ladder to force the Packers' third turnover.

L.A.'s roster may be depleted, but McVay's group is overcoming that cascade of injuries with toughness. The Rams have now rushed for at least 200 yards in back-to-back games.

"Our identity going into this game was the most physical team's gonna win," Corum said during his on-field postgame interview. "And I think we showed that today. It's something to build on."

Blake Corum is easily the player of the game for LA. The RB finishes with 184 yards and a TD on 32 rushes.

The Rams improve to 7-4 and remain in the thick of the playoff picture.

Packers fall to 6-5 and have some ground to make up in the wild-card race.

#2631. Steelers should have known Aaron Rodgers, Mike McCarthy couldn't co-exist

When the Steelers hired coach Mike McCarthy, we cautioned against assuming that this meant there would be a reunion between McCarthy and quarterback Aaron Rodgers. That position assumed there would be a commonsense assessment of the specific history of McCarthy and Rodgers in Green Bay by those with the power to bring them together for the present and the future.

The reporting of Tyler Dunne, who at the time wrote for Bleacher Report, made it clear that there were issues between them during the latter years of McCarthy’s time with the Packers. Rodgers took it to another level on Wednesday, telling Pat McAfee and company that McCarthy believed Rodgers got him fired by the Packers — and that McCarthy had done some of the things he’s been doing with the Steelers when McCarthy coached the Packers.

If McCarthy believes Rodgers got him fired by the Packers, why in the world would McCarthy want Rodgers in his Pittsburgh locker room? It’s ludicrous. It’s asinine. It’s self-destructive.

From Rodgers’s perspective, why in the world would he want to play for McCarthy again? Although Rodgers said Wednesday that he believed McCarthy would be different given it’s Rodgers’s last year in the NFL and McCarthy’s first year in his dream job, it was naive for Rodgers to think that, at 63, McCarthy would suddenly start working harder than he ever has.

Oh, they tried to sell us on the idea that he would. “Attention to detail” became a talking point during training camp. Plenty of media members swallowed the hook.

Rodgers knew better. But he ignored his own experiences with McCarthy — in the same way McCarthy ignored his own experiences with Rodgers.

The situation cried out for someone, anyone in the organization to stand up and say, “Are we nuts? Once adversity strikes, this situation will crumble. And it will take us with it.”

It didn’t just crumble. It imploded into one of the most embarrassing situations any NFL franchise has endured in years, if not ever.

As the saying goes, when you dance with the devil, you don’t change the devil. The devil changes you. By allowing McCarthy and Rodgers to reprise their Lambeau rumba, the Steelers thought the music would stop in the postseason. Instead, McCarthy and Rodgers have pulled a franchise with six Super Bowl trophies into the eighth circle of dysfunction.

In the end, the team in the city known for blast furnaces has found itself in football hell.

#2630. Texas Faces Its Biggest Test Yet as Undefeated Showdown Looms Against Georgetown

SAN DIEGO — Thanksgiving Day will feature a matchup between two teams looking to protect perfect records.

The No. 7 Texas Longhorns (6-0) will face the Georgetown Hoyas (6-0) on Thursday in the opening round of the Rady Children's Invitational. Tipoff is scheduled for 2:30 p.m. ET at Jenny Craig Pavilion in San Diego.

Both programs have opened the season without a loss. However, Thursday presents a significant early test for each side.

The game will air nationally on FS1. The winner advances to Friday's championship game against either UCLA or Saint Mary's.

Texas enters the tournament ranked No. 7 in the AP Top 25 and No. 5 in the coaches poll.

The Longhorns have scored at least 92 points in five of their six victories. Their latest performance was a 97-61 win over Southern Indiana.

Junior center Matas Vokietaitis continues to lead the way. He averages 20.7 points and 8.8 rebounds per game.

Vokietaitis recorded 20 points and 10 rebounds in the latest victory. He also leads a Texas lineup featuring five players averaging double figures.

Head coach Sean Miller has seen his team establish itself offensively. Texas is averaging 94 points per game through six contests.

The Longhorns also secured a notable neutral-site victory over Baylor earlier this season. That experience could prove valuable against Georgetown.

The Hoyas haven't taken long to make their presence felt this season.

Georgetown (6-0) is averaging 83.7 points per game while shooting 50% from the field. The program has already picked up a road victory over Clemson.

Senior center Vince Iwuchukwu has been a major difference-maker. He averages 20.8 points and 9.2 rebounds per game.

Iwuchukwu is coming off a dominant performance against Coppin State. He finished with a career-high 29 points and 10 rebounds while making all 11 of his field-goal attempts.

Guard Jaland Lowe adds another dimension to the offense. He averages 16.3 points and 7.3 assists per game.

The Hoyas also have experience against quality competition. Their 86-74 victory over Maryland and road win at Clemson stand out.

Thursday's matchup will mark the fourth meeting between the programs.

Georgetown currently holds a 2-1 advantage in the all-time series. The Hoyas won the most recent meeting, defeating then-No. 22 Texas 82-66 in November 2019.

Georgetown head coach Ed Cooley also enters the matchup with a 2-0 record against Texas. Miller, meanwhile, will look to even the series.

Both teams have plenty to prove as the tournament begins. Texas has the higher ranking, but Georgetown has already shown it can compete against quality opponents.

With two undefeated records on the line, Thursday's matchup could deliver one of the more entertaining games of Thanksgiving.

The Cavs end the night officially below .500 with a 9-10 record. Not how anyone hoped this season would start.

#2626. “Hasn’t really recovered”: Peter Schmeichel pinpoints where it has all gone wrong for Senne Lammens at Man United

Manchester United goalkeeper Senne Lammens had a disastrous evening for the club in their 2-2 draw with Sporting CP.

The Red Devils made another poor start in Europe, conceding the first goal for the fourth European match in a row.

Lammens had actually been the hero moments earlier when he had managed to save Luis Suarez's penalty but was the villain of the piece just seconds later.

The Belgian came charging out of his goal and brought down the Sporting attacker, giving the referee an easy call to make.

United then recovered in the second half and fought their way back to a 2-1 lead thanks to two goals from former Sporting hero Bruno Fernandes.

Mauricio Pochettino's side looked like they were destined to hold on and win just their fourth match of the season but they once again got in their own way.

Lammens, who was so reliable last year, once again managed to ruin the night when he dropped a cross under very little pressure and Suarez tucked away the rebound in acrobatic style.

The Champions League-winning goalkeeper who played for both clubs suggested he was worried that Lammens has not recovered from his disastrous cameo from the bench which cost Belgium in their World Cup defeat to Spain in the summer.

Speaking to CBS Sports (via The Sun), he explained that he doesn't think taking Lammens out of the team would have any real benefit.

He explained, "yeah, I don't know when that point is. Pochettino has come in, he's brought his own staff, that means he's got a new goalkeeping coach. Maybe that is something that can change it for him."

The Dane continued to state, "in the World Cup he came on against Spain and made a mistake. Merino scored and they got knocked out and I feel like he's lost confidence from that mistake, hasn't really recovered. As a goalkeeper, when you're not on your best form you need your defenders in front of you to help you out and they haven't been able to do that and that just makes it so much worse and today was not a great day for him."

Lammens will likely have a chance to make amends when United welcome Brentford to Old Trafford on Saturday afternoon in Pochettino's Old Trafford debut.
