#2623. NFL Thanksgiving Day preview: Eagles QB Joe Milton hopes to spoil Cowboys' hopes in his old home

The middle game of the Thanksgiving trio — the stuffing, if you will — lacks the meat and potatoes of the first and final games of the day. But Eagles at Cowboys still carries some potential playoff importance.

The 5-6 Cowboys host the 3-7 Eagles in a game that matters to Dallas. The Cowboys' offense has hit its stride recently, scoring 40 and 51 points, respectively, the past two games. Over the past five games, Dallas hasn't scored fewer than 27.

The problem? They're only 3-2 in that stretch, and the NFC East-leading Giants have kept that same pace.

The other problem? Dallas' defense. There were modest signs of improvement on that side of the ball in Mike Pettine's first game calling plays, especially compared to how the defense played against the 49ers. And for the first game possibly all season, the Cowboys could make a strong claim that they had the better defense — the Titans are that bad now, yes.

The Eagles limped into the bye with seven straight losses, but they're coming off one of their best efforts of the season in beating the Steelers on Sunday. Defensively, they kept the Steelers under wraps for most of the four quarters and gave QB Joe Milton and the offense a chance.

Milton returns to Dallas, where he spent parts of the past two seasons and saw action in four games last season behind Dak Prescott. Now Milton gets a chance to take on his former QB1 on Thursday after signing with the Eagles after Jalen Hurts landed on injured reserve.

The Cowboys left Milton, a preseason star the past two years, unprotected on their practice squad, and the Eagles gladly scooped him up. He has a chance to make the Cowboys pay for letting him walk — and before one of the biggest TV audiences Milton might have.

The Eagles' playoff hopes hover at less than 1%, according to Next Gen Stats. The Cowboys, however, are still alive at 22%, barely above the Commanders (21%) and behind the Giants (65%).

A loss would be pretty devastating. But beating Philadelphia would give the Cowboys a 3-1 mark in the division, which is the one edge they have right now on the Giants, who are 2-1-1 in NFC East games. The East could come down to the Week 17 Giants-Cowboys game, but every divisional matchup counts.

The Eagles, however, didn't look like a team ready to mail it in on Sunday. They showed some renewed vitality after the bye, with the team giving some younger players opportunities to stand out, including Milton. For Milton, there's personal pride on the line after spending parts of the past two years in Dallas, facing a defense that has plenty to prove.

When the Eagles traded Jordan Davis to the Jaguars, it shone the spotlight more on Jalen Carter, who started this season slowly but whose play has been on the rise lately. In Sunday's upset of the Steelers, Carter was active and effective despite suffering a quad injury just prior to the game. He was a full participant in practice this week, but the player he might have faced off against, Cowboys right guard Tyler Booker, did not practice and is not expected to play Thursday. The Eagles know they have to slow down Dallas' passing attack and pressure Dak Prescott, and the best way to do that might be to detonate the operation from the inside. Carter and the Eagles also can't have a repeat of the last meeting, in Week 7, when Dallas ran for 204 yards on 38 carries.

Sportsbooks opened this game Cowboys -1.5 in the summer — now it's -8.5. Losing Jalen Hurts for the season, along with a 3-7 record (and a putrid 2-8 record against the spread) will do that. Philadelphia put up a spirited defensive effort in a 19-16 win over the Pittsburgh Steelers as 3.5-point home underdogs on Sunday, but this Dallas offense will provide a much stiffer test. Eagles starting QB Joe Milton and Philadelphia collected only 11 first downs and 214 yards of total offense; that's not going to get it done. Dak Prescott & Co. can't control how well a rejuvenated defense plays under Mike Pettine, but the Cowboys have scored at least 27 points in each of the past five games. Dallas scored 51 points against Tennessee on Sunday; the Eagles have scored 40 points combined in their past three contests. The Eagles D will put up a good fight, but barring several turnovers, I don't believe it will be able to keep up with Dallas. Cowboys -2.5 is another good teaser leg to go with that Packers +8.5, which cashed on Wednesday. — Ben Fawkes

Check out more of Ben's work at the Yahoo Sports betting hub.

We always expect plentiful points in any Dallas game, even with a struggling Philadelphia offense on the other side. Here are three fantasy themes to track as you watch the middle of the three Thanksgiving games.

1. What version of Saquon Barkley do we see? Barkley is expected to play despite a neck injury, but he hasn't been a high-volume back in 2026. Last week's 17 touches against the Steelers were actually his high for the year, which tells you a lot about Philly's season. This is also the worst Philadelphia offensive line in several years.

2. How involved will George Pickens be? Dallas seemed to lose the plot with Pickens a few weeks ago, sending him just 10 targets over a two-week period (including a 4-57-1 game against Philadelphia). Since then, Dak Prescott has peppered both Pickens and CeeDee Lamb, and the Cowboys have scored 91 points over two weeks. Several teams in the NFL employ wide usage trees. Dallas doesn't want to be one of them.

3. Is DeVonta Smith ready to pop? Smith saw plenty of opportunity in the win over Pittsburgh (12 targets), though he returned a modest 5-41-0 line. The Eagles have a capped ceiling with Joe Milton at quarterback, but at least they're trying to get Smith going. Dallas' secondary has been torched for most of the season. — Scott Pianowski

#2622. Player Grades: Cavs at Knicks – Another fourth quarter implosion

The Cleveland Cavaliers took another painful loss to the New York Knicks after going off the rails in the fourth quarter.

Grades are based on our usual expectations for each player.

30 points, 4 rebounds, 4 assists, 4 steals, 2 blocks, 2 turnovers

Mitchell was lights out in the first half, storming downhill and busting New York's point-of-attack defense. The Knicks had no answer for him as Mitchell began the game 9-for-10 from inside of two-point range.

His defensive activity was strong, as well, with Mitchell racking up stocks throughout the game.

The fourth quarter was less efficient. Mitchell's drives became erratic, including one play where he was lost in the trees twice in the same possession. There's value in putting pressure on the rim, but Mitchell has to do a better job of moving away from pressure when the time calls for it.

It was hard to choose between Mitchell and Harden for Player of the Game. Mitchell's defense tipped the scales.

28 points, 5 assists, 1 rebound, 4 turnovers

Good things happen when Harden touches the paint. He's looked more spry and youthful in recent games, dancing with his dribble to work into the restricted area and create from there. Whether it was shooting a floater, kicking to the perimeter, or dropping it into Jarrett Allen's lap — Harden put on a clinic on how to drive the ball.

Unfortunately, Harden shot 1-of-8 from downtown and wasn't able to sprint through the finish line as everything went wrong in the fourth quarter.

10 points, 5 rebounds, 1 assist, 2 steals

Allen was able to roam freely on defense while Harden accepted the challenge of guarding Karl-Anthony Towns on the perimeter. This strategy worked throughout the night as Cleveland was able to safely navigate New York's off-ball actions with Allen standing in the paint to clean up any mistakes. It wasn't until the wheels came off in the fourth quarter, when the Knicks were able to sprint in transition and break the mundanity of the half-court, that Allen became nonconsequential.

8 points, 5 rebounds, 2 assists, 2 steals, 2 blocks

It feels like I'm trying to figure out what to expect from Watson. He hasn't been featured enough in the offense for me to know where he fits. Watson only attempted four shots tonight and didn't spend much time with the ball in his hands.

Proctor had a rough shooting night (1-for-6) but was able to bolster the Cavaliers' defense by being aggressive away from the ball. Proctor does a good job of using his body and bumping guys who try to cut around him. The Cavs could use more of that mentality.

Sometimes, Thomas plays basketball like an adrenaline junkie. He bursts around the court and tries to get involved in every defensive play. That's helped him cause a handful of turnovers, like when he jump-scared Josh Hart, forcing him to lose the ball out of bounds in the second half.

This was not a standout game for Hezonja. He only attempted one shot in 10 minutes and finished as a minus-14.

Merrill hasn't had it going this season. He shot 1-of-7 from downtown tonight and is shooting 32% from deep this season.

6 points, 6 rebounds, 4 assists, 4 turnovers

Tyson is in a funk. It's that simple. He hasn't looked as confident or spry as he was last season. The Cavs will have to figure out how to correct this if they want to reach their ceiling this season. Tyson is too important as a potential sixth man for this to continue.

His last shot attempt was swatted into the stands.

Bryant gave the Cavs good minutes tonight by boxing out and cleaning the glass. I'm glad we were able to end this on a positive note.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2621. Ollie Bassett: Packington to Toronto and life as a professional footballer in Canada

In the latest instalment of GOAL's 'Brits Abroad' series, a man that has played in both hemispheres talks futsal, life Down Under, growing tired of plane travel and what to do in Canada

There was a time not all that long ago when British footballers plying their trade abroad were rarer than hen's teeth. Individual reluctance to leave geographical comfort zones led to collective wings being clipped.

This is, however, a brave new world, with cultural advancement meaning that no corner of the planet should be considered too far-flung. Flying the nest and enjoying a different way of life is now an experience to be embraced, rather than feared.

Of course there are challenges - professional and personal hurdles that need to be cleared on the way - but why not embrace those instead of shying away from them? More and more players are adopting that mindset.

Taking that into account, and as we are forever told that travel broadens the mind, GOAL will be helping to tell the story of some of the intrepid explorers that have broken down barriers and found themselves in a position to impart both sporting and holidaying wisdom.

Next up is Ollie Bassett, a man who has taken the scenic route from Leicestershire in the East Midlands to the vibrant Canadian city of Toronto…

Aston Villa started Bassett's development process

"I started kicking a ball when I was extremely young, probably three or four. Then I joined my local team that my dad used to coach and manage, with my dad's best friend. They would coach together, so I played for them. When I was about seven or eight, we had a tournament - like a summer event - and there were scouts there. There was a Villa scout. He came over after one of the games and spoke to my dad and his mate about my situation. I joined the academy at Villa when I was seven, but I couldn't properly sign until I was eight because the lowest age group was U8. I trained with them for a little bit and as soon as I signed for the academy, obviously I stopped playing with all my mates. I was at Villa from eight until 16."

"Southampton was kind of a weird story about how it happened. I left Villa when they decided on the scholarships - I didn't get offered one. Then I went to, it wasn't Southampton's actual academy, they call it a second chance programme. They were based down in Bath, and alongside that I was doing college. That was meant to be a two-year programme, like two years for your scholarship. The whole purpose of that programme was to promote players into the Southampton academy. But Southampton at the time thought they weren't generating enough players into their full academy, so they knocked it on the head. That was meant to be two years, but they knocked it on the head after a year. We were like, 'well, we've signed a two-year deal, what are we going to do for the second year?'

"It just happened that at the time, because we were based down in Bath, Yeovil Town never used to have an academy. As they cancelled the second chance programme, Yeovil Town restarted their academy with a youth team, underneath the first team. So all the second year scholars from the Southampton thing went and did their second year with Yeovil. I'm still studying at this point, I'm still at college. I'm still living in Bath, but traveling to Yeovil to train. I went there and after two or three months, I started training with the first team. A month later, I made my debut. And then a month after that, I signed my first pro deal, which took me to the end of that year, plus the whole of the second year - an 18-month thing."

Heavier ball helps to improve technical ability

"I loved futsal. I still love it. I must have started when I was like 10. I played that alongside being at Villa. I did it on the side, just here and there. As I got older, about 14 or 15, and after I got released from Villa, I played futsal a lot more. I used to play that all the time. Every Friday night we were in a league. We trained maybe once a week. I was playing that alongside the academy. And then after I got released, I was playing a lot more until I went to the Southampton thing. I feel like when you shift to 11-a-side from playing small-sided, you don't naturally get as many touches. In futsal, the ball is not designed to be pinged 60, 70 yards in the air. It's obviously a lot heavier and it doesn't bounce as much, so the ball is always on the floor. That helped me massively. The technical side of my game, I think, is good. I feel a lot of it, or a good portion of it, was because I played a lot of futsal growing up."

Nuneaton Borough provided a brief pitstop

"My contract finished in Yeovil. At the time, the last six to eight months of that second year at Yeovil, I just wasn't enjoying it at all, to be honest. I understand the politics in football and all that sort of stuff, but as a kid, that was my first year as a pro and I wasn't playing. Sometimes I'd travel to not even be on the bench - just sit in the stand! I was like, 'I just don't really want to stay here'. I knew that they weren't going to renew my contract. I was happy at the time because I just didn't want to stay. Even though it was League Two, I just didn't want to stay. And after that, I was like, 'OK, I'm only 18, I'm going to try and find a club with a reserve team'. Obviously, Yeovil only had youth teams, so after 18 you're either playing first team football or you're not. There were no U21s in that.

"I went on trial at Barnsley and Burton Albion. I didn't get offered anything for their reserves. I ended up dropping down to play non-league in England. I was like, I've literally got no choice. I played non-league, which I think at the time was the league below the Conference North. So, it would be step three in the non-league pyramid. I'm a small midfielder, so the ball's just constantly going over my head! At this point, I'm not really enjoying playing football. The game's not suited to how I like to play. Then a coach from New Zealand reached out and was asking about me through the head of recruitment at Burton Albion. He put me in contact with him.

"Originally, I didn't really go to New Zealand to play football. I just went because I needed to figure out what I'm actually going to do. If I'm not going to play football, if I'm not enjoying playing football, then what am I going to do? So, I went there. I travelled for a little bit and then started training again with a local team. From there, I went to the National League of New Zealand. For me, it was just about getting the enjoyment back for playing. If I didn't have that, then I wasn't going to force myself to keep playing.

"I started enjoying it again and was like, 'okay, this is obviously what I want to do'. It was then a case of, how do I get back into playing full-time? I spoke to a few people over there. One of the boys connected me with his agent, who's still my agent to this day, who works in Canada. My thing was, can I get to the CPL? Because it was a new professional league. It was full-time. It's still developing. But the way they promote players, if you do well, it gives you a decent platform to go somewhere else. I tried to go there for the 2020 season from New Zealand. At the time, my agent was sending my clips. I think he spoke to three clubs in the CPL. All of them were just like, 'yeah, we don't really respect the New Zealand league, it's not professional, we're not looking to scout players from there'. This would have been mid-2019, end of 2019. I wanted to go for the 2020 season. So, I'm like, 'okay, I'll just forget about it, it's not going to happen, at least I tried'.

"That's when I decided to go from New Zealand to Australia for the start of 2020. I'm enjoying football and the Australian NPL league is better than the New Zealand league, you can earn more money there. I went at the start of 2020. I was playing and that, obviously, was the COVID year. The season wasn't even postponed, it just got straight up cancelled! At that time, New Zealand and Australia were the strictest countries in the world when it came to restrictions around COVID. We're all in lockdown, the season's been cancelled and there's no talk about it restarting. That's when I was like, 'I'm just going to go back to the UK, back to my parents, wait for this to die down a little bit and then just see what happens'.

"I went back to the UK mid-2020 and ended up signing for another step three team, Nuneaton Borough. I played for them for a little bit and then had an offer for the start of 2021 to go and play in the top division of Iceland. I was keen because it was full-time. But it wasn't professional. It was more like three, four times a week, training in the evening. It's classed as a professional league, but it's still similarly structured to non-league in England. All the other players probably have jobs during the day. I was set on going there because I didn't have anything else.

"And then, two or three days after Christmas at the end of 2020, the coach from Pacific, Pa-Modou Kah, who's now coaching in the USL Championship, called my agent. He was like, 'listen, I've seen his clips'. I didn't know this until later on, but he told me when I got there that my agent had sent a 10-minute highlights video. He just watched the first 30 seconds - the ball came to me, I dropped my shoulder and chopped a guy, and he decided that he wanted me. This is what I wanted back in 2020. I said to my agent, 'don't even ask'. At that point, I didn't really care if it was a one-year contract, five-year contract, if the money was good, if the money was bad. I just said, 'put me on a salary, give me my accommodation and get me there'. I knew that once I was there, I was playing full-time, playing professionally, I knew that I would do well. That's how the whole thing unfolded from New Zealand to the CPL."

Auckland was Bassett's first landing spot in New Zealand

"When I first moved to New Zealand, I moved to Auckland. Played a season there. Then the second season in the National League, I went to Wellington. They were one of the best teams in the New Zealand National League. So, I was like, 'okay, I'm going to be playing for a better team, if I want to move somewhere after this, I need to be scoring or making assists and winning - even if it is only New Zealand, I need to be doing well'. Football's obviously not their main sport. It's still part-time, semi-professional. So, I was working jobs as well. I worked in a few bars, worked construction with one of my close mates. It's funny to look back. It was just part of the journey.

"It is a beautiful country, though, the scenery and the people. If I was later on in my career and I wanted to go somewhere to retire and work after, that'd be pretty high on the list just because of how relaxed it is and how nice everyone is. But, I was 19, 20 at the time and in my head, when I started enjoying it again, I was like, 'I'm going to use this to get to whatever professional league I can'."

There is always plenty going on in Melbourne

"Melbourne is an incredible city. It's similar to New Zealand because they're obviously so close, but there's just so much more going on. The city's so big, lively and vibrant. Any day of the week, people are out. The NPL, I think, is a better league standard-wise than New Zealand. Even CPL clubs now are probably looking at Oz a little bit more because some NPL players are doing well and getting moves to the A-League. At that time, back in 2020, it wasn't like that. I think people probably just saw it as being similar. But having played in both, the NPL is a lot more competitive. And there was always talk around players in that football world of Australia.

"Australia is also better because you get paid more - you earn three, four, five times more than you would playing football in New Zealand. It's still part-time, it's still just two training sessions a week and a game on the weekend. You can work during the day, play football, but you earn significantly more money than you would in New Zealand. That's the attraction for a lot of players who probably aren't looking to go pro, or can't go pro, they can pick Australia over New Zealand because financially it makes more sense."

Vancouver Island lies on Canada's Pacific Coast

"My first team in Canada was Pacific in 2021. You've got Vancouver, then you've got Vancouver Island. I was on a small island, not a crazy amount going on. But again, another beautiful place. If you were a little bit older and you wanted to go somewhere in Canada, people would go there because it is nice. When my agent told me about the team, I didn't really do much research or find out where it was. For me it was literally, I don't care what team it is, where I live or where I go, it was just the opportunity to be back playing professionally and full time. I tried to use it as a stepping stone to go somewhere else or to get a better contract in the league.

"The CPL is one of those leagues where, because it's still new and there's not that many teams, everyone knows everyone. All the players know each other. You play against each other so much, the coaches know you, so, if you do well and you've proven yourself within the league, then you're always going to have teams that are interested in you. I feel like the majority of clubs will bring in internationals from outside but some of them hit and some of them completely miss. For clubs, it's important that you've proven what you can do in the league, that you're reliable. They know what they're going to get. That's the mentality that I went in with - just go, be back playing full time and then try and make the most of the opportunity."

Ottawa felt like home for a couple of years

"I'd say Ottawa started to feel like home. I moved there at the start of the 2022 season. That's the longest I've stayed at one club since I left Yeovil. That definitely felt like a second home away from home. I was really comfortable there. Towards the end of 2024, I'd been there three years. We reached the final in 2022. Individually, I'd won awards and stuff. We won the regular season in 2022. The only thing we didn't win was the play-offs. By the time the end of 2024 came, I just wanted to try something new. I wanted to have a new challenge and test myself in a different league. So, that was the reason why I left. But for the most part, that's the most comfortable I've felt away from football."

Warmer climes were experienced when arriving in Florida

"Tampa (Bassett joined Tampa Bay Rowdies in January 2025) happened because the USL (The United Soccer League) was always something that I was extremely interested in for the longest time. Even when I was in the CPL, the first few years were about doing well and proving myself. As I started growing and developing, getting better and producing more goals and assists, I was like, 'okay, can I look at the USL as a viable option?' I had a few offers at the end of 2023 to go there, but I was under contract with Ottawa. They were asking for money that USL clubs just weren't willing to pay at the time. They were like, 'we'll just wait one year and take him on a free'. Going into 2024, I didn't tell the club, but they kind of probably knew that this would be my last year. I was even telling my agent going into that 2024 season that he can start speaking to clubs midway through the season because I'm out of contract at the end of the year. That's what he did. He started speaking to a couple of clubs in the USL and we went back and forth with Tampa a few times.

"I ended up actually signing for Tampa in July of 2024, which was when I could sign a pre-contract. From there, I knew that once my season was done in Canada, I would be off to the USL. I feel like it's a step up from the CPL. I think it is a better league than the CPL. But it's very similar in the sense where if you do well in the league and you produce, then you can earn. Financially, you can better yourself. And also, clubs know what you can do so it's easier to stay in the league. It's similar to the CPL in that sense but I think the quality and the environments, it's a more complete league. It's more competitive. There's more teams, two conferences. That's why I decided to go there."

Inter Toronto showed faith after unfortunate injury issues

"I'll be honest, midway through when I was in the USL with Tampa, I was like, 'okay, I'm here now, I'm going to prove what I can do and I want to stay, whether it's with Tampa or someone else'. Then I got a crazy, weird injury that just wouldn't go away. I didn't play from the end of July until the end of the season. I didn't play another game for Tampa by the end of July. Then the end of the season comes and they don't take my option - because I had an option on my contract with Tampa and they don't take it because I'm injured and they don't know what's going on.

"I'm saying to my agent, 'I know I'm injured but I want to stay in the USL, I want to stay here, so we need to start speaking to clubs and see if that's going to be possible'. He was speaking to clubs and they were asking about my injury. A lot of clubs got put off by it because it was a really weird, strange injury that just wouldn't get better. It was a really deep core injury, to the point where getting out of bed was painful, twisting was painful, couldn't pass a ball, couldn't sprint, none of it. Clubs were put off by that. Toronto came in around October-November time. They offered me a contract and I just thought, 'you know what, maybe I need to go back to the CPL for now, get back fit and playing and doing well again'.

"Even after signing for them, I went into pre-season not fixed, I was still injured. It wasn't until I got checked by the medical department and got an injection for the injury that it started to get better. I was only really fully fit from June-July this year, because I was still dealing with it even after the injection. It's an injury that I've probably carried for 10, 11 months and just couldn't shake off. Looking back, I'm happy in Toronto, I like it as a city. We didn't have a great year this year, but as a city, it's nice. I'm looking forward to next season. I think in hindsight, if I was fully fit at the time, coming to the end of my year with Tampa, I probably would have had a lot more interest from the USL. It is what it is."

Bassett has mastered the art of packing a suitcase and racked up air miles

"Packing does still annoy me! Now, at the end of the season, I'm going home in two weeks for the off-season. I'll probably come back at the end of January or start of February for pre-season. It does get repetitive and boring after a while. So now, I travel extremely light. I'm not one of those people who bring three or four suitcases and take everything just in case they need to wear this certain piece of clothing. I generally just take one big suitcase, one small one and a backpack. I know at the end of the year I've got to repack everything! The packing side of it and the moving, it's obviously tedious and a little bit annoying.

"Canada as well, our season finished at the end of October and we won't start training again until the start of pre-season, which will probably be the end of January or the start of February. The season won't start until the first or second week of April. The off-season in Canada is extremely long. Which is obviously fine, everyone's under contract, but you have a long period of time where you're not playing and not training. I get a lot of time at home off the back of a CPL season. The packing and the moving and the flying… I'm over the flying! I don't know how many flights I've taken over the last eight years, but it's a lot!"

FIFA's flagship event passed through Toronto in 2026

"Toronto is a top city. A lot of people in Canada say it's between Toronto and Vancouver for the two best cities. I've not spent enough time in Vancouver to know but being in Toronto, I can understand why people like it. A lot of people are drawn to coming here. I never really fully experienced it when I was in Ottawa, because that's only a five-hour drive away from Toronto. Every time we would play Toronto, we would drive the night before and then as soon as the game's done, we would drive back. I never got to experience it. Everyone that I'd spoken to said it's a great city, and I can see why.

"I feel like this year was probably a lot better because we had the World Cup as well. Toronto was one of the host cities, so while that was going on it was unbelievable - to be in and around it was incredible. There's a mix and lots of different cultures. I feel like Victoria, my first year in Canada, out on the island, that's very Canadian. But Toronto's obviously a bigger city, so lots of different people, lots of different cultures. The food scene in Toronto especially is unbelievable. Any type of food you want, you just search it online and they've got it. It's just a big, popular city. We live close to downtown and it's easy to get around. It's just a lot of fun."

Life in big cities around the world is rarely cheap

"I feel like Canada's expensive. That's one thing I noticed when I went to Pacific, and then even Ottawa, I just feel like Canada is expensive. Toronto, obviously, the cost of living is expensive as well because it's a big city. You go out to eat and you pay your tax and then you've got a tip and that stuff. It all adds up. It's obviously expensive, but I think I'd compare Toronto to Melbourne in terms of cost of living-wise. It's probably very similar. You can understand why people want to go there and move there and live there because there's just so much going on. But it's definitely not cheap."

Playing chess helps to keep the mind sharp

"Over the last few weeks, me and one of my boys from back home, we're going to start a little coaching business. We're trying to use this off-season to get it up and running. And then next year when I come back, hopefully we've built it up enough to offer one-on-one and small group sessions. If I'm not doing that, I love cooking. Obviously we get breakfast and lunch provided by the club, so I'll eat that, but in the evenings I like to put some music on and cook, and chill. I play a lot of chess. No-one else on the team plays! One of the strength and conditioning coaches plays but no-one other than that. I play a lot online. Sometimes my dad will send me requests for a random game so I'll play against him.

"Obviously I go to the gym and stuff - that's not mandatory, but I feel like that's part of being a footballer, to stay healthy. This year was good because all of the international guys, the lads from outside of Toronto, we all lived in the same apartment building. Everyone had their own unit and lived by themselves, but in the same building. Outside of training we would go for coffee and hang out.

"That's one thing I've learned a lot over my career, being a footballer is unbelievable - it's the best job in the world - but you train in the morning, get home by 12:30-1 o'clock and you have got the rest of the day doing nothing. For me, you have to do stuff that keeps you stimulated or busy, just do something. That's why I learned to cook, because it kills time and you are eating good food. If you don't, every day looks the same and feels the same, you don't do anything. I think it's important to stay busy. On the weekends, if we are at home then we're here, if not then we are flying and coming back so that breaks your week up a little bit. For the most part, staying busy is super important."

The iconic Niagara Falls lie on the Canada-United States border

"If you're in Toronto, I'd say go to an NBA basketball game - something different from football. We have an MLS team here, but I'd look to catch an NBA game if they're in season, or baseball or hockey. Hockey's huge in Canada. It's not somewhere that I've been yet, I probably will go next year, but Niagara Falls isn't far from Toronto. Ice skating is very Canadian, they love it. When I was in Ottawa - this was pretty cool to be fair - they have a canal that runs all the way through Ottawa, a big lake. During the winter, when it would get cold enough, it would freeze over. People would, instead of driving or taking the train to work, they would skate from wherever they are on the canal up to wherever their offices are, change their skates and go to work. When I heard about that, I was like, that's crazy!"

Heavy snowfall is common when temperatures plummet

"The weather gets so cold during the winter. I'm lucky because we miss most of it because I'm gone at the end of November and not back until the start of February. I miss December and January, which are the worst two months. But I remember coming back at the end of 2022 in December, just before Christmas to see some of my mates when I was in Ottawa. I think at that time it was minus 25 or minus 30 - snow up to your knees and hip! That's one thing that everyone says about it - during the winter, it's seasonal depression for sure because it's just way too cold. No one's doing anything during the winter."

Trips to Las Vegas are more exciting than most away days

"A lot of players now don't get trapped, but they just want to stay at home or stay local. I understand it because not everyone's the same. If you're playing at a good level back home, then of course it doesn't make any sense to leave. But if you find yourself dropping down to another league, like what I did, then I've always had a passion for travelling. From when I was young, I always said I'd love to play abroad one day. When the opportunity came up, I was always going to take it. I just think it's an amazing thing to do. You can better your career from it. If you build a career, a successful career playing in the USL championship, then you've done really well.

"I feel like you learn a lot about yourself. You're moving away from home for the first time. You're putting yourself in situations that are uncomfortable, that you have to deal with. From that, the only way you deal with it is by growing through it. I've always encouraged people to do it. It's not an easy thing to do, but if you can get through the difficult times, you get to see different parts of the world, you give yourself opportunities through football to experience different markets in the world. I've seen players move from Canada and the US to top divisions in Southeast Asia and Asia. Those opportunities will never happen if you're playing in the lower leagues in England. You can be comfortable playing back home, but I feel like the doors that can open from playing overseas are massive. I would encourage anyone to do it if they've been thinking about it.

"Ever since I made the decision to move to New Zealand, football's taken me to places that I would never have visited in my life. I was in Auckland, Wellington, and Melbourne, and New Zealand, Australia. I was in Victoria, Ottawa, Toronto, Tampa. Even the cities that you go and play in, when you're playing away in the USL, we had Vegas first away game of the season in Tampa! It's places like that where you can go on holiday, but going there through being able to play football is crazy. Even the people you meet as well. My friend from back home that I'm really close with, he was traded to Detroit halfway through last season. I was staying with him for a few weeks. It's not a glamorous place and I would never have chosen to visit, but one of my close friends ended up there. I went for 10, 11 days to visit him. That's the whole point, you meet people and you travel to different cities that you would never have gone to through football or through playing in England. You're away from home but I think it's amazing, honestly. I wouldn't have changed any of it."

Bassett is not ruling out more far-flung adventures

"I've got a year left on my deal here in Toronto. This year was up and down. Second half of the season, I was back fit and I was playing and I wasn't injured. I was good, felt good. But this year as a whole was a bit disrupted with injuries. I've got a year left so I'll be back for the 2027 season. After that, who knows? I couldn't see myself going back to playing in England, I'll be honest. I don't see myself playing in England and retiring there. I see the rest of my career probably in Canada or the US.

"I've always been interested and intrigued about playing in Asia one day. That's always been something that's been in the back of my mind. Whether that happens or not, I have no idea. Cambodia is pretty, not popular, but a few players go there. The one that interests me is Indonesia. I don't know why, but I've had friends who played there and they said they're crazy about football. They love their football over there. So, just playing in an environment like that, I feel would be completely different. That's something I've thought about, but not something that has actually happened yet. And whether it does happen or not, I don't know. I don't think I want to go back to play in the UK any time soon."

How life in Canada ranks across five important areas

Weather: 8/10. "During the summer, eight or nine, but during the winter, one or two!"

Food: 9/10. "You can get anything you want - any type of food, they've got it."

People: 7/10. "That's a difficult one, because obviously it's a big city and you're not going to know everyone."

Things to do: 7/10. "There's a few things. If you want to travel outside of Toronto, like to Niagara Falls, if you're willing to travel - drive one, two, three hours - then there's plenty."

Cost of living: 4/10. "It's definitely not London prices, but it's expensive."

Access to Ollie was provided courtesy of Eikon Sports Group.

#2620. One House, Three Owners: The Ballooning Cost of the American Dream

The story of home affordability in the U.S. told from a single front porch.

#2619. Kyren Williams makes it 13-3 Rams in second quarter

The Rams have had the ball three times and all three drives have ended in points.

Running back Kyren Williams finished off the third possession with a seven-yard sprint into the end zone that pushed the Rams’ lead over the Packers to 13-3 with 11:29 to play in the first half. It’s the first time the Rams have scored on their first three possessions this season and it’s also the first time the Packers have allowed their opponents points on their first three drives.

Most of the other movement on the drive came through the air. Rams quarterback Matthew Stafford completed passes to Williams, tight end Davis Allen, wide receiver Konata Mumpfield, and tight end Tyler Higbee for first downs before Williams’s touchdown. The throw to Williams came just before Stafford took a hit from Packers defensive end Micah Parsons.

Stafford is 10-of-12 for 87 yards while Jordan Love is 6-of-10 for 70 yards, including a 32-yarder to Christian Watson that set up Trey Smack’s 54-yard field goal.

#2617. Rams Winners & Losers: LA beats Packers to bolster playoff odds

It's no secret that Wednesday's pre-Thanksgiving matchup between the Los Angeles Rams and Green Bay Packers carried immense weight for both teams' playoff chances.

The Rams took care of business behind Blake Corum's big rushing day on his birthday. They dealt a near-death blow to Green Bay's postseason odds.

The schedule to get to the playoffs is still long and littered with stiff competition. That is a tomorrow problem. For today, let's take inventory of all the individual contributions that led the Rams to their most important win on the season yet.

Corum made it seven-straight games with a touchdown. He has officially taken the starting running back job from Kyren Williams and continues to outshine his counterpart in terms of production. The third-year back rushed 32 times (!) for 184 yards (5.8 average) and a touchdown. He also added two catches for 21 yards through the air.

The Rams have needed an offensive spark nearly all season. It's clear that Corum has been the answer and is someone the team must rely on moving forward in order to make due on their sky-high expectations.

Jordan Love is not a quarterback that gets sacked often, even if his pass protection is often porous these days. Donald and Garrett were key to generating pressure. Love was only brought down a single time by Byron Young. The pressure helped force Love into some very curious decisions—of which resulted in key takeaways by the defense.

Donald and Garrett are as advertised, even if they are hitting their stride later into the season than originally expected.

You don't rush for 241 yards without a strong performance from the big guys up front. The Rams got the job done even with injuries that caused linemen to play out of position. Warren McClendon, Bill Murray, Tyler Higbee, and Davis Allen all dealt with injuries at some point. Rookie Keagen Trost, Justin Dedich, and David Quessenberry all played parts in relief.

The perseverance from this group in an all-important game was impressive.

With no Puka Nacua for the rest of the season and Davante Adams' return to health timeline currently uncertain, the only pass catcher who seems interested in stepping up is second-year receiver Konata Mumpfield. He added four receptions for 69 yards on Wednesday night. He also snagged a ball at the goal line that was originally called a touchdown and later reversed to incomplete. It was probably at least a completion even if the touchdown call was borderline and the ball short of the end zone.

Mumpfield's step forward is an encouraging sign for the offense. Their passing game is not where it needs to be. Mumpfield's production with Adams back in the fold should help matters.

Wesley Bailey and Josaiah Stewart are still playing sizeable rotational roles on defense. Last week Bailey snagged an interception. Against the Packers, Stewart made multiple key plays in coverage to prevent big gains. You won't see Garrett in coverage often—the Rams want him moving towards the quarterback. Someone has to do the dirty work and play the linebacker role. Stewart did an excellent job in this facet versus Green Bay.

With the Rams up one score late into the third quarter, Matthew Stafford threw an inexplicable first-down interception right to a Packers defender. It was a chance for Green Bay and Love to regain momentum, although Love made an even worse throw for a turnover just two plays later. The Rams stubbed their toe. The Packers let them off the hook.

It's still an open question whether the Rams have enough juice in the passing game to keep up with the likes of the Chiefs, 49ers, Cowboys, and Seahawks—all teams they will see in the coming weeks.

Stafford must be better both in terms of production and in his decision making. It's tough to win games when your leading rusher has more yards than your quarterback (184 versus 140).

But the passing game struggles do not rest entirely on Stafford's shoulders. The receiving corps aside from Mumpfield is not inspiring confidence. The injuries at the tight end position from this game spell concern moving forward. Sean McVay's play calling leaned more towards "don't lose" then "go for the win". LA was reliant on screen passes that often went for minimal gain and there were a few close laterals that could have resulted in disaster.

Something needs to change. Do the Rams have the right players in order to turn things around, or will the passing attack be the reason they fall short of Super Bowl expectations?

#2613. Women's World Cup 2027 MVPs: USWNT standout Trinity Rodman is ready to show the world what the fuss is all about

The 24-year-old has starred in the NWSL and at the Olympic Games, winning both titles, and will be expected to spearhead the U.S. attack in Brazil this summer

There are not many bigger stars in the world of women's football than Trinity Rodman. Nothing highlights that quite like what unfolded at the turn of the year when clubs in Europe were circling as her contract with the Washington Spirit expired.

Desperate not to lose one of the most talented and recognisable players in the league, the NWSL introduced its High Impact Player rule, quickly nicknamed 'the Rodman rule', in order to allow the Spirit to offer Rodman the sort of contract that could keep her around, making her the highest-paid women's footballer in the world.

How Rodman performed in the season that unfolded afterwards only served to justify the deal. A return of 14 goals and 10 assists helped fire the Spirit into the 2026 NWSL Championship game, in which she also scored despite her side's eventual defeat.

Though still only 25 years old, it's not the first time Rodman has performed to an incredible level when all eyes were on her. Be it the way she marked herself out as a star in her own right during her rookie year, firing the Spirit to its first NWSL Championship crown after entering the league to much fanfare given her father Dennis' status as a basketball icon, or her displays in the United States' Olympic triumph in 2024, which saw the national team respond remarkably well to the disappointments of the previous year's World Cup, Rodman is no stranger to excelling under pressure.

It all adds up to make her one of the most fascinating figures heading into the 2027 Women's World Cup. Rodman has yet to really mark herself out on that stage, which is undoubtedly the biggest in the sport. But looking at her career to date, there are not many reasons to bet against her doing so in Brazil.

The spotlight was on Rodman in her first NWSL season, but she performed under that pressure, helping the Washington Spirit to win its first NWSL Championship title

Rodman probably knew that she was never going to enter the world of women's football quietly. When she became the youngest player to be drafted in NWSL history back in 2021, as an 18-year-old, it was her mother who stood proudly beside her, but it was her father's name that added to the attention she would receive.

It wasn't until several years later that Rodman would fully air the frustrations that came with that, which went well beyond the irritation many children of famous sporting parents endure of simply wanting to be a star in their own right.

"He's not a dad. Maybe by blood, but nothing else," she said on the Call Her Daddy podcast in 2024, explaining her father's limited presence in her childhood and how her mother struggled financially to raise her two children, with them even living in their car at one point.

"In terms of what I've filtered and what I've talked about, I feel like me and my brother have been very generous with the way that we've talked about it and very unselfish," Rodman added. "I think we never want to make him look bad and that is at the cost of holding in a lot, and a lot of issues we've gone through and just like, trauma, per se."

Somehow, as an 18-year-old, she managed to compartmentalise all of that and, as the attention on 'Dennis Rodman's daughter' grew, she performed so well that she helped the Washington Spirit win an incredible first NWSL title, earning Best XI honours and the NWSL Rookie of the Year award in the process.

Everyone was excited to watch Rodman at her first World Cup in 2023, but it could hardly have gone much worse for the USWNT

When the 2023 Women's World Cup came around, then, Rodman was prime among the players to watch. Still only 21 years old, she became the youngest player to score two goals in a game for the USWNT in one of their send-off friendlies, with her second goal dubbed "world-class" by head coach Vlatko Andonovski.

But after beginning the tournament with a 3-0 win over Vietnam, nothing else really went to plan for the U.S. Draws with the Netherlands and Portugal saw the four-time world champions scrape through the group stages, as Portugal came within the width of a post of knocking the U.S. out and progressing at their expense.

Then came the infamous penalty shootout against Sweden, when Lina Hurtig's spot-kick crept over the line by a millimetre to send the Scandinavian nation through and condemn the USWNT to a last-16 exit - its worst result in Women's World Cup history. Andonovski subsequently left his role, icons like Megan Rapinoe and Julie Ertz retired and a new era beckoned for the national team, who would be taken over by Emma Hayes in the summer of 2024, after her 12th and final year in charge at Chelsea.

Just a year later, the USWNT was on top of the world again, with Rodman playing a starring role at the Olympic Games in 2024 as part of the 'Triple Espresso' attack

It was hard to know what to expect from the U.S. at the Olympic Games in Paris in 2024. Hayes wouldn't take the role until the end of Chelsea's season, meaning she had only had a few weeks with the team, and the squad she chose boasted much less experience than that of 12 months prior, with the likes of Alex Morgan and Kelley O'Hara joining Rapinoe and Ertz in watching from afar.

But the youthful revolution proved fruitful. Defenders Naomi Girma and Emily Fox announced themselves to the world, but it was Sophia Wilson, Mallory Swanson and Rodman who really captured the attention as the most electric and prolific attack in the tournament fired the United States to an unexpected gold medal.

'Triple Espresso' scored 10 of the team's 12 goals, while also accounting for five assists, earning all three forwards places in the top 10 in the Ballon d'Or voting a few months later. Their contributions were vital in showing that 2023 was merely a blip for the USWNT; this was still one of the very best teams in the international game.

After overcoming a back issue and shining during the 2026 NWSL season, all signs point to Rodman putting on a show at the 2027 Women's World Cup, especially with 'Triple Espresso' reuniting

Remarkably, in the two-and-a-half years since, Hayes hasn't been able to name a single starting XI featuring 'Triple Espresso'. In fact, the three have only shared the pitch as a trio once in that time, in the USWNT's second-most recent game - a 2-0 defeat to Spain which Rodman and Wilson started, with Swanson joining them for the final 25 minutes from the bench.

Two big reasons for that are that Wilson and Swanson both had their first children in that time, Wilson giving birth in September of last year before Swanson followed in November. Rodman, meanwhile, has had issues with a recurring back injury, making it rare for all three to be available at once.

Heading into the 2027 Women's World Cup, though, the signs are positive when it comes to a reunion of the attack that took the Olympics by storm. Wilson's first season back was a strong one, especially considering she didn't score in any of her first five games. In the end, she racked up 15 goals and five assists as the Portland Thorns made the post-season. Swanson, meanwhile, made her return mid-season for a struggling Chicago Stars team that would eventually finish bottom of the league, making it harder for her to really shine, though she did enjoy some promising moments in her first few months back, particularly in the win over Racing Louisville in August, before netting her first USWNT goal since the Olympics in a 2-2 draw with Spain in October.

Rodman, meanwhile, was able to put that nagging back injury to one side as she simply blew the NWSL away. Despite January's record-breaking contract putting her into the limelight even more, Rodman reeled off 15 goals and 10 assists as the Washington Spirit made the NWSL Championship game, earning herself a well-deserved nomination for the Most Valuable Player accolade in the process.

She's shone repeatedly in the NWSL and she's risen to the occasion at the Olympics. In 2027, it's time for Rodman to show the Women's World Cup stage what she is all about.

#2611. Rams lean on Blake Corum's 184 yards and a strong defense to defeat Packers

The stadium rocked. The crowd roared. The players delivered big hits.

And that's just what happened during legendary rock band U2's halftime performance of the Rams' game against the Green Bay Packers on Wednesday night at SoFi Stadium.

Before and after, the Rams put together perhaps their most important effort of the season in a 26-13 victory.

Blake Corum rushed for a touchdown and a career-best 184 yards, Kyren Williams rushed for a touchdown and the Rams' defense forced three turnovers as the Rams improved their record to 7-4.

Rams linebacker Byron Young sacks Green Bay Packers quarterback Jordan Love in the fourth quarter Wednesday. (Allen J. Schaben / Los Angeles Times)

Corum, who turned 26 on Wednesday, carried the ball a career-high 32 times and eclipsed 100 yards rushing for the second consecutive game. Brandon McManus kicked four field goals.

The Rams, the preseason Super Bowl favorite, had stumbled to a 2-4 start. But they have now won five games in a row.

The previous four came against the Las Vegas Raiders, Chargers, Washington Commanders and Arizona Cardinals, none of which have winning records.

But the matchup against the Packers was different, and it carried huge postseason implications.

The Packers (6-5) are in the hunt for a playoff spot out of the NFC North, where the Detroit Lions (8-2), Chicago Bears (8-2), and Minnesota Vikings (7-3) are also battling for postseason berths.

Now the Rams have the head-to-head tiebreaker if the Rams and Packers end up with the same record at the end of the regular season.

Rams running back Kyren Williams scores on a seven-yard touchdown run in the second quarter against the Packers on Wednesday. (Allen J. Schaben / Los Angeles Times)

The Rams have games remaining against the Kansas City Chiefs (8-2), the NFC West-leading San Francisco 49ers (9-1), the Dallas Cowboys (5-6) and two games against the defending Super Bowl-champion Seattle Seahawks (6-4) sandwiched around a trip to play the Tampa Bay Buccaneers (3-7).

With receiver Puka Nacua out for the season and Davante Adams still on injured reserve, second-year pro Konata Mumpfield stepped up and caught four passes for 69 yards.

Quarterback Matthew Stafford completed 16 of 23 passes for 140 yards, with an interception.

On a night the Rams broke out their "Fearsome White" uniforms to honor the team's famed Fearsome Foursome defensive line of the 1960s, Myles Garrett, Aaron Donald and Co. did not disappoint.

Byron Young sacked Packers quarterback Jordan Love. Garrett, Donald and Young tackled running backs for losses. Donald batted down a pass.

Linebacker Nate Landman forced a fumble and safety Jaylen McCollough and cornerback Emmanuel Forbes Jr. intercepted passes.

It wasn't all good news: Tight ends Davis Allen and Tyler Higbee left the game because of ankle and chest injuries, respectively. Offensive lineman Bill Murray also left the game because of an injury.

In the first half, McManus kicked two field goals and Williams rushed for a touchdown, and the Packers kicked two field goals and scored on Christian Watson's short touchdown reception for a 13-13 tie at halftime.

U2 performs during halftime between the Rams and Green Bay Packers at SoFi Stadium on Wednesday. (Allen J. Schaben / Los Angeles Times)

Watson left the game in the second quarter after sustaining a concussion on a hit by Kam Kinchens that led to the Rams safety being ejected.

The Rams started the second half with a 14-play, 64-yard drive that Corum capped with a short touchdown run.

On the Rams' next possession, Packers linebacker Ty'Ron Hopper intercepted a pass by Stafford and returned it 20 yards. But on the Packers' second play, Rams safety McCollough intercepted a deep pass.

McManus kicked his third field goal with just over six minutes left to increase the lead to 10 points.

The Rams clinched the victory when Forbes intercepted a pass with 3:39 remaining, and McManus added another field goal with 27 seconds left.

#2608. LaMelo Ball injury update: Timberwolves star hurt late in win vs. Thunder

The Minnesota Timberwolves were already without their biggest star due to injury. Now they may have lost their second-best player, at least for the short term.

Late in Minnesota's game Wednesday, Nov. 25 against the Oklahoma City Thunder, with 2:29 left in the fourth quarter, point guard LaMelo Ball was defending a play in the middle of the paint, when he jumped and landed awkwardly, rolling his left ankle. Ball immediately grabbed at his foot and writhed on the floor in obvious discomfort. He remained on the floor for several moments before being helped toward the bench.

Trainers assessed Ball's foot while he sat on the end of the bench. After some minutes, he eventually got up and limped toward the locker room.

"No," Timberwolves coach Chris Finch told reporters after the game when asked if he had an update on Ball. "It looked pretty bad, but I'm not sure."

The Athletic reported, however, that Ball was in good spirits in the postgame locker room and was laughing and joking with his teammates.

Ball scored 19 points on 7-of-18 shooting before leaving the game, adding 8 assists and 7 rebounds.

Ball is an absence the Timberwolves cannot afford. The team has been without All-Star Anthony Edwards, the most dynamic shooting guard in the NBA, since the fifth game of the regular season. Edwards underwent an arthroscopic procedure on his knee to clean up the area and reduce swelling.

Despite Edwards' absence, the Timberwolves have thrived, sprinting out to a 14-5 record, good for third place in the Western Conference.

Guard Ayo Dosunmu and forward Jaden McDaniels have also had a big offensive impact with Edwards sidelined, and if Ball is forced to miss some time, even more will be asked of the pair.

The Timberwolves would eventually beat the Thunder in their game, 113-106.

#2602. Knicks rally to beat Cavaliers behind unlikely hero, expanded lineup to keep slim NBA Cup hopes alive

Mike Brown coached Wednesday's NBA Cup contest like a preseason game. He used a 12-man rotation, throwing out Tyler Nickel for his NBA debut and giving important minutes to sparsely used Pacôme Dadiet.

Brown, as he noted before tipoff, sees the scheduling benefits of being eliminated from Adam Silver's beloved tournament.

But it didn't matter. The Knicks (12-6), winners of six straight overall, surged in the fourth quarter behind unlikely hero Jose Alvarado.

Mikal Bridges celebrates after hitting a 3-pointer during the Knicks' 121-102 win over the Cavaliers on Nov. 25, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Jason Szenes for NY Post

They avoided Cup elimination — at least for a couple of days — with a 121-102 win over the collapsing Cavaliers, who blew an 11-point lead late in the third quarter.

The Knicks (1-2 in group play) remain unlikely to advance. To win the group, they'd need a victory over the Heat on Friday by at least seven points and a Sixers loss to the Pacers. Advancing as the wild card is more complicated and unlikely.

But the defending Cup champs aren't technically eliminated as of Thanksgiving.

They were short-handed Wednesday without OG Anunoby (calf strain) and Miles McBride (left adductor tightness). They lost backup center Andre Drummond to a head injury in the first half.

Then they punished the Cavs (9-10) by winning the fourth quarter 39-17 with Alvarado scoring 16 of his 21 points in the final period and Brown going deep into his bench.

"I looked at the bench in the first quarter and realized, 'Wow, we played like 10 people in that first quarter,' " said Josh Hart, who had 15 points and 11 assists.

Jose Alvarado celebrates after hitting a second 3-pointer in the fourth quarter of the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers. Jason Szenes for NY Post

Mikal Bridges finished with an efficient 19 points. Karl-Anthony Towns extended his double-double streak to 15 (barely) with 12 points and 10 boards.

If and when Brown's squad is out of the tournament, the coach is looking at elimination as an NBA Cup half full. Losing translates to an easier schedule for the Knicks, which wasn't lost on the coach before Wednesday's comeback win.

Brown knows because the NBA shared the possibilities. Advancement potentially brings another three road games — including the final in Indiana.

Josh Hart reacts during the second quarter of the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers at Madison Square Garden. Jason Szenes for NY Post

And elimination means two games — including one at home — over a nine-day stretch.

"You look at it and you're like, 'Whoa, hell, that's a lot of days off if you don't advance,' " Brown said. "And so, again, you wanna compete, you wanna win, but if you don't … there's an opportunity if you got some guys banged up to get rest and or to work on some things in between the games that you have if you're not involved in the Cup."

Silver won't like that answer from the defending NBA Cup champs. But Brown was speaking from experience.

The Knicks added a long road trip to their schedule last season because of the NBA Cup, then an 83rd game with the final in Las Vegas.

The results were phenomenal for the Knicks. They won the Cup, earned the prize money (each player won over $500,000) and months later captured the big NBA championship.

But the Knicks, as an organization, didn't fully embrace Silver's tournament. It took them nearly a full year to hang the banner.

Then Wednesday, the coach of the Cup champs was openly espousing the virtues of being eliminated.

Mikal Bridges rises up for a layup during the second quarter of the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers at Madison Square Garden. Jason Szenes for NY Post

No crying over a spilled NBA Cup. For now, though, the Cup is still on the table.

"When I talk about the Cup, it adds a level of stress maybe on your mind and your body because the games are intense as you go along and you're playing more games in a short amount of time," Brown said. "I remember last year it was like, 'Game, Game, Game.' And then you win the Cup and you celebrate it that night and you hop on a plane and go play in Indiana the next night. And so it's great. You like the competition and the setup but it adds a lot of stress to you physically and mentally. I'm saying this because especially with the extended season we had last year and how taxing the games were for our playoff run mentally and physically, I'm trying to be smart."

But T.J. Lang, who played with Rodgers on the Packers from 2009 to 2016 and is now an analyst on the Lions Radio broadcast, criticized his former quarterback on 97.1's "The Valenti Show with Rico."

Aaron Rodgers looks on during the third quarter of the Steelers' loss to the Bengals on Nov. 15, 2026, in Cincinnati. Getty Images

"I think it's despicable," Lang said. "I think that Aaron Rodgers just became everything that he has bitched about and moaned about over the last 10 years with former coaches, former teammates, airing grievances out, giving opinions out in the media.

"He has blacklisted, I don't know how many, former teammates from just simple comments telling the truth because he just— he was sick of it. Honestly, I don't even know if I want to watch the whole thing. It's just unnecessary. It's a victim mentality. It's a poor me, woe is me. Oh, I thought this, and it wasn't that.' And I'm sick, man.

Packers guard T.J. Lang on the sidelines during a game against the Cardinals. Getty Images

"I've been in a group chat with a whole lot of former teammates that feel the exact same way that I do."

Lang admitted that he does not have the greatest relationship with Rodgers, while still maintaining one with McCarthy, who he said that he talked with on Monday.

"I haven't talked to Aaron Rodgers in probably five years," Lang said. "I can't remember the last time he returned a text. I don't have a relationship with him. And I think it's just absolutely despicable what he just did to Big Mike."

Pittsburgh head coach Mike McCarthy looks on during the first quarter of the Steelers' loss to the Eagles on Nov. 22, 2026, in Philadelphia. Getty Images

McCarthy spoke with reporters Wednesday evening and did acknowledge the miscue referring to the Steelers as the Packers in the locker room, and called it "embarrassing."

Various reports indicated that the Steelers (6-4) don't have any plans to make a change at head coach and that McCarthy has the organization's support. Rodgers reportedly gave McCarthy an ultimatum to give up playcalling duties or cut him as frustrations over the offense's success came to a head.

Mason Rudolph will start at quarterback for the Steelers when they face the Broncos on Black Friday.

#2600. Here's a roadmap to the Aaron Rodgers developments, one of the craziest stories in years

If you were traveling on Wednesday or otherwise not plugged in for one of the most rollicking, unpredictable days in years if not ever, you’re in luck.

We’ve reconstructed the full timeline as to the events regarding now-former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers.

It actually started on Monday, when coach Mike McCarthy was asked whether he has considered using one of the team’s other three quarterbacks when the offense is struggling. McCarthy, whose body language in hindsight suggests he was dreading the question, said without equivocation, “Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback.”

On Tuesday, Rodgers took his usual reps at practice. He conducted his usual midweek press conference. Unbeknownst to most of the media or the general public, the Steelers were changing their play-calling procedures to have quarterbacks coach Tom Arth, and not McCarthy, communicate the calls to Rodgers through the speaker in his helmet.

Then came Tuesday night. At 11:10 p.m. ET, the Steelers issued a statement — not from coach Mike McCarthy or G.M. Omar Khan or owner Art Rooney II but from team spokesman Burt Lauten — that Mason Rudolph will start on Friday against the Broncos and Rodgers will be inactive.

The news forced the Broncos to make a quick pivot in their preparations from Rodgers to Rudolph. As one source put it, the Broncos weren’t sure whether it was a good thing or a bad thing.

It was definitely a bad thing, all things considered, for the Steelers.

By Wednesday morning, reports emerged that it was a coach’s decision. At 8:00 a.m. ET, McCarthy informed the team of the decision.

Rodgers wasn’t there. As it turns out, he was excused.

Enter Pat McAfee. He teased a surprise guest at 1:40 p.m. ET. As expected, it was Rodgers.

Rodgers took a flamethrower to McCarthy, airing out dirty laundry that created a major mess for his now-former team. He exposed the fact that, at halftime of the Week 11 loss to the Eagles, McCarthy broke down the locker-room gathering by saying, “1-2-3 Packers.”

Rodgers also said McCarthy was falling asleep during some meetings and missing other meetings, expressing (in passive-aggressive fashion) concern for McCarthy’s health.

But Rodgers also said it was nothing new, and that McCarthy had done the same things in Green Bay, when they worked there together a decade ago.

Rodgers also made it clear he wouldn’t stay with the Steelers. After his session with McAfee and company, Dulac reported that the Steelers were expected to release Rodgers. By the official close of business, they’d waived him.

A question lingers as to whether the Steelers will try to deny Rodgers the balance of his $22 million salary. It won’t be easy; they’ll need to both void the guarantees and wipe out his eligibility for termination pay (in the form of his remaining salary) under the Collective Bargaining Agreement.

McCarthy met with reporters on Wednesday, after all hell had broken loose. He admitted to the “1-2-3 Packers” comment, calling it an “[e]mbarrassing moment.”

McCarthy also said that Rodgers was benched after issuing an “ultimatum” that was “clearly outside of his job description.”

ESPNFL thereafter reported that McCarthy “has the full support” of the organization, and that the team is “not planning to make any immediate changes.” The key word may be “immediate"; with few viable options only two days before a Black Friday game against the Broncos, changes could come later.

The overriding takeaway is that someone with the Steelers knew or should have known that McCarthy and Rodgers couldn’t co-exist. Because, obviously, they didn’t co-exist.

If, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy believed Rodgers got him fired in Green Bay, McCarthy shouldn’t have wanted a reunion with Rodgers. And if, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy was doing the same kinds of things in Green Bay that he did in Pittsburgh, Rodgers shouldn’t have wanted a reunion with McCarthy.

The reunion has now ended abruptly, with seven games left in the 2026 regular season. It remains to be seen whether Rodgers wants to play with another team, or whether another team wants him. Any team that signs him risks being on the receiving end of a future gripe session from Rodgers in which he lists all the things that team and/or the coach did wrong — without ever acknowledging his own role in the reality that it didn’t work out.

The good news, if there is any, is this: If Rodgers never plays again, his final NFL pass will have been a touchdown.

Which is a better outcome than if he’d retired after the 2025 season. His final pass that year was a pick-six in the playoffs.

#2598. Why Wu-Tang Clan is performing during halftime at Bears vs. Lions NFL Thanksgiving game

Why Wu-Tang Clan is performing during halftime at Bears vs. Lions NFL Thanksgiving game originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Of all the performers the NFL has enlisted to rock the stage on Thanksgiving Day, few have paired style with substance quite like Wu-Tang Clan.

The Staten Island-based outfit appears to be the largest act the NFL has booked in terms of number of members, with nine contributors — RZA, GZA, Inspectah Deck, U-God, Ghostface Killah, Method Man, Raekwon, Masta Killa and Cappadonna — expected to feature in some way during Bears vs. Lions.

So, just why did Wu-Tang Clan get the call over other noteworthy acts, some of whom call Motown home? Here's a look at why the NFL opted to give the rap group the nod in the first game of the Thanksgiving Day slate.

MORE:Meet the full list of 2026 NFL Thanksgiving performers, from U2 to national anthem singers

Wu-Tang Clan is one of rap's seminal acts, a dizzying blend of lyricism and gritty deliveries that helped put East Coast rap on the map in the early-1990s. The group hasn't released in album in more than a decade. Still, the brand remains as relevant as ever, influencing legions of rappers young and old.

Wu-Tang Clan charmed listeners with its first release, "Enter the Wu-Tang: 36 Chambers" in 1993, a titan of a project headlined by "C.R.E.A. M." and "Protect Ya Neck." An underground release that centered around our protagonists' experiences growing up in New York, "Enter the Wu-Tang" didn't project to garner much airplay. The content — namely its humor and imagery — proved too difficult for listeners to ignore. By 1995, the album was certified Platinum and 30 years on, it has sold more than four million records.

Those sales were no aberration. The project is adorned with kung-fu references, musings on the Nation of Islam and good old-fashion braggadocio.

MORE: Best NFL Thanksgiving halftime shows ever, ranked from Creed to Dolly Parton

"This is the sound of accidental fame," wrote Pitchfork's Rollie Pemberton in a retrospective review. "Something as unique and unusual as this record isn't supposed to find itself at the height of commercial viability; it's supposed to smolder underground, hidden from the view of mainstream America, who surely would not be ready for such a challenge. But America was ready, in part because this one challenged convention, not listeners."

The album — and its follow-up, "Wu-Tang Forever" — remains tethered to the public consciousness, as does the myriad solo projects crafted by the group's nine members. Predictably, the group is getting its due, best encapsulated by its recent induction into the Rock and Roll Hall of Fame.

Wu-Tang Clan isn't your typical Thanksgiving Day performers. But its pronounced impact on rap, music and pop culture more broadly can't be denied. That seems to have been enough to land the group a coveted spot on the marquee for Thursday's Thanksgiving Day fixtures.
